\section*{How to use this book}\label{how-to-use-this-book}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{How to use this book}

If you have taken P-Chem 1 at Florida Tech, you should already be familiar with this format. Everything we did with the Live Textbook of P-Chem 1 will be happening this semester with P-Chem 2. Please read this book carefully, since everything that will be in your exams is explained here.
Since this book is specifically tailored for the CHM 3002 course at Florida Tech, there are no superfluous parts. In other words, everything in it might be subject to question in the quizzes and the final exam.

\begin{quote}
Definitions and exercises are usually numbered and are highlighted in the text in this format (lighter grey, indented, and following a grey vertical bar). Please study the definitions carefully since they are fundamental concepts that will be used several times in the remainder of the text, and they will be subject to quizzes and exams. Exercises are essential for cementing the concepts, and you should attempt to execute them first without looking at the solution. Even if you were able to solve an exercise on your own, always read the solution after, since it might contain additional explanations expanding the main concepts in the text.
\end{quote}

Navigating the book should be straightforward. On each page, there is a useful sidebar on the left that gives you an overview of all chapters, and a toolbar at the top with important tools. Arrows to shift between chapters might also be present, depending on your browser. If you are old-school and prefer a pdf, you can download a printout by clicking on the toolbar's corresponding icon. If you are \emph{really} old-school and prefer a printed book, the best solution is to download the pdf and print it yourself. It is a LaTeX book, and I can promise you it will look good on paper. However, I cannot provide physical copies to each student. In the toolbar, you will find a useful search box that is capable of searching the entire book. The most adventurous will find in the toolbar a link to the raw GitHub source code. Feel free to head on \href{https://github.com/peverati/PChem2}{over there} and fork the book.

\chapter{Brief Summary of Classical Physics}\label{Classical}

Quantum mechanics cannot be derived from classical physics, but it was built by physicists who knew classical physics very well, and many of its ideas and equations are borrowed from it. Before discussing, in the next chapter, the experiments that classical physics could not explain, we will review the classical concepts that we will need the most: the description of motion through forces and through energy, the electrostatic interaction between charged particles, and the physics of waves.

\section{Classical Mechanics}\label{classical-mechanics}

\subsection{Newtonian Formulation}\label{newtonian-formulation}

Classical mechanics as formulated by Isaac Newton (1643--1727) is all about forces. Newtonian mechanics works well for problems where we know the forces and have a reasonable coordinate system. In these cases, the net force acting on a system at position \(\mathbf{r}\) in Cartesian 3-dimensional space \(\left\{ x,y,z \right\}\) is simply:

\begin{equation}
F_{\mathrm{net}}(\mathbf{r}) = m\ddot{\mathbf{r}} = m \frac{d^2 \mathbf{r}}{dt^2}.
\label{eq:newt1}
\end{equation}

Or, in other words, if we know the net force acting on a system of mass \(m\) at position \(\mathbf{r}\) at some time \(t_0\), we can use eq. \eqref{eq:newt1} to calculate the position of the system at any future (or past) time. We have completely determined the dynamical evolution of the system.\footnote{Notice that \(\mathbf{r}\) \(\in\) \(\mathbb{R}^{3}\) is the position vector and \(\dot{\mathbf{r}}\) \(\in\) \(\mathbb{R}^{3}\) is the velocity vector. As such, all the equations of classical mechanics are vector equations in 3-dimensional space, and not just simple numerical equations. To simplify the picture, we can restrict ourselves to a 1-dimensional space, replace \(\mathbf{r}\) with \(x\), and forget the complications of vector algebra, as we do in the examples.}

\begin{quote}
\begin{example}
\protect\hypertarget{exm:newtonianEx}{}\label{exm:newtonianEx}A ball of mass \(m\) is at ground level and tossed straight up from an initial position \(x_0\) with an initial velocity \(\dot{x}_0\) and subject to gravity alone. Calculate the equation of motion for the ball (i.e.~where is the ball going to be after some time \(t\)?).\footnote{This example is based on \href{https://rhettallain.com/2018/10/31/classical-mechanics-newtonian-lagrangian-and-hamiltonian/}{Rhett Allain's blog post on classical mechanics}.}

\emph{Solution:} Since the only force acting on the ball is gravity, we can use the equation for the gravitational force to start our derivation:

\begin{equation}
F_{\mathrm{gravity}}=-mg,
\end{equation}

with \(g\) the gravitational acceleration (\(g=9.8\; \mathrm{m}/\mathrm{s}^{2}\)). We can then replace this expression into eq. \eqref{eq:newt1}, to obtain:

\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
-mg &=m \ddot{\mathbf{r}} \\
-g &=\ddot{\mathbf{r}} \\
-g &=\frac{d\dot{\mathbf{r}}}{dt}. \\
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

Working in a 1-dimensional case, we can replace \(\mathbf{r}\) with position \(x\) to obtain:

\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
-mg &=m \ddot{x} \\
-g &=\ddot{x} \\
-g &=\frac{d\dot{x}}{dt}. \\
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

which can then be integrated with respect to time, to obtain:

\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
-g\int_{t=0}^{t} dt &=\int_{\dot{x}_0}^{\dot{x}} d\dot{x}\\
\dot{x} &= \dot{x}_0-gt\\
\frac{dx}{dt} &= \dot{x}_0-gt,\\
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

which can be further integrated with respect to time, to give:

\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\int_{x_0}^{x} dx &= \int_{t=0}^{t} \dot{x}_0 dt -g \int_{t=0}^{t}tdt\\
x &= x_0 + \dot{x}_0 t -\frac{1}{2}gt^2.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

This final equation is the equation of motion for the ball, from which we can calculate the position of the ball at any time \(t\). Notice how the equation of motion does not depend on the mass of the ball!

\emph{Numerical problem:} How much time will a ball ejected from a height of \(1 \;\mathrm{m}\) at an initial velocity of \(10 \;\mathrm{m/s}\) take to hit the floor?

We can use the equation of motion obtained above to solve this problem, and obtain for this specific case \(t\simeq2.14\;\mathrm{s}\).\footnote{Can you write a python program to do this calculation?}
\end{example}
\end{quote}

Newton's equation is not the only way of writing the laws of classical mechanics. Physicists have developed other, equivalent formulations, which give exactly the same results but are more convenient for some problems. The most famous are the \emph{Lagrangian} formulation, based on the difference between the kinetic and the potential energy of a system, and the \emph{Hamiltonian} formulation, based on their sum.\footnote{The Lagrangian formulation is very powerful for complicated mechanical systems, such as a pendulum hanging from a moving support, but we will not need it in this textbook. Interested readers can find an introduction in the \href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagrangian_mechanics}{Wikipedia article on Lagrangian mechanics}.} Only the second one will be important for us, because it is the one that quantum mechanics is built upon.

\subsection{Hamiltonian Formulation}\label{hamiltonian-formulation}

Newton's mechanics is built around \emph{forces}. Hamiltonian mechanics, instead, is built around \emph{energy}. Its goal is to describe the motion of a system starting from a single function, the total energy, written in terms of where the particle is and how much momentum it has. This is exactly the starting point that we will use in quantum mechanics, which is why we need to get familiar with it now.

The first ingredient is the \textbf{momentum} of a particle of mass \(m\) moving with velocity \(\dot{x}\) (we will work in one dimension to keep things simple):

\begin{equation} 
p = m\dot{x}.
\label{eq:ham1}
\end{equation}

Momentum carries the same information as velocity, but, as we will see in the next chapter, it is the quantity that appears naturally when we describe particles in quantum mechanics.

The second ingredient is the total energy of the system, which is the sum of the kinetic energy, \(K\), and the potential energy, \(V\):

\begin{equation}
H = K + V.
\label{eq:hamdef}
\end{equation}

When we write this total energy as a function of the position, \(x\), and the momentum, \(p\), we call it the \textbf{Hamiltonian} of the system, and we use the symbol \(H\). Since the kinetic energy is \(K=\frac{1}{2}m\dot{x}^2\), and \(\dot{x}=p/m\), we can write \(K=\frac{p^2}{2m}\), and the Hamiltonian of a particle becomes:

\begin{equation} 
H(x,p) = \frac{p^2}{2m} + V(x).
\label{eq:ham2}
\end{equation}

This equation is the most important result of this section. The first term depends only on the momentum, and it is the same for every system. The second term depends only on the position, and it is what makes each system different: a ball in the gravitational field of the Earth, an electron attracted by a nucleus, or two atoms connected by a chemical bond differ only in their potential energy, \(V(x)\).

Once we know the Hamiltonian, the motion of the system can be obtained from two simple equations, called \textbf{Hamilton's equations}:

\begin{equation} 
\begin{aligned}
\dot{x}=\frac{dx}{dt} &= +\frac{\partial H}{\partial p}, \\
\dot{p}=\frac{dp}{dt} &= -\frac{\partial H}{\partial x}.
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:ham3}
\end{equation}

These two equations are less mysterious than they look. If we apply them to eq. \eqref{eq:ham2}, the first one gives \(\dot{x}=\frac{p}{m}\), which is just the definition of momentum, eq. \eqref{eq:ham1}, read backwards. The second one gives \(\dot{p}=-\frac{dV}{dx}\). Since \(\dot{p}=m\ddot{x}\), and since minus the derivative of the potential energy is the force, \(F=-\frac{dV}{dx}\), this is just Newton's equation, \(F=m\ddot{x}\), written in a different form. Hamilton's equations, in other words, contain no new physics: they are a different way of organizing the same information, starting from the energy instead of the forces.

\begin{quote}
\begin{example}
\protect\hypertarget{exm:hamex1}{}\label{exm:hamex1}Let's apply the Hamiltonian formulation to the same problem as in the previous example: a ball of mass \(m\) tossed straight up and subject only to gravity.

The potential energy of the ball at height \(x\) is \(V(x)=mgx\), so the Hamiltonian, eq. \eqref{eq:ham2}, is:

\begin{equation}
H=\frac{p^2}{2m}+mgx.
\label{eq:ham4}
\end{equation}

Hamilton's equations, eq. \eqref{eq:ham3}, give:

\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\dot{x} &= \frac{\partial H}{\partial p} = \frac{p}{m}, \\
\dot{p} &=-\frac{\partial H}{\partial x} = -mg.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

The second equation tells us that the momentum of the ball decreases at a constant rate, \(p(t)=p_0-mgt\), where \(p_0=m\dot{x}_0\) is the initial momentum. Replacing it in the first equation, we obtain \(\dot{x}=\dot{x}_0-gt\), and integrating once more we obtain the same equation of motion that we found with Newton's method:

\begin{equation}
x = x_0 + \dot{x}_0 t -\frac{1}{2}gt^2.
\end{equation}
\end{example}
\end{quote}

So, if the Hamiltonian gives the same answers as Newton's equation, why do we bother? There are two reasons, both important for what follows:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{The Hamiltonian is the total energy, and the total energy is conserved.} We can check this for the ball in the previous example: replacing \(p(t)\) and \(x(t)\) in eq. \eqref{eq:ham4}, all the terms that depend on time cancel out, and \(H=\frac{p_0^2}{2m}+mgx_0\) at all times. As the ball rises, kinetic energy is converted into potential energy and vice versa, but their sum, \(H\), never changes. Energy, and not force, is the central quantity in chemistry, and the energy levels of atoms and molecules are what we measure in experiments.
\item
  \textbf{The Hamiltonian is the bridge to quantum mechanics.} In quantum mechanics we will not be able to talk about the trajectory of a particle, so Newton's equation will be of little use. The Hamiltonian, on the other hand, will survive almost unchanged. In chapter \ref{Operators}, we will see that the central equation of quantum mechanics, the Schrödinger equation, is obtained by taking eq. \eqref{eq:ham2} and replacing the momentum, \(p\), with a mathematical operation (a derivative) acting on a wave function.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Coulomb's Law}\label{coulombs-law}

Coulomb's Law states that the magnitude of the electrostatic force of attraction or repulsion between two point charges is directly proportional to the product of the magnitudes of charges and inversely proportional to the square of the distance between them:

\begin{equation}
|\mathbf{F}| = k_e \frac{|q_1q_2|}{r^2} = \frac{1}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}\frac{|q_1q_2|}{r^{2}}.
\label{eq:coulomb1}
\end{equation}

The constant \(k_e\) is called the Coulomb constant and is equal to \({1}/{4\pi\varepsilon_0}\), where \(\varepsilon_0\) is the vacuum permittivity constant; \(k_e \simeq 8.988\times10^9\;\mathrm{Nm^2C^{-2}}\). If the product \(q_1q_2\) is positive, the force between the two charges is repulsive; if the product is negative, the force between them is attractive.

\subsection{Vector Form of Coulomb's Law}\label{vector-form-of-coulombs-law}

Coulomb's law in vector form states that the electrostatic force \({\textstyle \mathbf {F}_{1}}\) experienced by a charge, \(q_{1}\) at position \(\mathbf {r}_{1}\), in the vicinity of another charge, \(q_{2}\) at position \(\mathbf {r}_{2}\), in a vacuum is equal
\begin{equation}
{\displaystyle \mathbf{F}_{1}={\frac {q_{1}q_{2}}{4\pi \varepsilon _{0}}}{\frac {\mathbf{r}_{1}-\mathbf{r}_{2}}{|\mathbf {r}_{1}-\mathbf{r}_{2}|^{3}}}={\frac {q_{1}q_{2}}{4\pi \varepsilon_{0}}}{\frac{\mathbf{\hat{r}} _{12}}{|\mathbf {r} _{12}|^{2}}}},
\label{eq:coulomb2}
\end{equation}

where \({\textstyle {\boldsymbol {r}}_{12}={\boldsymbol {r}}_{1}-{\boldsymbol {r}}_{2}}\) is the vectorial distance between the charges, \({\textstyle {\widehat {\mathbf {r} }}_{12}={\frac {\mathbf {r} _{12}}{|\mathbf {r} _{12}|}}}\) a unit vector pointing from \({\textstyle q_{2}}\) to \({\textstyle q_{1}}\), and \(\varepsilon_{0}\) the vacuum permittivity constant. Here, \({\textstyle \mathbf {\hat {r}} _{12}}\) is used for the vector notation.

The vector form of Coulomb's law is simply the scalar definition of the law with the direction given by the unit vector, \({\textstyle {\widehat {\mathbf {r} }}_{12}}\), parallel with the line from charge \(q_{2}\) to charge \(q_{1}\). If both charges have the same sign (like charges) then the product \(q_1q_2\) is positive and the direction of the force on \(q_{1}\) is given by \({\textstyle {\widehat {\mathbf {r} }}_{12}}\); the charges repel each other. If the charges have opposite signs then the product \(q_1q_2\) is negative and the direction of the force on \(q_{1}\) is \({\textstyle -{\hat {\mathbf {r} }}_{12}}\); the charges attract each other.

The electrostatic force \({\textstyle \mathbf {F} _{2}}\) experienced by \(q_{2}\), according to Newton's third law, is \({\textstyle \mathbf {F} _{2}=-\mathbf {F} _{1}}\).

\subsection{Electric field}\label{electric-field}

An electric field is a vector field that associates to each point in space the Coulomb force experienced by a unit test charge. The strength and direction of the Coulomb force \({\textstyle \mathbf {F} }\) on a charge
\({\textstyle q_{t}}\) depends on the electric field \({\textstyle \mathbf {E} }\) established by other charges that it finds itself in, such that
\({\textstyle \mathbf {F} =q_{t}\mathbf {E} }\). In the simplest case, the field is considered to be generated solely by a single source point charge. If the field is generated by a positive source point charge \({\textstyle q}\), the direction of the electric field points along lines directed radially outwards from it, i.e.~in the direction that a positive point test charge \({\textstyle q_{t}}\) would move if placed in the field. For a negative point source charge, the direction is radially inwards.

The magnitude of the electric field, \(|\mathbf{E}|\), can be derived from Coulomb's law, eq. \eqref{eq:coulomb1}, by choosing one of the point charges to be the source (\(q_1=q\)), and the other to be the test charge (\(q_2=q_t\)), and is given by:

\begin{equation}
{\displaystyle |\mathbf {E} |=|\mathbf{F}|\cdot \frac{1}{q_t}=\frac{1}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}{\frac {|q|}{r^{2}}}}
\label{eq:coulomb3}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Electric Potential Energy}\label{electric-potential-energy}

The electric potential energy of a system of point charges is defined as the work required to assemble this system of charges by bringing them close together from an infinite distance.

The electrostatic potential energy, \(E\), of one point charge \(q\) at position \(\mathbf{r}\) in the presence of an electric field \(\mathbf{E}\) is defined as the negative of the work \(W\) done by the electrostatic force to bring it from a reference position at infinite distance \(\mathbf{r}_{\infty}\) to that position \(\mathbf{r}\):

\begin{equation}
{\displaystyle E=-W_{\mathbf{r}_{\infty}\rightarrow \mathbf{r}}=-\int _{{\mathbf {r}}_{\infty}}^{\mathbf {r} }q\mathbf {E} (\mathbf {r'} )\cdot d \mathbf {r'} },
\label{eq:coulomb4}
\end{equation}

where \(\mathbf{E}\) is the electrostatic field and \(d\mathbf{r'}\) is the displacement vector in a curve from the reference position \(\mathbf{r}_{\infty}\) to the final position \(\mathbf{r}\).

In the simplest case of two point charges \(q_1\) and \(q_2\), the electrostatic potential energy at position \(r\), can be derived from Coulomb's Law, eq. \eqref{eq:coulomb1}, as:

\begin{equation}
{\displaystyle E=|\mathbf{F}|\cdot r = {\frac{1}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}}{\frac{q_1q_2}{r}}}.
\label{eq:coulomb5}
\end{equation}

This last equation will become very important in future chapters. Notice the difference between eq. \eqref{eq:coulomb5} for the \emph{energy}, \(E\), and eq. \eqref{eq:coulomb3} for the \emph{magnitude of the electric field}, \(|\mathbf{E}|\).

\section{Classical Waves}\label{ClassicalWaves}

The last ingredient of classical physics that we need is the description of waves. Waves are everywhere: on the surface of water, in a vibrating guitar string, in sound, and in light. As we will see in the next two chapters, the central idea of quantum mechanics is that particles such as electrons also behave like waves, and the mathematics of quantum mechanics is, to a large extent, the mathematics of waves.

\subsection{Describing a wave}\label{describing-a-wave}

A wave is a disturbance that repeats itself in space and time. Think of a long rope that you shake up and down at one end: a pattern of crests and troughs travels along the rope, even though each piece of rope only moves up and down. A snapshot of such a wave is shown in figure \ref{fig:Fig1c1}. We can describe it with a few quantities:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  The \textbf{amplitude}, \(A\), is the maximum displacement from the rest position.
\item
  The \textbf{wavelength}, \(\lambda\), is the distance between two neighboring crests (or troughs).
\item
  The \textbf{frequency}, \(\nu\), is the number of crests that pass by a fixed point every second. Its inverse, \(1/\nu\), is the \textbf{period}, the time between two crests.
\item
  The \textbf{speed}, \(v\), is how fast the crests move. Since one wavelength passes by every period, the three quantities are related by:
\end{itemize}

\begin{equation}
v = \lambda\nu.
\label{eq:wave1}
\end{equation}

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.9\linewidth,alt={A sine wave at time zero and the same wave shifted slightly to the right a moment later, with arrows marking the wavelength between two crests, the amplitude from the axis to a crest, and the direction of travel.}]{./img/Wave_traveling} 

}

\caption{A traveling wave at $t=0$ (solid line) and a short time later (dashed line), showing its wavelength and amplitude. The pattern moves to the right with speed $v$.}\label{fig:Fig1c1}
\end{figure}

Mathematically, the displacement \(y\) of the rope at position \(x\) and time \(t\) for a wave moving to the right can be written as:

\begin{equation}
y(x,t) = A\sin(kx-\omega t),
\label{eq:wave2}
\end{equation}

where we have introduced two quantities that make the formulas simpler: the \textbf{wave vector}, \(k=\frac{2\pi}{\lambda}\), which tells us how many radians the wave advances per unit length, and the \textbf{angular frequency}, \(\omega=2\pi\nu\), which tells us how many radians it advances per unit time. You can check that eq. \eqref{eq:wave2} repeats itself every time \(x\) increases by \(\lambda\) (at fixed \(t\)) or \(t\) increases by \(1/\nu\) (at fixed \(x\)), and that the crests move with speed \(v=\omega/k=\lambda\nu\).

\subsubsection*{A useful mathematical tool: complex waves}\label{a-useful-mathematical-tool-complex-waves}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{A useful mathematical tool: complex waves}

In quantum mechanics, waves are usually written using complex numbers instead of sines and cosines. The reason is \textbf{Euler's formula}, which connects the exponential function with the trigonometric functions:

\begin{equation}
e^{i\theta}=\cos\theta + i\sin\theta,
\label{eq:euler}
\end{equation}

where \(i=\sqrt{-1}\) is the imaginary unit. Using this formula, we can write a wave as:

\begin{equation}
y(x,t) = A\,e^{i(kx-\omega t)} = A\cos(kx-\omega t) + iA\sin(kx-\omega t),
\label{eq:wave3}
\end{equation}

which contains both a cosine wave (the real part) and a sine wave (the imaginary part). The advantage is that exponentials are much easier to differentiate than sines and cosines: taking a derivative with respect to \(x\) simply multiplies the function by \(ik\), since \(\frac{d}{dx}e^{ikx}=ike^{ikx}\). We will start from exactly this form of a wave when we introduce the Schrödinger equation in chapter \ref{Schrodinger}.

\subsection{The wave equation}\label{the-wave-equation}

Just as Newton's equation describes the motion of a particle, there is an equation that describes the motion of a wave. For a wave moving along a string, it is called the \textbf{classical wave equation}:

\begin{equation}
\frac{\partial^2 y}{\partial x^2} = \frac{1}{v^2}\frac{\partial^2 y}{\partial t^2}.
\label{eq:waveeq}
\end{equation}

The left-hand side measures how curved the string is at a given point, and the right-hand side measures how fast that point accelerates up or down. In words, the equation says that the more a piece of string is curved, the faster it accelerates back towards a straight line. We can check that eq. \eqref{eq:wave2} is a solution: taking two derivatives with respect to \(x\) gives \(-k^2y\), and taking two derivatives with respect to \(t\) gives \(-\omega^2y\). Replacing them in eq. \eqref{eq:waveeq}, we obtain \(-k^2y=-\frac{\omega^2}{v^2}y\), which is true as long as \(v=\omega/k\), the relation between speed, frequency, and wavelength that we already found.

An important property of the wave equation is that it is \textbf{linear}: if \(y_1(x,t)\) and \(y_2(x,t)\) are two solutions, their sum, \(y_1+y_2\), is also a solution. This is called the \textbf{principle of superposition}, and it means that when two waves meet, their displacements simply add up. If the crests of the two waves coincide (they are \emph{in phase}), they reinforce each other and produce a larger wave, which is called \textbf{constructive interference}. If the crests of one wave coincide with the troughs of the other (they are \emph{out of phase}), they cancel each other, which is called \textbf{destructive interference}. The two cases are shown in figure \ref{fig:Fig2c1}. Interference is the signature of wave behavior, and we will see in chapter \ref{Weirdness} that it is also observed for electrons.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=1\linewidth,alt={Two panels. Left: two identical sine waves in phase add to a wave of twice the amplitude. Right: two sine waves out of phase cancel, giving a flat line.}]{./img/Wave_interference} 

}

\caption{Superposition of two waves with the same wavelength. Left: when the waves are in phase, they add up to a wave with twice the amplitude (constructive interference). Right: when they are out of phase, they cancel each other (destructive interference).}\label{fig:Fig2c1}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Standing waves on a string}\label{standing-waves-on-a-string}

Something very interesting happens when a wave is confined, as on a guitar string of length \(L\) fixed at both ends. The waves travel along the string, bounce back at the ends, and interfere with themselves. Most of them cancel out, and only a few special patterns survive. These patterns do not travel along the string; they just oscillate up and down in place, and they are called \textbf{standing waves}.

To find them, we look for solutions of the wave equation in which the shape of the string does not change, but only its size, which oscillates in time:

\begin{equation}
y(x,t) = \psi(x)\cos(\omega t).
\label{eq:standing1}
\end{equation}

Here \(\psi(x)\) describes the shape of the string, and \(\cos(\omega t)\) describes how it oscillates. Replacing eq. \eqref{eq:standing1} in the wave equation, eq. \eqref{eq:waveeq}, the time-dependent factor cancels out, and we are left with an equation for the shape only:

\begin{equation}
\frac{d^2 \psi}{d x^2} = -k^2\psi(x), \qquad k=\frac{\omega}{v}.
\label{eq:standing2}
\end{equation}

The solutions of this equation are sines and cosines, \(\psi(x)=a\sin(kx)+b\cos(kx)\). Now we use the fact that the string is fixed at both ends, which gives two \textbf{boundary conditions}:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  At \(x=0\), the string cannot move, so \(\psi(0)=0\). Since \(\sin(0)=0\) and \(\cos(0)=1\), this requires \(b=0\), and \(\psi(x)=a\sin(kx)\).
\item
  At \(x=L\), the string cannot move either, so \(\psi(L)=a\sin(kL)=0\). If \(a=0\) the string does not vibrate at all, so we need \(\sin(kL)=0\), which is only true when \(kL\) is a whole multiple of \(\pi\).
\end{itemize}

The second condition can only be satisfied by special values of \(k\), and therefore of the wavelength, \(\lambda=2\pi/k\), and of the frequency, \(\nu=v/\lambda\):

\begin{equation}
k_n = \frac{n\pi}{L}, \qquad \lambda_n = \frac{2L}{n}, \qquad \nu_n = \frac{nv}{2L}, \qquad n = 1, 2, 3, \ldots
\label{eq:standing3}
\end{equation}

and the corresponding shapes are:

\begin{equation}
\psi_n(x) = a\sin\left(\frac{n\pi x}{L}\right).
\label{eq:standing4}
\end{equation}

The first four standing waves are shown in figure \ref{fig:Fig3c1}.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.7\linewidth,alt={The first four standing waves on a string fixed at both ends, stacked vertically. They have one, two, three, and four half-wavelengths between the ends, and zero, one, two, and three nodes, marked as dots.}]{./img/Wave_standing} 

}

\caption{The first four standing waves on a string of length $L$ fixed at both ends (black squares). The solid and dashed lines show the string at its two extreme positions, and the light lines show intermediate positions. The nodes are reported as purple dots.}\label{fig:Fig3c1}
\end{figure}

A few important considerations can be made by analyzing these results:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Quantization}: a confined wave can only have certain wavelengths and frequencies, labeled by a whole number, \(n\). This is why a guitar string plays a definite note (the frequency with \(n=1\), called the \emph{fundamental}) together with its \emph{overtones} (\(n=2,3,\ldots\)), and not any frequency at all. Notice that nothing in the wave equation itself is quantized: the quantization comes entirely from the boundary conditions, i.e., from confining the wave.
\item
  \textbf{Nodes}: the standing wave with quantum number \(n\) has \(n-1\) points between the two ends where the string never moves, called \textbf{nodes}. The higher the frequency, the more nodes the wave has.
\item
  \textbf{The link with quantum mechanics}: in chapter \ref{Models}, we will solve the Schrödinger equation for an electron confined in a one-dimensional box, and we will obtain \emph{exactly} the same functions as in eq. \eqref{eq:standing4}, with the same conditions on the wavelength. The quantization of the energy of atoms and molecules has the same origin as the quantization of the notes of a guitar: a wave that is confined in a region of space.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Light as an electromagnetic wave}\label{light-as-an-electromagnetic-wave}

One more type of wave will be important for us: light. In the 19th century, James Clerk Maxwell showed that light is a wave of oscillating electric and magnetic fields, called an \emph{electromagnetic wave}. In a vacuum, all electromagnetic waves travel at the same speed, the speed of light, \(c=2.998\times10^8\;\text{m/s}\), so eq. \eqref{eq:wave1} becomes:

\begin{equation}
c = \lambda\nu.
\label{eq:light1}
\end{equation}

Different wavelengths correspond to different types of radiation, which together form the \emph{electromagnetic spectrum}: from radio waves (wavelengths of meters or more), to microwaves (millimeters to centimeters), infrared (micrometers), visible light (\(400\)--\(700\;\text{nm}\)), ultraviolet, X-rays, and gamma rays (shorter than a billionth of a meter). In classical physics, the energy carried by an electromagnetic wave depends on its amplitude (the brighter the light, the more energy it carries), and not on its frequency. As we will see in the next chapter, this classical picture of light is one of the first things that quantum mechanics had to change.

\section{Chapter Review}\label{rev1}

\subsection{Study Questions}\label{quest1}

\textbf{1. What is the central quantity in Newtonian mechanics?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Force\\
\item
  Action\\
\item
  Wavefunction\\
\item
  Electric field\\
\item
  Partition function
\end{enumerate}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \(F_{\text{gravity}} = +mg\)
\item
  \(F_{\text{gravity}} = -mg\)
\item
  \(F_{\text{gravity}} = -k x\)
\item
  \(F_{\text{gravity}} = qE\)
\item
  \(F_{\text{gravity}} = 0\)

\begin{enumerate}

\tightlist
\item
  It is valid only for very small masses\\
\item
  It is valid only in two dimensions\\
\item
  It assumes variable gravitational acceleration
\item
  It requires relativistic corrections\\
\item
  It does not depend on the mass of the ball
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{4. In the Hamiltonian formulation of classical mechanics, what does the Hamiltonian \(H\) represent for a particle?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  the force acting on the particle\\
\item
  the total energy, \(K+V\)\\
\item
  the difference between kinetic and potential energy, \(K-V\)\\
\item
  the momentum of the particle\\
\item
  the acceleration of the particle
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{5. Which of the following is the Hamiltonian of a particle of mass \(m\) moving in one dimension with potential energy \(V(x)\)?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \(H = \dfrac{p^2}{2m} + V(x)\)\\
\item
  \(H = \dfrac{p^2}{2m} - V(x)\)\\
\item
  \(H = m\ddot{x}\)\\
\item
  \(H = p\,V(x)\)\\
\item
  \(H = \dfrac{p}{m} + V(x)\)
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{6. For a traveling wave with wavelength \(\lambda\) and frequency \(\nu\), what is the speed \(v\) of the wave?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \(v = \lambda/\nu\)\\
\item
  \(v = \nu/\lambda\)\\
\item
  \(v = \lambda\nu\)\\
\item
  \(v = 2\pi\lambda\)\\
\item
  \(v = \lambda^2\nu\)
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{7. What are the allowed wavelengths of the standing waves on a string of length \(L\) fixed at both ends?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  any wavelength\\
\item
  \(\lambda_n = nL\)\\
\item
  \(\lambda_n = L/2n\)\\
\item
  \(\lambda_n = 2L/n\), with \(n=1,2,3,\ldots\)\\
\item
  \(\lambda_n = 2\pi L\)
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{8. What is the origin of the quantization of the frequencies of a vibrating string?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  the boundary conditions imposed by the fixed ends of the string\\
\item
  the mass of the string\\
\item
  the principle of superposition\\
\item
  the amplitude of the vibration\\
\item
  friction with the air
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{9. The magnitude of the electric field \(|E|\) created by a point charge \(q\) at distance \(r\) is given by which of the following formulas?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \(|E| = \dfrac{1}{4\pi \varepsilon_0} \dfrac{|q|}{r^2}\)
\item
  \(|E| = \dfrac{1}{4\pi \varepsilon_0} |q| r^2\)
\item
  \(|E| = \dfrac{|q|}{r}\)
\item
  \(|E| = k_B T / r^2\)
\item
  \(|E| = \dfrac{1}{4\pi \varepsilon_0} \dfrac{|q|}{r}\)
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{10. The electrostatic potential energy \(E\) of two point charges \(q_1\) and \(q_2\) separated by distance \(r\) is given by which of the following formulas?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \(E = \dfrac{1}{4\pi \varepsilon_0} \dfrac{q_1 q_2}{r^2}\)
\item
  \(E = \dfrac{1}{4\pi \varepsilon_0} \dfrac{q_1 q_2}{r}\)
\item
  \(E = q_1 q_2 r\)
\item
  \(E = k_B T r\)
\item
  \(E = \dfrac{1}{4\pi \varepsilon_0} q_1 q_2 r^2\)
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

Answers: 1.a, 2.b, 3.e, 4.b, 5.a, 6.c, 7.d, 8.a, 9.a, 10.b

\chapter{The Failure of Classical Physics}\label{Motivation}

\section{Historical Context}\label{historical-context}

In the previous chapter, we reviewed the fundamental concepts of classical physics. In this chapter, we will see how these concepts fail when applied to atoms and light, and how these failures led to the development of quantum mechanics.

By the end of the 19th century, many physicists believed that their job was almost done. Newton's mechanics described the motion of everything from falling apples to planets, and Maxwell's equations described light as an electromagnetic wave. Only a few small puzzles were left to solve. Within thirty years, those few small puzzles had brought down the whole classical picture of the microscopic world, and replaced it with quantum mechanics: the theory that is at the foundation of all of modern chemistry.

The first cracks came from the discovery that atoms are not indivisible. J.J. Thomson discovered the electron in 1897, a year after Henri Becquerel had discovered radioactivity, which Marie and Pierre Curie then studied in depth. Atoms were clearly made of smaller charged pieces, and physicists started to build models of how these pieces fit together. Every attempt to describe them with classical physics, however, ran into contradictions. In 1911, Ernest Rutherford showed that the positive charge of an atom is concentrated in a tiny nucleus, with the electrons around it. According to classical electromagnetism, an electron orbiting a nucleus should continuously radiate energy and spiral into the nucleus in a fraction of a second. Atoms, in other words, should not exist.

The key idea that resolved these contradictions was \emph{quantization}. In 1900, Max Planck showed that he could explain the light emitted by hot objects if he assumed that energy is exchanged only in discrete amounts, or ``quanta.'' In 1905, Albert Einstein used the same idea to explain the photoelectric effect, proposing that light itself is made of quanta, later called photons. In 1913, Niels Bohr applied quantization to the hydrogen atom, assuming that the electron could move only on certain orbits with fixed energies, and that light is emitted or absorbed when the electron jumps between them. Bohr's model reproduced the spectrum of hydrogen with remarkable accuracy, but it worked only for atoms and ions with a single electron, and it had no justification other than the fact that it worked. In 1924, Louis de Broglie suggested that, if light waves can behave like particles, then particles such as electrons might behave like waves.

A complete theory finally arrived in 1925--1926, in two different forms. Werner Heisenberg, together with Max Born and Pascual Jordan, developed \emph{matrix mechanics}, a formulation based on arrays of numbers (matrices) that deals only with observable quantities, such as the frequencies and intensities of spectral lines. Shortly after, Erwin Schrödinger developed \emph{wave mechanics}, based on the equation that now bears his name and that describes particles using waves. The two formulations looked completely different, but they were soon shown to be mathematically equivalent. Together, they form the theory that we will study in this textbook.

In the rest of this chapter, we will look in more detail at the experiments that classical physics could not explain, and at how their explanations led to the main ideas of quantum mechanics. These are the ``ultraviolet catastrophe,'' the photoelectric effect, the line spectra of atoms, and the wave-particle duality.

\section{The Ultraviolet Catastrophe}\label{the-ultraviolet-catastrophe}

Every object emits electromagnetic radiation, and the hotter the object, the more radiation it emits and the shorter its wavelengths. This is why a piece of metal heated in a flame first glows red, then orange, and then white. To study this radiation without having to worry about the details of a specific material, physicists use an idealized object called a \emph{black body}, which absorbs all the radiation that hits it and emits radiation that depends only on its temperature. The radiation emitted by a black body at a few temperatures is shown in figure \ref{fig:Fig1c2}.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.9\linewidth,alt={Plot of emitted intensity versus wavelength for a black body at 5800, 4500, and 3000 kelvin. Each curve rises to a peak and falls to zero at short wavelength; hotter curves are higher and peak at shorter wavelength. The classical Rayleigh-Jeans curve rises without limit at short wavelength.}]{./img/Blackbody} 

}

\caption{Intensity of the radiation emitted by a black body at three temperatures, calculated with Planck's law (solid lines). The purple dots mark the maximum of each curve. The dashed line is the classical Rayleigh–Jeans prediction at 5800 K, which diverges at short wavelengths. The shaded region is the visible part of the spectrum.}\label{fig:Fig1c2}
\end{figure}

Using classical electromagnetism and classical statistical mechanics, Lord Rayleigh and James Jeans derived an equation for the intensity of the radiation emitted by a black body at temperature \(T\) as a function of the wavelength \(\lambda\). The result, known as the Rayleigh--Jeans law, is:

\begin{equation}
I(\lambda,T)={\frac {2ck_{\mathrm{B}}T}{\lambda^{4}}},
\label{eq:rjlaw}
\end{equation}

where \(I\) is the intensity of the radiation (more precisely, the power emitted per unit area of the emitting surface, per unit solid angle, and per unit wavelength, a quantity called \emph{spectral radiance}), \(c\) is the speed of light, \(k_{\mathrm{B}}\) is the Boltzmann constant, and \(T\) is the temperature in kelvins. This formula works well at long wavelengths, but it fails dramatically at short ones (dashed line in figure \ref{fig:Fig1c2}). If we take the limit for \(\lambda \rightarrow 0\) in eq. \eqref{eq:rjlaw}, we obtain \(I \rightarrow \infty\). In other words, the shorter the wavelength (towards the UV and beyond), the more radiation the black body should emit, and the total energy emitted over all wavelengths should be infinite. This prediction is clearly absurd: if it were true, every warm object would be emitting an infinite amount of energy as UV light, X-rays, and gamma rays. This failure of classical physics is called the \textbf{ultraviolet catastrophe} (or Rayleigh--Jeans catastrophe).

Max Planck solved the problem in 1900 by making an assumption that had no justification in classical physics: the energy of the oscillating electrons that emit the radiation cannot take any value, but only integer multiples of a minimum amount proportional to the frequency \(\nu\) of the radiation:

\begin{equation}
E = n h \nu = n h \frac{c}{\lambda},
\label{eq:uvcat}
\end{equation}

with \(n\) a positive integer. With this assumption, Planck obtained the following formula for the intensity:

\begin{equation}
I(\lambda ,T)={\frac {2hc^{2}}{\lambda ^{5}}}{\frac {1}{e^{hc/(\lambda k_{\mathrm {B} }T)}-1}}.
\label{eq:uvcatpl}
\end{equation}

At short wavelengths, the exponential in the denominator grows much faster than \(\lambda^5\) decreases, and \(I\) goes to zero as \(\lambda \rightarrow 0\), in agreement with the experiments. (At long wavelengths, instead, \(e^{x}-1\approx x\), and Planck's law reduces to the Rayleigh--Jeans law.) Physically, quantization works because at short wavelengths the minimum quantum of energy, \(hc/\lambda\), becomes much larger than the typical thermal energy, \(k_{\mathrm{B}}T\), and the high-frequency oscillations are simply too ``expensive'' to be excited. By fitting eq. \eqref{eq:uvcatpl} to the experimental data, Planck also obtained the value of the new constant, \(h = 6.626 \times 10^{-34} \; \text{J s}\), which is now called the \emph{Planck constant}.

A few important considerations can be made by analyzing figure \ref{fig:Fig1c2}:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Color and temperature}: as the temperature increases, the maximum of the curve moves to shorter wavelengths. The position of the maximum follows a simple rule, called \emph{Wien's displacement law}, which can be derived from eq. \eqref{eq:uvcatpl}: \(\lambda_\text{max}T = 2.898\times10^{-3}\;\text{m K}\). The surface of the Sun (\(T\approx5800\;\text{K}\)) has its maximum at \(\lambda_\text{max}\approx500\;\text{nm}\), in the middle of the visible region (our eyes evolved to see the light that the Sun emits the most). An incandescent light bulb (\(T\approx3000\;\text{K}\)) has its maximum at about \(970\;\text{nm}\), in the infrared, which is why it looks yellowish and why most of its energy is wasted as heat. Our bodies (\(T\approx310\;\text{K}\)) emit mostly at about \(9{,}000\;\text{nm}\), in the infrared, where thermal cameras can see it.
\item
  \textbf{Brightness and temperature}: the area under each curve, which is the total power emitted, grows very quickly with temperature (as \(T^4\), a result known as the Stefan--Boltzmann law). Doubling the temperature of an object makes it emit sixteen times more energy.
\item
  \textbf{Where classical physics fails}: the classical and quantum curves agree at long wavelengths, where the quanta \(h\nu\) are small compared to \(k_\mathrm{B}T\) and the energy behaves as if it were continuous. They disagree completely at short wavelengths, where the quanta are large. This is a pattern that we will see again: quantum effects become important when the spacing between energy levels is large compared to the thermal energy.
\end{enumerate}

Planck himself considered quantization a mathematical trick rather than a property of nature, and the idea was not taken seriously until Einstein used it to explain the photoelectric effect.

\section{The Photoelectric Effect}\label{the-photoelectric-effect}

When light shines on a clean metal surface, electrons can be ejected from it. This phenomenon is called the photoelectric effect. It was first observed by Heinrich Hertz in 1887, and studied in detail by Philipp Lenard around 1900. Albert Einstein explained it in 1905, proposing that light energy is quantized into discrete particles, called photons, each with an energy proportional to its frequency.

To see why an explanation was needed, let's start from the classical prediction. As we saw at the end of chapter \ref{Classical}, in classical physics light is a continuous electromagnetic wave, and the energy that it delivers every second depends on its amplitude (its brightness, or \emph{intensity}), and not on its frequency. If we apply this picture to the photoelectric effect, we expect the electrons in the metal to absorb energy from the wave continuously, and to be ejected when they have accumulated enough of it to escape the surface. A brighter light delivers energy faster, so the electrons should come out with more energy. In other words, the kinetic energy of the ejected electrons, \(K\), should be proportional to the intensity of the incident light, \(I_{\text{in}}\):

\begin{equation}
K= c_{\text{PE}} \; I_{\text{in}},
\label{eq:pe3}
\end{equation}

with \(c_{\text{PE}}\) being a constant that depends on the metal and on the characteristics of the incident light. The same picture also makes two other predictions: any color of light should be able to eject electrons, provided that it is bright enough, and with a very dim light the electrons should come out only after some delay, the time necessary to accumulate enough energy.

The experiments contradicted all three predictions. The kinetic energy of the ejected electrons does not depend on the intensity of the light at all (a brighter light ejects \emph{more} electrons, but not faster ones), while it increases with the frequency of the light. Below a certain threshold frequency, which depends on the metal, no electrons are ejected, no matter how bright the light is. Above the threshold, electrons are ejected immediately, even with very dim light.

Einstein explained these results by proposing that a beam of light is not a continuous wave, but a stream of discrete packets of energy, the photons. The energy of each photon is equal to the smallest possible energy quantum in Planck's theory of the black body (corresponding to \(n=1\) in eq. \eqref{eq:uvcat}):

\begin{equation}
E = h \nu.
\label{eq:pe0}
\end{equation}

Each photon interacts with a single electron, and gives it all of its energy at once. If the energy of the photon is smaller than the energy required to remove an electron from the metal, called the \emph{work function}, \(\phi\), the electron cannot escape, and since there is no way of accumulating the energy of several photons, increasing the intensity (i.e., the number of photons) does not help. If the energy of the photon is larger than the work function, the electron escapes immediately, and the extra energy becomes its kinetic energy. The maximum kinetic energy of the ejected electrons, \(K_{\text{max}}\), is therefore:

\begin{equation}
K_{\text{max}} = E_{\text{in}} - \phi = h\nu_{\text{in}} - \phi,
\label{eq:pe4}
\end{equation}

which depends on the frequency of the light, but not on its intensity, in agreement with the experiments. Eq. \eqref{eq:pe4} is plotted for three metals in figure \ref{fig:Fig2c2}. For each metal, \(K_\text{max}\) is zero up to the threshold frequency, \(\nu_0=\phi/h\), and then increases linearly with the frequency. The lines for all metals are parallel, since their slope is always the Planck constant, \(h\). In 1916, Robert Millikan measured these lines very carefully, confirming Einstein's prediction and obtaining a value of \(h\) in agreement with Planck's. Einstein received the Nobel Prize in Physics in 1921 for this explanation.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.9\linewidth,alt={Plot of maximum electron kinetic energy versus light frequency for cesium, sodium, and zinc. Each is zero below a threshold frequency and then rises as a straight line; the three lines are parallel with slope equal to the Planck constant. Zinc's threshold is in the ultraviolet.}]{./img/Photoelectric} 

}

\caption{Maximum kinetic energy of the electrons ejected from three metals as a function of the frequency of the light, according to the photoelectric equation. The threshold frequencies are marked with purple dots, and the shaded region is the visible part of the spectrum. Work functions are approximate.}\label{fig:Fig2c2}
\end{figure}

For example, the work function of sodium is about \(2.3\;\text{eV}\).\footnote{The electronvolt, eV, is the energy gained by an electron accelerated through a potential difference of 1 volt: \(1\;\text{eV}=1.602\times10^{-19}\;\text{J}\). It is a convenient unit for the energies of single electrons and photons. A useful shortcut for photons is \(E(\text{eV})\approx1240/\lambda(\text{nm})\).} The threshold wavelength is \(\lambda_0=hc/\phi\approx1240/2.3\approx540\;\text{nm}\), so green, blue, and violet light can eject electrons from sodium, but yellow and red light cannot, no matter how bright. Ultraviolet light with \(\lambda=250\;\text{nm}\) carries photons of \(4.96\;\text{eV}\), and ejects electrons with a maximum kinetic energy of \(4.96-2.3\approx2.7\;\text{eV}\), corresponding to a speed of about \(10^6\;\text{m/s}\).

\subsection{Why chemists care about photons}\label{why-chemists-care-about-photons}

The energy of a single photon looks tiny in joules, but chemists usually think in terms of moles. A photon of green light (\(\lambda=500\;\text{nm}\)) has an energy of \(E=hc/\lambda=3.97\times10^{-19}\;\text{J}\), or \(2.48\;\text{eV}\). A mole of such photons carries \(3.97\times10^{-19}\;\text{J}\times6.022\times10^{23}\;\text{mol}^{-1}\approx239\;\text{kJ/mol}\). This is the same order of magnitude as the energy of a chemical bond (a \(\mathrm{C}-\mathrm{C}\) single bond is about \(350\;\text{kJ/mol}\)). Ultraviolet photons (\(\lambda=250\;\text{nm}\), \(479\;\text{kJ/mol}\)) carry enough energy to break chemical bonds, which is why UV light causes sunburns and can be used to sterilize surfaces, while visible photons can only excite electrons in molecules (which is how our eyes, and photosynthesis, work), and infrared photons can only make molecules vibrate. The interaction between light and molecules, one photon at a time, is the basis of spectroscopy, which we will discuss in chapter \ref{Spectroscopy}.

\section{Atomic Spectra and the Bohr Model}\label{atomic-spectra-and-the-bohr-model}

The third failure of classical physics is the one that matters most for chemistry, since it concerns atoms themselves. When a gas of hydrogen atoms is heated or excited by an electric discharge, it emits light. If this light is passed through a prism, it does not produce a continuous rainbow, like the light from a black body, but a series of sharp lines, each with a precise wavelength. In the visible region, hydrogen emits only four lines: red (\(656\;\text{nm}\)), blue-green (\(486\;\text{nm}\)), violet (\(434\;\text{nm}\)), and deep violet (\(410\;\text{nm}\)). Every element has its own characteristic set of lines, which can be used to identify it, as a fingerprint (this is how helium was discovered in the spectrum of the Sun, before it was found on Earth).

In 1885, Johann Balmer found a simple formula that reproduces the wavelengths of the visible lines of hydrogen, and in 1888 Johannes Rydberg generalized it to all the lines of hydrogen, including those in the ultraviolet and infrared:

\begin{equation}
\frac{1}{\lambda} = R_H\left(\frac{1}{n_1^2}-\frac{1}{n_2^2}\right), \qquad n_2 > n_1,
\label{eq:rydberg}
\end{equation}

where \(n_1\) and \(n_2\) are positive integers, and \(R_H=1.0968\times10^{7}\;\text{m}^{-1}\) is the \emph{Rydberg constant} for hydrogen. The lines with \(n_1=1\) are in the ultraviolet (the \emph{Lyman series}), those with \(n_1=2\) are mostly in the visible (the \emph{Balmer series}), and those with \(n_1=3\) are in the infrared (the \emph{Paschen series}). For example, for \(n_1=2\) and \(n_2=3\), eq. \eqref{eq:rydberg} gives \(1/\lambda=R_H\cdot\frac{5}{36}\), or \(\lambda=656\;\text{nm}\), the red line of hydrogen. The formula worked perfectly, but nobody knew why. Classical physics could not explain why atoms emit only certain wavelengths. Worse, as we mentioned at the beginning of this chapter, according to classical physics an electron orbiting a nucleus should continuously emit radiation of all wavelengths while spiraling into the nucleus.

In 1913, Niels Bohr proposed a model that explained the hydrogen spectrum by combining Rutherford's nuclear atom with Planck's idea of quantization. The \textbf{Bohr model} is based on two assumptions:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  The electron moves around the nucleus only on certain circular orbits, called \emph{stationary states}, on which it does not emit radiation. On these orbits, the angular momentum of the electron is quantized: it can only be a whole multiple of \(\hbar=h/2\pi\), \(L=n\hbar\), with \(n=1,2,3,\ldots\)
\item
  The atom emits or absorbs light only when the electron jumps from one orbit to another. The energy of the photon is equal to the difference between the energies of the two orbits, \(h\nu=E_{n_2}-E_{n_1}\).
\end{enumerate}

Combining the first assumption with the classical laws of motion for an electron attracted by a proton (Coulomb's law, chapter \ref{Classical}), Bohr found that the allowed energies of the electron are:

\begin{equation}
E_n = -\frac{m_e e^4}{8\varepsilon_0^2h^2}\frac{1}{n^2} = -\frac{13.6\;\text{eV}}{n^2}, \qquad n=1,2,3,\ldots
\label{eq:bohr1}
\end{equation}

and the radius of the smallest orbit, now called the \emph{Bohr radius}, is \(a_0=0.529\;\text{Å}\). The energy levels of eq. \eqref{eq:bohr1} and the transitions between them are shown in figure \ref{fig:Fig3c2}.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.9\linewidth,alt={Energy level diagram of hydrogen with levels at minus 13.6 electronvolts divided by n squared. Downward arrows show the Lyman series ending on n equals 1, the Balmer series ending on n equals 2, and the Paschen series ending on n equals 3. Below, a dark strip shows the visible Balmer lines at 656, 486, 434, 410, and 397 nanometers.}]{./img/H_spectrum} 

}

\caption{Top: energy levels of the hydrogen atom according to the Bohr model, and the transitions that produce the Lyman, Balmer, and Paschen series of emission lines. Bottom: the visible lines of the Balmer series, with their wavelengths in nm.}\label{fig:Fig3c2}
\end{figure}

A few important considerations can be made by analyzing this diagram:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Quantized energy levels explain line spectra}: since the electron can only have the energies of eq. \eqref{eq:bohr1}, the photons emitted when it jumps from a higher level to a lower one can only have the energies \(E_{n_2}-E_{n_1}=13.6\;\text{eV}\left(\frac{1}{n_1^2}-\frac{1}{n_2^2}\right)\). Dividing by \(hc\) gives exactly the Rydberg formula, eq. \eqref{eq:rydberg}, with \(R_H=13.6\;\text{eV}/hc=1.097\times10^{7}\;\text{m}^{-1}\), in perfect agreement with the experimental value.
\item
  \textbf{Ionization energy}: the levels get closer and closer as \(n\) increases, and they converge to \(E=0\), which corresponds to the electron completely separated from the proton. The energy needed to remove the electron from the lowest level, \(13.6\;\text{eV}\) (or \(1{,}312\;\text{kJ/mol}\)), is the ionization energy of hydrogen, again in perfect agreement with experiments.
\item
  \textbf{The meaning of the series}: all lines of a series end on the same lower level. The Lyman series, which ends on \(n=1\), involves the largest energy jumps, and it is in the ultraviolet. The Balmer series, which ends on \(n=2\), involves smaller jumps, and its first lines are visible.
\end{enumerate}

Despite this spectacular success, the Bohr model has serious problems. It works only for hydrogen and for ions with a single electron (such as \(\mathrm{He}^+\) and \(\mathrm{Li}^{2+}\)), and it fails already for the helium atom. It cannot explain the intensities of the lines, nor why some lines split in a magnetic field. Most importantly, it does not explain \emph{why} angular momentum should be quantized, or why an electron on an orbit should not radiate: these are assumptions made to fit the data. As we will see in chapter \ref{Hydrogen}, the Schrödinger equation gives exactly the same energies as eq. \eqref{eq:bohr1}, but without any of these assumptions, and with a completely different picture of the electron, which is not a particle moving on an orbit, but a wave.

\section{Wave-Particle Duality}\label{wave-particle-duality}

If light is made of particles, these particles should also carry momentum, and Einstein showed that the momentum of a photon is:

\begin{equation}
p = \frac{h}{\lambda}.
\label{eq:wp1}
\end{equation}

We can see where this result comes from in two steps. First, combining \(E = h \nu\) for a photon with \(\lambda \nu = c\) for an electromagnetic wave, we obtain the energy of a photon as a function of its wavelength:

\begin{equation}
E = \frac{h c}{\lambda}.
\label{eq:wp2}
\end{equation}

Second, according to Einstein's theory of relativity, the energy of a particle with no mass, such as a photon, is related to its momentum by \(E = pc\).\footnote{This might be surprising, since in classical mechanics momentum is \(p=mv\), and a particle without mass should have no momentum. In relativity, however, momentum and energy are more general concepts, and a massless particle that always travels at the speed of light can carry both. The fact that light carries momentum can be measured directly: light pushes on the objects that absorb or reflect it, a phenomenon called \emph{radiation pressure}.} Replacing this relation in eq. \eqref{eq:wp2} and solving for \(\lambda\) we obtain:

\begin{equation}
\lambda = \frac{h}{p},
\label{eq:wp3}
\end{equation}

which is equivalent to eq. \eqref{eq:wp1}. Light, therefore, behaves as a wave in some experiments (it can be diffracted, and it has a wavelength) and as a particle in others (it comes in packets with energy \(h \nu\) and momentum \(h/\lambda\)). In 1924, Louis de Broglie made the bold suggestion that this dual behavior is not unique to light, and that matter should also behave like a wave, with a wavelength given by the same eq. \eqref{eq:wp3}. In 1927, Clinton Davisson and Lester Germer confirmed de Broglie's hypothesis by observing a diffraction pattern when they shot a beam of electrons at a nickel crystal, exactly as if the electrons were waves. We will discuss the wave behavior of electrons in more detail in chapter \ref{Weirdness}.

Why don't we notice the wave nature of everyday objects? Because \(h\) is so small. A baseball with a mass of \(0.145\text{ kg}\) thrown at \(40\text{ m/s}\) has a de Broglie wavelength of \(\lambda = h/mv \approx 1\times10^{-34}\text{ m}\), far too small to be observed in any experiment. An electron accelerated to a speed of \(10^6\text{ m/s}\), instead, has a wavelength of about \(7\times10^{-10}\text{ m}\), comparable to the size of an atom. This is why wave behavior dominates the world of electrons, atoms, and molecules, and why chemistry needs quantum mechanics.

Rewriting the previous equations in terms of the wave vector, \(k=\frac{2\pi}{\lambda}\), and the angular frequency, \(\omega=2\pi\nu\), that we introduced in chapter \ref{Classical}, we obtain the following two equations:

\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
p &= \hbar k \\
E &= \hbar \omega,
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:wp1b}
\end{equation}

which are known as \textbf{de Broglie's equations}. We will use these equations to develop wave mechanics in the next chapter.

\section{Summary}\label{summary}

The four experiments discussed in this chapter, and the new ideas that they introduced, are summarized in table \ref{tab:tab1c2}.

\begin{table}

\caption{\label{tab:tab1c2}The experiments that led to quantum mechanics.}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{l|l|l|l}
\hline
Experiment & Classical prediction & Observation & Quantum idea\\
\hline
Black-body radiation & Intensity grows without limit at short wavelengths & Intensity peaks and goes to zero at short wavelengths & Energy is exchanged in quanta, $E=nh\nu$ (Planck, 1900)\\
\hline
Photoelectric effect & Electron energy grows with light intensity; any color works & Electron energy depends only on frequency; threshold frequency & Light is made of photons, $E=h\nu$ (Einstein, 1905)\\
\hline
Atomic line spectra & Continuous emission; electrons spiral into the nucleus & Sharp lines at specific wavelengths; atoms are stable & Atoms have quantized energy levels (Bohr, 1913)\\
\hline
Electron diffraction & Electrons are particles and cannot interfere & Diffraction patterns, as for waves & Matter behaves as waves, $\lambda=h/p$ (de Broglie, 1924)\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The common thread of all these results is that, at the scale of atoms and molecules, energy is not continuous, and particles and waves are not separate categories. In the next chapter, we will see how Schrödinger combined these ideas into a single equation.

\section{Chapter Review}\label{rev2}

\subsection{Study Questions}\label{quest2}

\textbf{1. The ``ultraviolet catastrophe'' refers to which failure of classical physics?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}

  Prediction that all objects emit only in the ultraviolet region

\chapter{The Schrödinger Equation}\label{Schrodinger}

As we saw in the previous chapter, quantum mechanics was born in two different forms: Heisenberg's matrix mechanics and Schrödinger's wave mechanics. The two are mathematically equivalent, and we are free to start from either one. In this textbook, we start from Schrödinger's version, because it is written in the language of waves and differential equations, which is closer to the physics and mathematics that chemistry students already know. Schrödinger's main idea was simple: if matter behaves as a wave, as de Broglie had proposed, there should be a wave equation that describes it.

\section{The Time-Independent Schrödinger Equation}\label{the-time-independent-schruxf6dinger-equation}

The Schrödinger equation cannot be derived from classical physics, since it contains new physics that classical physics does not have. We can, however, make it \emph{plausible}, by combining the mathematical description of a classical wave with de Broglie's relations. We start from the simplest wave that travels through space, the complex plane wave moving along \(x\) that we introduced in section \ref{ClassicalWaves}:

\begin{equation}
\psi(x,t)=\exp[i(kx-\omega t)],
\label{eq:sch1}
\end{equation}

where \(x\) is the position, \(t\) is time, \(k=\frac{2\pi}{\lambda}\) is the wave vector, and \(\omega=2\pi\nu\) is the angular frequency of the wave. If we are not concerned with the time evolution, we can consider uniquely the derivatives of eq. \eqref{eq:sch1} with respect to the location, which are:

\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{\partial \psi}{\partial x} &=ik\exp[i(kx-\omega t)] = ik\psi, \\
\frac{\partial^2 \psi}{\partial x^2} &=i^2k^2\exp[i(kx-\omega t)] = -k^2\psi,
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:sch2}
\end{equation}

where we have used the fact that \(i^2=-1\).

Assuming that particles behave as waves---as proposed by de Broglie---we can now use the first of de Broglie's equations, eq. \eqref{eq:wp1b}, to replace \(k=\frac{p}{\hbar}\) and obtain:

\begin{equation}
\frac{\partial^2 \psi}{\partial x^2} = -\frac{p^2\psi}{\hbar^2},
\label{eq:sch3}
\end{equation}

which can be rearranged to:

\begin{equation}
p^2 \psi = -\hbar^2 \frac{\partial^2 \psi}{\partial x^2}.
\label{eq:sch4}
\end{equation}

The total energy associated with a wave moving in space is simply the sum of its kinetic and potential energies:

\begin{equation}
E = \frac {p^{2}}{2m} + V(x),
\label{eq:sch5}
\end{equation}

from which we can obtain:

\begin{equation}
p^2 = 2m[E - V(x)],
\label{eq:sch6}
\end{equation}

which we can then replace into eq. \eqref{eq:sch4} to obtain:

\begin{equation}
2m[E-V(x)]\psi = - \hbar^2 \frac{\partial^2 \psi}{\partial x^2},
\label{eq:sch7}
\end{equation}

which can then be rearranged to the famous \textbf{time-independent Schrödinger equation (TISEq)}:

\begin{equation}
- \frac{\hbar^2}{2m} \frac{\partial^2 \psi}{\partial x^2} + V(x) \psi = E\psi,
\label{eq:TISEq}
\end{equation}

Remember that we did not \emph{prove} this equation: we only showed that it is consistent with a plane wave and with de Broglie's relations. Its real justification is that its predictions agree with experiments, as we will see in the following chapters. Notice also that the equation describes a single particle of mass \(m\). If we want to describe the relative motion of two particles, such as the two atoms in a diatomic molecule or the electron and the proton in a hydrogen atom, we can use the same equation, as long as we replace \(m\) with the \emph{reduced mass} of the two particles, \(\mu = \frac{m_1 m_2}{m_1+m_2}\).

\section{The Meaning of the Wave Function}\label{BornInterpretation}

We now have an equation for the wave function, but we have not yet said what the wave function \emph{is}. Unlike the displacement of a guitar string or the electric field of a light wave, the wave function of a particle is not something that we can measure directly. It is, in general, a complex function, so it cannot itself be a measurable quantity. Its physical meaning was proposed by Max Born in 1926, and it is known as \textbf{Born's interpretation}: the square magnitude of the wave function gives the probability of finding the particle at a given position. More precisely, for a particle moving along \(x\):

\begin{equation}
\text{probability of finding the particle between } x \text{ and } x+dx = |\psi(x)|^2\,dx,
\label{eq:born1}
\end{equation}

where \(|\psi(x)|^2=\psi^*(x)\psi(x)\) is called the \textbf{probability density}. Notice that, even if \(\psi\) is complex, \(\psi^*\psi\) is always real and never negative, as a probability must be. This is similar to what happens for classical waves (chapter \ref{Classical}), where the energy carried by a wave depends on the square of its amplitude, and not on the amplitude itself. For this reason, the wave function is sometimes called a \emph{probability amplitude}.

Since the particle must be found somewhere, the probabilities of finding it at all possible positions must add up to one:

\begin{equation}
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty} |\psi(x)|^2\,dx = 1.
\label{eq:born2}
\end{equation}

This requirement is called the \textbf{normalization condition}, and a wave function that satisfies it is said to be \emph{normalized}. For a particle moving in three dimensions, the integral runs over all three coordinates, \(\int |\psi(\mathbf{r})|^2\,d\mathbf{r}=1\). If we multiply a solution of the Schrödinger equation by any constant, we still have a solution, so the normalization condition is what fixes the overall size of the wave function. We will see how this works in practice for the particle in a box in chapter \ref{Models}.

The probabilistic interpretation is one of the most radical departures of quantum mechanics from classical physics. A classical particle has a definite position at every moment; a quantum particle, instead, is described by a wave function that tells us only how \emph{likely} it is to be found at each position. We will come back to the consequences of this idea, which still puzzle physicists and philosophers, in chapter \ref{Weirdness}.

\section{The Time-Dependent Schrödinger Equation}\label{the-time-dependent-schruxf6dinger-equation}

The argument that we used in the previous section does not work for the time dependence of the wave function. Historically, Schrödinger also published the time-independent equation first, and only later proposed the more general time-dependent one. We will follow the same order, and simply state the time-dependent equation without derivation, as a basic assumption of the theory, whose justification is that its predictions agree with experiments. For a single particle moving in one dimension, the time-dependent Schrödinger equation (TDSEq) is:

\begin{equation}
i\hbar\frac{\partial \psi(x,t)}{\partial t}=-\frac{\hbar^2}{2m} \frac{\partial^2 \psi(x,t)}{\partial x^2}+V(x)\psi(x,t)
\label{eq:TDSEq}
\end{equation}

where \(V(x)\) is the potential energy of the system, which we assume does not depend on time. The TISEq is a special case of this equation. To see how, we try a wave function written as the product of a function of position and a function of time, \(\psi(x, t) = \psi(x) f(t)\). Replacing it into eq. \eqref{eq:TDSEq}, we obtain:

\begin{equation}
\psi(x) i \hbar \frac{df(t)}{dt} = f(t) \left[-\frac{\hbar^2}{2m} \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x^2} + V(x) \right] \psi(x),
\label{eq:TDSEq2}
\end{equation}

which can be rearranged to:

\begin{equation}
\frac{i \hbar}{f(t)} \frac{df(t)}{dt} = \frac{1}{\psi(x)} \left[-\frac{\hbar^2}{2m} \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x^2} + V(x) \right] \psi(x).
\label{eq:TDSEq3}
\end{equation}

The left-hand side of eq. \eqref{eq:TDSEq3} depends only on \(t\), and the right-hand side only on \(x\). The only way for the two to be equal for every value of \(x\) and \(t\) is for both of them to be equal to the same constant. The right-hand side has the dimensions of energy, so we call this constant \(E\). We have now split one partial differential equation into two ordinary differential equations:

\begin{equation}
\frac{1}{f(t)} \frac{df(t)}{dt} = - \frac{i E}{\hbar}
\label{eq:TDSEq4}
\end{equation}

and:
\begin{equation}
-\frac{\hbar^2}{2m} \frac{\partial^2\psi(x)}{\partial x^2} + V(x) \psi(x) =
E \psi(x).
\label{eq:TDSEq5}
\end{equation}

The second equation is the TISEq that we obtained in the previous section. The first one is easy to solve, giving:

\begin{equation}
f(t) = e^{-iEt / \hbar}
\label{eq:TDSEq6}
\end{equation}

This function oscillates in time, but its magnitude is always equal to one. Therefore, if the wave function is:

\begin{equation}
\psi(x, t) = \psi(x) \exp\left(\frac{-iEt}{\hbar}\right),
\label{eq:TDSEq6b}
\end{equation}

then \(\psi(x, t)\) differs from \(\psi(x)\) only by a phase factor that rotates in the complex plane without changing its size. As a consequence, the quantity \(\vert \psi(x, t) \vert^2\) does not depend on time:

\begin{equation}
\vert \psi(x, t) \vert^2 = \psi^{*}(x, t) \psi(x, t)=
\psi^{*}(x)\exp\left(\frac{iEt}{\hbar}\right)\psi(x)\exp\left(\frac{-iEt}{\hbar}\right)= \psi^{*}(x) \psi(x).
\label{eq:TDSEq7}
\end{equation}

Wave functions of the form of eq. \eqref{eq:TDSEq6b} are called \textbf{stationary states}. As we saw in section \ref{BornInterpretation}, \(\vert \psi(x, t) \vert^2\) is a probability density, the quantity that we can measure, so in a stationary state nothing that we can observe changes in time. This does not mean that the particle is standing still, but only that its probability distribution does not change. This is why the solutions of the TISEq are so important: they are the states with a well-defined energy, and they do not change in time. Not every solution of the TDSEq is a stationary state, but every solution can be written as a linear combination (a \emph{superposition}) of stationary states:\footnote{A similar presentation can be found in Prof.~C. David Sherrill's \href{http://vergil.chemistry.gatech.edu/notes/quantrev/node1.html}{A Brief Review of Elementary Quantum Chemistry} notes.}

\begin{equation}
\psi({\bf r}, t) = \sum_i c_i e^{-iE_it / \hbar} \psi_i({\bf r}).
\end{equation}

\section{Solution for the Free Particle}\label{solution-for-the-free-particle}

We will now show how to solve the time independent portion for the simple system of a particle in motion and not subject to any force. By definition, this free particle does not feel any external force, therefore \(V(x)=0\), and the TISEq is written simply:

\begin{equation}
- \frac{\hbar^2}{2m} \frac{d^2\psi}{dx^2} = E \psi(x).
\label{eq:FP1}
\end{equation}

This equation can be rearranged to:

\begin{equation}
\frac{d^2\psi}{dx^2} =- \frac{2mE}{\hbar^2} \psi(x),
\label{eq:FP2}
\end{equation}

which corresponds to a mathematical problem where the second derivative of a function should be equal to a constant, \(- \frac{2mE}{\hbar^2}\), multiplied by the function itself. The most general solution to this problem is the function:

\begin{equation}
\psi(x) = A \exp(ikx)+B \exp(-ikx),
\label{eq:FP3}
\end{equation}

with \(A\) and \(B\) arbitrary (complex) constants. The first and second derivatives of this function are:

\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{d \psi(x)}{dx} &= ik A \exp(ikx) - ikB\exp(-ikx) \\
\frac{d^2 \psi(x)}{dx^2} &= -k^2 A\exp(ikx) -k^2 B\exp(-ikx) = -k^2 \psi(x).
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:FP4}
\end{equation}

Comparing the second derivative in eq. \eqref{eq:FP4} with eq. \eqref{eq:FP2}, we immediately see that if we set:

\begin{equation}
k^2 = \frac{2mE}{\hbar^2},
\label{eq:FP5}
\end{equation}

we solve the original differential equation. Considering de Broglie's equation, eq. \eqref{eq:wp1b}, we can replace \(k=\frac{p}{\hbar}\), to obtain:

\begin{equation}
E = \frac{k^2 \hbar^2}{2m} = \frac{p^2}{2m},
\label{eq:FP6}
\end{equation}

which is exactly the classical value of the kinetic energy of a free particle moving in one direction of space. Since the function in eq. \eqref{eq:FP3} solves the Schrödinger equation for the free particle, it is called an \emph{eigenfunction} (or \emph{eigenstate}) of the TISEq. The energy result of eq. \eqref{eq:FP6} is called an \emph{eigenvalue} of the TISEq. Notice that, since \(k\) is continuous in the eigenfunction, the energy eigenvalue is also continuous (i.e., all values of \(E\) are acceptable).

\subsection{What does the free-particle solution look like?}\label{what-does-the-free-particle-solution-look-like}

Let's take a closer look at one of the solutions in eq. \eqref{eq:FP3}, for example the one with \(B=0\), which describes a particle moving to the right with momentum \(p=\hbar k\):

\begin{equation}
\psi(x) = A\, e^{ikx} = A\left[\cos(kx) + i\sin(kx)\right].
\label{eq:FP7}
\end{equation}

Its real and imaginary parts are a cosine and a sine wave with wavelength \(\lambda=2\pi/k=h/p\), exactly as de Broglie proposed. As we saw in section \ref{BornInterpretation}, however, the quantity that has a direct physical meaning is not the wave function itself, but its square magnitude, \(|\psi(x)|^2\), which gives the probability of finding the particle at position \(x\). For a plane wave, \(|\psi(x)|^2 = |A|^2\left[\cos^2(kx)+\sin^2(kx)\right]=|A|^2\), a constant. The plane wave and its probability density are shown in figure \ref{fig:fig1c3}.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.85\linewidth,alt={Top: a cosine wave and a sine wave of the same wavelength, shifted by a quarter wavelength, representing the real and imaginary parts of a plane wave. Bottom: the probability density is a flat horizontal band, the same at every position.}]{./img/FP_planewave} 

}

\caption{Top: real (solid) and imaginary (dashed) parts of a free-particle plane wave. Bottom: its probability density, which is the same everywhere.}\label{fig:fig1c3}
\end{figure}

This result is surprising. A particle described by a plane wave has a perfectly well-defined momentum, \(p=\hbar k\), but its probability density is the same everywhere: the particle is equally likely to be found at any point in the universe. The plane wave also extends forever in both directions, so the total probability, \(\int|\psi|^2dx\), is infinite, and the wave function cannot be normalized. A real particle, such as an electron shot from an electron gun, is certainly found somewhere in a limited region of space. Plane waves, therefore, are useful mathematical solutions, but they do not describe real particles by themselves.

\subsection{Wave packets}\label{WavePackets}

The way out of this problem comes from the principle of superposition that we discussed for classical waves in chapter \ref{Classical}. Since the Schrödinger equation is linear, any sum of plane waves with different wave vectors is also a solution. When we add waves with slightly different wavelengths, they interfere constructively in some regions and destructively in others. This is illustrated in figure \ref{fig:fig2c3}.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.85\linewidth,alt={Three panels. Top: the sum of two plane waves shows regularly repeating beats. Middle: the sum of five waves shows sharper bunches that still repeat. Bottom: the sum of 41 waves shows a single localized bunch of oscillations, with the wave canceling to zero everywhere else.}]{./img/FP_buildpacket} 

}

\caption{Superposition of plane waves with wave vectors close to $k_0$. Two waves produce a periodic pattern of beats; five waves produce sharper but still repeating bunches; many waves with a smooth distribution of wave vectors produce a single, localized wave packet. The thin line is the real part of the sum, and the thick line is its envelope.}\label{fig:fig2c3}
\end{figure}

Two waves produce a regular pattern of \emph{beats}, the same effect that musicians use to tune two instruments to the same note. As we add more waves, with wave vectors spread over a range around a central value \(k_0\), the regions of constructive interference get narrower and farther apart. With a continuous distribution of wave vectors, only one region of constructive interference survives, and the waves cancel each other everywhere else. The result is a \textbf{wave packet}: a wave that oscillates with wavelength close to \(2\pi/k_0\) inside a limited region of space, and is essentially zero outside of it. Mathematically, a wave packet is written as a continuous sum (an integral) of plane waves, each with its own amplitude, \(\phi(k)\):

\begin{equation}
\Psi(x,t) = \int \phi(k)\, e^{i\left(kx - \omega(k) t\right)} dk, \qquad \omega(k) = \frac{E}{\hbar} = \frac{\hbar k^2}{2m},
\label{eq:FP8}
\end{equation}

where each plane wave evolves in time with its own frequency, as we saw in eq. \eqref{eq:TDSEq6b}. Unlike a single plane wave, a wave packet can be normalized, and it describes a particle that is localized in a region of space. A wave packet is the quantum mechanical picture of a free particle such as an electron in a beam.

A few important considerations can be made by analyzing wave packets:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{The price of localization}: to build a packet that is localized in space, we need plane waves with a \emph{range} of wave vectors, and therefore a range of momenta, \(p=\hbar k\). The more localized we want the particle to be, the wider the range of momenta that we need, as shown in figure \ref{fig:fig3c3}. A plane wave is the extreme case of a perfectly defined momentum and a completely undefined position. A very narrow packet has a well-defined position, but a very uncertain momentum. For the packets in figure \ref{fig:fig3c3}, the widths in position, \(\Delta x\), and in wave vector, \(\Delta k\), are related by \(\Delta x\,\Delta k = \frac{1}{2}\), or, using \(p=\hbar k\), \(\Delta x\,\Delta p = \frac{\hbar}{2}\). This is a first glimpse of the Heisenberg uncertainty principle, which we will discuss in chapter \ref{Weirdness}.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.9\linewidth,alt={Two rows. Top: a narrow wave packet with few oscillations, paired with a broad distribution of wave vectors around k zero.
