  \textbf{Bohmian mechanics} (also called the de Broglie--Bohm or \emph{pilot-wave} theory), proposed by Louis de Broglie in 1927 and developed by David Bohm in 1952, is the best known nonlocal hidden variable theory. In this view, particles always have definite positions and follow well-defined trajectories, and the wave function is a real field that guides their motion, according to an equation derived from the Schrödinger equation. There is no collapse: a measurement simply reveals where the particles already are, and the randomness of the outcomes comes from our ignorance of their exact initial positions. Bohmian mechanics is the interpretation closest to the way chemists usually think: electrons are particles that are actually somewhere, molecules have real shapes, and nuclei and electrons move along trajectories. Its price is nonlocality: the motion of each particle depends instantaneously on the positions of all the others.
\item
  \textbf{Many worlds}, proposed by Hugh Everett in 1957, takes the opposite route: it keeps the Schrödinger equation and nothing else. There is no collapse and no special role for measurements. When a measuring device interacts with a superposition, the device, the observer, and eventually the whole universe become part of the superposition, which splits into \emph{branches}, one for each possible outcome. Each branch contains a version of the observer who sees one definite result, and the branches cannot interact with each other because of a process called \emph{decoherence}. Many worlds is the most mathematically and philosophically clean of all the interpretations, since it requires no additional rules beyond the equation that we already use. Its price is a reality much larger than the one we see, and the difficulty of explaining why the probabilities of the outcomes are given by \(|c_i|^2\) when all of them actually happen.
\item
  \textbf{Spontaneous collapse theories}, such as the one proposed by Giancarlo Ghirardi, Alberto Rimini, and Tullio Weber in 1986, treat the collapse as a real physical process and modify the Schrödinger equation to include it. In these theories, the wave function of any particle collapses spontaneously and at random, but extremely rarely (about once every hundred million years for a single particle). A macroscopic object, however, contains so many particles that one of them collapses almost immediately, dragging the whole object with it, which explains why we never see a cat or a measuring device in a superposition. Unlike the other interpretations, these theories make predictions that differ slightly from standard quantum mechanics, and they are currently being tested in experiments.
\end{itemize}

The main features of these interpretations are summarized in table \ref{tab:tab1c8}.

\begin{table}

\caption{\label{tab:tab1c8}Comparison of some interpretations of quantum mechanics.}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{l|l|l|l|l}
\hline
Interpretation & What is the wave function? & Collapse? & Definite particle positions? & Deterministic?\\
\hline
Copenhagen & Tool to compute probabilities & Yes, upon measurement (not explained) & No, until measured & No\\
\hline
Bohmian mechanics & Real field guiding the particles & No (only apparent) & Yes, always & Yes\\
\hline
Many worlds & Real, and the only thing that exists & No (branching instead) & Yes, in each branch & Yes\\
\hline
Spontaneous collapse & Real & Yes, as a random physical process & Only after collapse & No\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

For the purposes of chemistry, all these interpretations give the same answers: energy levels, spectra, bond lengths, and reaction rates do not depend on which one we choose (the tiny deviations predicted by spontaneous collapse theories are far too small to affect any chemical property). This is why, in the rest of this textbook, we will keep using the convenient language of the Copenhagen interpretation. You should be aware, however, that it is a language, and not the final word on what quantum mechanics tells us about reality.

\section{The double-slit experiment}\label{the-double-slit-experiment}

The double-slit experiment is considered by many the seminal experiment in quantum mechanics. The reason why we see it only at this advanced point is that its interpretation is not as straightforward as it might seem from a superficial analysis. The famous physicist Richard Feynman was so fond of this experiment that he used to say that all of quantum mechanics can be understood from carefully thinking through its implications.

The premises of the experiment are very simple: cut two slits in a solid material (such as a sheet of metal), send light or electrons through them, and observe what happens on a screen positioned at some distance on the other side. The results of this experiment, though, are far from straightforward.

Let's first consider the single-slit case. If light consisted of classical particles, and these particles were sent in a straight line through a single-slit and allowed to strike a screen on the other side, we would expect to see a pattern corresponding to the size and shape of the slit. However, when this ``single-slit experiment'' is actually performed, the pattern on the screen is a diffraction pattern in which the light is spread out. The smaller the slit, the greater the angle of spread. This behavior is typical of waves, where diffraction explains the pattern as being the result of the interference of the waves with the slit.

If one illuminates two parallel slits, the light from the two slits again interferes. Here the interference is a more pronounced pattern with a series of alternating light and dark bands. The width of the bands is a property of the frequency of the illuminating light. The pattern observed on the screen is the result of this interference, as shown in figure \ref{fig:Fig1c8}.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth,alt={Two rows. Top: a wave passes through one slit and produces on the screen a single broad bright band with faint side bands. Bottom: a wave passes through two slits and produces a series of narrow bright and dark fringes under the same broad envelope.}]{./img/Slits_patterns} 

}

\caption{Diffraction through one slit (top) and interference through two slits (bottom). Left: schematic of the experiment. Right: intensity on the screen and the corresponding pattern of light and dark regions.}\label{fig:Fig1c8}
\end{figure}

The interference pattern resulting from the double-slit experiment is observed not only with light, but also with a beam of electrons, and other small particles. This is the same wave behavior of electrons that Davisson and Germer first observed in 1927, when they diffracted electrons from a nickel crystal (chapter \ref{Motivation}).

\subsection{The individual particles experiment}\label{the-individual-particles-experiment}

The first twist in the plot is if we perform the experiment by sending individual particles (e.g., either individual photons, or individual electrons). Sending particles through a double-slit apparatus one at a time results in single particles appearing on the screen, as expected. Remarkably, however, an interference pattern emerges when these particles are allowed to build up one by one (figure \ref{fig:Fig2c8}). The resulting pattern on the screen is the same as if each individual particle had passed through both slits.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=1\linewidth,alt={Four dark panels with white dots. With 20 electrons the dots look randomly scattered; with 200 they begin to cluster; with 2,000 and 20,000 electrons the dots clearly form vertical bright and dark interference fringes.}]{./img/Slits_buildup} 

}

\caption{Simulated arrival of electrons, one at a time, on the screen of a double-slit experiment. Each electron arrives at a single random point, but as more electrons accumulate, the interference fringes emerge.}\label{fig:Fig2c8}
\end{figure}

This variation of the double-slit experiment demonstrates the wave--particle duality: the particle is measured as a single pulse at a single position, while the wave describes the probability of absorbing the particle at a specific place on the screen.

\subsection{``Which way'' experiment}\label{which-way-experiment}

A second twist happens if we place particle detectors at the slits with the intent of showing through which slit a particle goes. The interference pattern in this case will disappear. The two situations are compared in figure \ref{fig:Fig4c8}. Notice that a single detector, placed on one of the two slits, is enough: if it does not click, we know that the particle went through the other slit. With the detector on, the pattern on the screen is simply the sum of the patterns produced by each slit separately, exactly what we would expect from classical particles.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth,alt={Two columns. Left: two slits without a detector produce narrow bright and dark interference fringes on the screen. Right: the same slits with a detector labeled D on the upper slit produce a single broad bright band with no fringes, equal to the sum of two single-slit patterns.}]{./img/Slits_whichway} 

}

\caption{The which-way experiment. Left: without a detector, the particles produce interference fringes. Right: with a detector (D) on one slit, which reveals which slit each particle goes through, the fringes disappear, and the pattern is the sum of the two single-slit patterns (dashed lines).}\label{fig:Fig4c8}
\end{figure}

This experiment illustrates that photons (and electrons) can behave as either particles or waves, but cannot be observed as both at the same time. The simplest interpretation of this experiment is that the wave function of the photon collapses into a deterministic position due to the interaction with the detector on the slit, and the interference pattern is therefore lost. This result also proves that in order to measure (detect) a photon, we must interact with it, an act that changes its wave function.

The interpretation of the results of this experiment is not simple. As for other situations in quantum mechanics, the problem arises not because we cannot describe the experiment in mathematical terms, but because the math that we need to describe it cannot be related to the macroscopic classical world we live in. According to the math, in fact, particles in the experiment are described exclusively in probabilistic terms (given by the square of the wave function). The macroscopic world, however, is not probabilistic, and outcomes of experiments can be univocally measured. Several different ways of resolving this controversy have been proposed, including for example the possibility that quantum mechanics is incomplete (the emergence of probability is due to the ignorance of some more fundamental deterministic feature of nature), or assuming that every time a measurement is done on a quantum system, the universe splits, and every possible measurable outcome is observed in different branches of our universe (we only happen to live in one of such branches, so we observe only one non-probabilistic result).\footnote{The interested student can read more in the \href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interpretations_of_quantum_mechanics}{Wikipedia article on interpretations of quantum mechanics}, and in section \ref{Interpretations}.} The interpretation of quantum mechanics is still an unsolved problem in modern physics (luckily, it does not prevent us from using quantum mechanics in chemistry).

\section{Heisenberg's Uncertainty Principle}\label{heisenbergs-uncertainty-principle}

As we saw in section \ref{WavePackets}, a free particle described by a single plane wave, \(\psi(x)=Ae^{ipx/\hbar}\), has a perfectly defined momentum, but it is spread over all of space, so we cannot say where it is. To describe a particle that is localized, we need a wave packet: a superposition of many plane waves with different momenta, which interfere constructively in a small region of space and cancel everywhere else (figure \ref{fig:fig3c3}). Written as a continuous sum over momenta, a wave packet is:

\begin{equation}
\psi (x)=\frac {1}{\sqrt{2\pi\hbar}}\int_{-\infty }^{\infty }\varphi (p) \exp
\left(\frac{ip x}{\hbar} \right)\,dp,
\label{eq:heis2}  
\end{equation}

where \(\varphi(p)\) gives the amplitude of each plane wave in the packet. The function \(\varphi(p)\) is called the \emph{wave function in momentum space}, and \(|\varphi(p)|^2\) is the probability density of finding the particle with momentum \(p\), just as \(|\psi(x)|^2\) is the probability density of finding it at position \(x\). In mathematical terms, \(\varphi(p)\) is the \emph{Fourier transform} of \(\psi(x)\), and \(x\) and \(p\) are called \emph{conjugate variables}. The two functions contain exactly the same information, written in two different ways, and they cannot both be narrow: localizing the particle in space requires a broad mixture of momenta, and vice versa. This connection is shown in figure \ref{fig:Fig5c8} for three wave packets of different width.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.9\linewidth,alt={Three rows of paired bell curves. Top: a wide curve in position paired with a narrow curve in momentum. Middle: medium widths in both. Bottom: a narrow curve in position paired with a wide curve in momentum. Arrows mark twice the standard deviation of each curve.}]{./img/Uncertainty_xp} 

}

\caption{Probability densities in position space (left) and in momentum space (right) for three wave packets (in units where $\hbar=1$). As the packet becomes narrower in position (from top to bottom), it becomes broader in momentum, and the product of the two standard deviations stays equal to $\hbar/2$.}\label{fig:Fig5c8}
\end{figure}

One way to quantify the precision of the position and momentum is the standard deviation, \(\sigma\). Since \(|\psi (x)|^{2}\) is a probability density function for position, we calculate its standard deviation. The precision of the position is improved---i.e., reduced \(\sigma_x\)---by using many plane waves, thereby weakening the precision of the momentum---i.e., increased \(\sigma_p\). Another way of stating this is that \(\sigma_x\) and \(\sigma_p\) have an inverse relationship (once we know one with absolute precision, the other becomes completely unknown). This fact was discovered by Werner Heisenberg and is now called \textbf{Heisenberg's uncertainty principle}. The mathematical treatment of this procedure results in the simple formula:

\begin{equation}
\sigma_{x}\sigma_{p} \geq \frac{\hbar }{2}.
\label{eq:heis3}  
\end{equation}

The uncertainty principle can be extended to any pair of conjugate variables, including, for example, energy and time, angular momentum components along perpendicular directions, spin components along perpendicular directions, etc. It is also easy to show that conjugate variables in quantum mechanics correspond to non-commuting operators.\footnote{Therefore, a simpler way of finding if two variables are subject to the uncertainty principle is to check if their corresponding operators commute.}

\section{Tunneling}\label{tunneling}

Tunneling is a phenomenon where a particle may cross a barrier even if it does not have sufficient kinetic energy to overcome the potential of the barrier itself. In this situation, the particle is said to ``tunnel through'' the barrier following a purely quantum mechanical phenomenon (figure \ref{fig:Fig3c8}).

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.9\linewidth,alt={A wave of large amplitude on the left reaches a gray rectangular barrier, decays smoothly inside it, and continues on the right as a wave with the same wavelength but much smaller amplitude.}]{./img/Tunneling} 

}

\caption{Real part of the wave function of a particle with energy $E$ hitting a barrier of height $V_0>E$ between $0$ and $l$. The wave decays inside the barrier, and a smaller wave with the same wavelength continues on the other side: in this example, 13\% of the particles are transmitted.}\label{fig:Fig3c8}
\end{figure}

To explain tunneling we must resort once again to the TISEq. A traveling or standing wave function incident on a non-infinite potential barrier (\(V_0\)) decays in the potential as a function of \(C\exp[-\alpha x]\), where \(C\) is the amplitude at the boundary, \(\alpha\) depends on the difference between the height of the barrier and the energy of the particle, and \(x\) is the distance into the potential. If a second well exists at infinite distance from the first well, the probability goes to zero, so the probability of a particle existing in the second well is zero. If a second well is brought closer to the first well, the amplitude of the wave function at this boundary is not zero, so the particle may tunnel into that well from the first well. It would appear that the particle is ``leaking'' through the barrier; it can travel through it without having to surmount it. An important point to keep in mind is that tunneling conserves energy. The final sum of the kinetic and potential energy of the system cannot exceed the initial sum. Therefore, the potential on both sides of the barrier does not need to be the same, but the sum of the ground state energy and the potential on the opposite side of the barrier may not be larger than the initial particle energy and potential.

Tunneling can be described using the TISEq, eq. \eqref{eq:scheqsimple}. For the tunneling problem we can take the potential \(V\) to be zero for all space, except for the region inside the barrier (between \(0\) and \(l\)):

\begin{equation}
V=\begin{cases} 0\quad&\text{if}\; -\infty<x\leq 0 \\ V_0\quad&\text{if}\; 0<x<l \\ 0\quad&\text{if}\; l\leq x< \infty \end{cases}.
\label{eq:tunn1}  
\end{equation}

To solve the TISEq with this potential, we must solve it separately for each region, but we should make sure that the wave function stays single-valued, continuous and everywhere continuously differentiable. The general solution for each region, before applying the boundary conditions, is:

\begin{equation}
\psi=\begin{cases} A\sin (kx)+B\cos (kx)\quad&\text{if}\; -\infty<x\leq 0 \\ C \exp(-\alpha x) \quad&\text{if}\; 0<x<l \\ D\sin (kx)+E\cos (kx) \quad&\text{if}\; l\leq x< \infty \end{cases}
\label{eq:tunn2}  
\end{equation}

where \(k=\frac{\sqrt{2mE}}{\hbar}\), and \(\alpha=\frac{\sqrt{2m(V_0-E)}}{\hbar}\). To enforce continuity, we must have at the first boundary:

\begin{equation}
A\sin(0) +B \cos(0)=C\exp(0),
\label{eq:tunn3}  
\end{equation}

which implies that \(A=0\), and \(B=C\). In the final region, the coefficients \(D\) and \(E\) present a potentially intractable problem. However, if one realizes that the value at the boundary \(l\) is driving the wave in the region \(l\) to infinity, it may also be realized that the wave function could be rewritten as \(B\exp[-\alpha l]\cos[k(x-l)]\), phase shifting the wave function by the value of \(l\), and setting the amplitude to the boundary value. Summarizing, the wave function is:

\begin{equation}
\psi=\begin{cases} B\cos (kx)\quad&\text{if}\; -\infty<x\leq 0 \\ B \exp(-\alpha x)  \quad&\text{if}\; 0<x<l \\ B\exp(-\alpha l)\cos[k(x-l)] \quad&\text{if}\; l\leq x< \infty. \end{cases}
\label{eq:tunn5}  
\end{equation}

Comparing the wave function on the left of the barrier with the one on its right, we notice how the amplitude is attenuated by the barrier as \(\exp\left(-l\frac{\sqrt{2m(V_0-E)}}{\hbar}\right)\), where \(l\) is the width of the barrier, and \((V_0-E)\) is the difference between the potential energy of the barrier and the current energy of the particle. Since the square of the wave function is the probability distribution, the probability of transmission through a barrier is:

\begin{equation}
\exp\left(-2l\frac{\sqrt{2m(V_0-E)}}{\hbar}\right).
\label{eq:tunn6}  
\end{equation}

As the barrier width or height approaches zero, the probability of a particle tunneling through the barrier becomes one. We can also note that \(k\) is unchanged on the other side of the barrier. This implies that the energy of the particle is exactly the same as it was before it tunneled through the barrier, as stated earlier, the only thing that changes is the quantity of particles going in that direction. The rest is reflected off the barrier, and goes back the way it came. On the opposite end, as the barrier width or height approaches infinity, the probability of a particle tunneling through the barrier becomes zero, and the barrier behaves similarly to those that contained the particle in the particle in a box example discussed in chapter \ref{Models}.

\subsection{Tunneling in chemical reactions}\label{tunneling-in-chemical-reactions}

The ammonia umbrella inversion reaction provides an excellent example of the impact of quantum mechanical tunneling on the kinetic isotope effect (KIE). The reaction involves the inversion of the nitrogen atom in ammonia, which changes its position from above the plane of the three hydrogen atoms to below it and vice versa. This is a rapid oscillation of the atom and substituents, classically described by the molecule passing through a planar transition state. The ammonia interconversion is, however, faster than classically predicted. At room temperature, ammonia inverts 30 billion times per second. Two factors contribute to the rapidity of the inversion: a low energy barrier (24.2 kJ/mol) and a narrow width of the barrier itself, which allows for frequent quantum tunneling of the hydrogen atoms. In contrast, phosphine (\(\text{PH}_3\)) inverts very slowly at room temperature (energy barrier: 132 kJ/mol).

One of the primary effects of tunneling can be seen on the KIE. The probability of tunneling depends strongly on the mass of the particle, since the mass appears under the square root in the exponent of eq. \eqref{eq:tunn6}. Lighter isotopes therefore tunnel much more efficiently than heavier ones. As a result, tunneling speeds up the reaction of the lighter isotope much more than the reaction of the heavier one, leading to an increase in the KIE compared to the classical prediction. In the case of the umbrella inversion reaction, replacing ammonia with its deuterated counterpart, \(\text{ND}_3\), leaves the height of the barrier essentially unchanged (the potential energy does not depend on the mass of the nuclei), but it makes tunneling much less efficient: \(\text{ND}_3\) inverts more than ten times slower than \(\text{NH}_3\).

The KIE is defined as the ratio between the rate constants of the reactions involving the light and the heavy isotopes:

\begin{equation}
\text{KIE} = \frac{k(\text{H})}{k(\text{D})},
\label{eq:kie}  
\end{equation}

where \(k(\text{H})\) and \(k(\text{D})\) are the rate constants for the reaction involving hydrogen and deuterium, respectively. In the absence of tunneling, the KIEs of reactions that involve the transfer of a hydrogen atom are typically smaller than about 7 at room temperature, since they arise mostly from the difference in the zero-point energies of the two isotopes (see the harmonic oscillator in chapter \ref{Models}). Significantly larger values, together with curved (non-linear) Arrhenius plots, are commonly taken as experimental evidence of tunneling, an effect that must be considered when studying chemical reactions involving light atoms.

\section{Chapter Review}\label{rev8}

\subsection{Study Questions}\label{quest8}

\textbf{1. In the double‑slit experiment with single particles (e.g., electrons), what is observed on the screen after many particles have passed?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  a single sharp peak at the center\\
\item
  two non‑overlapping bands corresponding to the two slits\\
\item
  a classical shadow pattern with uniform intensity between slits\\
\item
  an interference pattern with alternating bright and dark fringes\\
\item
  a completely uniform distribution over the screen
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{2. When detectors are placed at the slits to determine ``which slit'' each particle goes through, what typically happens to the interference pattern?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  it becomes sharper and more pronounced\\
\item
  it disappears, leaving two classical‑like bands\\
\item
  it reverses (bright and dark fringes swap)\\
\item
  it doubles in fringe number\\
\item
  it becomes independent of slit separation
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{3. In the Heisenberg uncertainty principle for position and momentum along \(x\), eq. \eqref{eq:heis3}, what do \(\sigma_x\) and \(\sigma_p\) represent?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  exact measurement errors of the apparatus\\
\item
  spreads (standard deviations) in the probability distributions of \(x\) and \(p_x\)\\
\item
  mean values of position and momentum\\
\item
  classical trajectory deviations\\
\item
  arbitrary experimental tolerances chosen by the user
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{4. Which pair of observables is \emph{not} a standard example of canonically conjugate variables in the context of uncertainty relations?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  position and momentum\\
\item
  energy and time (in a suitable sense)\\
\item
  different components of spin\\
\item
  particle number and phase (in some systems)\\
\item
  mass and electric charge
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{5. Quantum tunneling refers to the phenomenon where a particle does what?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  moves faster than light through a barrier\\
\item
  passes through a region where its classical kinetic energy would be negative\\
\item
  reflects perfectly from any finite barrier\\
\item
  ceases to exist inside a barrier\\
\item
  behaves as a classical wave inside a barrier but as a particle outside
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{6. In a 1D potential‑barrier problem, what happens to the wave function in a classically forbidden region where \(E < V(x)\)?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  it vanishes identically\\
\item
  it oscillates with constant amplitude\\
\item
  it decays (or grows) exponentially with distance into the barrier\\
\item
  it becomes a delta function at the barrier edge\\
\item
  it becomes purely imaginary and non‑normalizable
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{7. For a finite barrier of width \(l\), which statement about transmission probability in the tunneling example is qualitatively correct?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  it is exactly zero if \(E < V_0\)\\
\item
  it is 1/2 regardless of barrier parameters\\
\item
  it decreases rapidly as barrier width and/or height increase\\
\item
  it is independent of the particle's mass\\
\item
  it is always 1 for normalized wave functions
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{8. Which of the following best explains \emph{why} tunneling can occur even when \(E < V_0\) for a barrier?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  energy is not conserved in quantum mechanics\\
\item
  the wave function penetrates the classically forbidden region and has nonzero amplitude beyond it\\
\item
  the particle temporarily borrows energy from the vacuum\\
\item
  relativistic corrections allow the particle to accelerate\\
\item
  the barrier momentarily disappears due to fluctuations
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{9. In a chemical reaction coordinate picture, what does tunneling correspond to?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  the reactant climbing \emph{over} the transition-state barrier\\
\item
  the reactant remaining in a metastable well forever\\
\item
  the system passing \emph{through} the barrier region where classical energy would be insufficient\\
\item
  the system skipping the transition state entirely with zero probability\\
\item
  the system following a purely classical minimum‑energy path
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{10. Which experimental signature is commonly taken as evidence for significant hydrogen tunneling in reactions?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  linear Arrhenius plots with classical slopes\\
\item
  very small kinetic isotope effects (\(k_\text{H}/k_\text{D} \approx 1\) at all temperatures)\\
\item
  large kinetic isotope effects and curved (non‑linear) Arrhenius plots\\
\item
  complete absence of isotope effects\\
\item
  rate constants independent of temperature and isotope
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{11. Once a measurement of \(A\) yields a particular eigenvalue \(a_k\), what happens to the wave function (in the idealized description)?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  It remains unchanged\\
\item
  It collapses to the corresponding eigenstate of \(\hat{A}\)\\
\item
  It becomes identically zero\\
\item
  It becomes a uniform superposition of all eigenstates\\
\item
  It collapses to the ground state of the Hamiltonian
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{12. Which interpretation of quantum mechanics describes electrons as particles with definite positions and trajectories, guided by the wave function?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Copenhagen interpretation\\
\item
  Many worlds\\
\item
  Bohmian mechanics\\
\item
  Spontaneous collapse\\
\item
  Local hidden variables
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

Answers: 1.d, 2.b, 3.b, 4.e, 5.b, 6.c, 7.c, 8.b, 9.c, 10.c, 11.b, 12.c

\chapter{Many-Electron Atoms}\label{Atoms}

As soon as a system contains two or more electrons, the TISEq can no longer be solved analytically. Since this is the case for every atom other than hydrogen, and for every molecule other than \(\mathrm{H}_2^+\), almost all of chemistry depends on approximate methods. In this and the following chapters we will introduce the most important of these approximations, starting from many-electron atoms. Keep in mind that, precisely because they are approximations, these methods are still an active field of research, and better ones are developed every year.

The electronic Hamiltonian for an atom with \(N\) electrons can be written as:

\begin{equation}
\hat{H}({\bf r}_1,{\bf r}_2,\ldots,{\bf r}_N)=\sum_{i=1}^N \left(-{\frac {\hbar ^{2}}{2m_e }}\nabla_{i}^{2}-{\frac {Ze^{2}}{4\pi \varepsilon _{0}r_{i}}} \right)+{\frac {e^{2}}{4\pi \varepsilon _{0}}}\sum_{i<j}\frac{1}{r_{ij}},
\label{eq:atom1}
\end{equation}

where \(Z\) is the nuclear charge, \(m_e\) and \(e\) are respectively the mass and charge of an electron, \({\bf r}_i\) and \(\nabla_i^2\) are the spatial coordinates and the Laplacian of each electron, \(r_{i}=|{\bf r}_i|\), and \(r_{{ij}}=|{\bf r}_i-{\bf r}_j|\) is the distance between two electrons (all other symbols have been explained in previous chapters). The TISEq is easily written using eq. \eqref{eq:scheqsimple}.

It is worth looking at the three pieces of this Hamiltonian one at a time:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Kinetic energy}: one term for each electron, identical to the one we used for the hydrogen atom.
\item
  \textbf{Electron--nucleus attraction}: also one term for each electron, identical to hydrogen except for the nuclear charge \(Z\).
\item
  \textbf{Electron--electron repulsion}: one term for each \emph{pair} of electrons. This is the only new ingredient, and it is the one that causes all the trouble. Each of these terms depends on the coordinates of two electrons at the same time, so the Hamiltonian can no longer be split into a sum of one-electron pieces, and the potential is no longer spherically symmetric for any one electron.
\end{enumerate}

If the third term were not there, the TISEq for the atom would separate into \(N\) hydrogen-like equations that we already know how to solve. Most of the approximations in this chapter can be understood as different ways of dealing with this single term.

\section{Many-Electron Wave Functions}\label{many-electron-wave-functions}

\subsection{Identical particles: fermions and bosons}\label{identical-particles-fermions-and-bosons}

When a system contains more than one electron, we need to deal with a property of quantum particles that has no classical counterpart: identical particles are truly \emph{indistinguishable}. In classical mechanics, we could in principle paint one electron red and the other blue, and follow each of them along its trajectory. In quantum mechanics there are no trajectories, and two electrons are completely identical, so there is no experiment that can tell us which one is which.

To describe this mathematically, we need to include the spin of each electron. We define a variable \({\bf x}\) that collects all four coordinates of an electron: the three spatial coordinates \({\bf r}\), and one spin coordinate \(\mathbf{s}\), i.e., \({\bf x} = \{ {\bf r}, {\bf s} \}\), and we write the wave function of \(N\) electrons as \(\Psi({\bf x}_1, {\bf x}_2, \ldots, {\bf x}_N)\). Since the electrons are indistinguishable, swapping the coordinates of any two of them cannot change any measurable quantity, and in particular it cannot change the probability density:

\begin{equation}
\left|\Psi\left({\bf x}_1,{\bf x}_2,\ldots, {\bf x}_N\right)\right|^2=\left|\Psi\left({\bf x}_2,{\bf x}_1,\ldots, {\bf x}_N\right)\right|^2.
\label{eq:identical1}
\end{equation}

This equation can be satisfied in two ways: the wave function can stay the same when we swap two particles (it is \emph{symmetric}), or it can change sign (it is \emph{antisymmetric}). Nature uses both options, for two different families of particles:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Bosons} have symmetric wave functions. Photons and helium-4 atoms are examples of bosons.
\item
  \textbf{Fermions} have antisymmetric wave functions. Electrons, protons, and neutrons are fermions.
\end{itemize}

Which family a particle belongs to is determined by its spin: particles with integer spin (\(s=0,1,2,\ldots\)) are bosons, and particles with half-integer spin (\(s=\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2},\ldots\)) are fermions. This connection is known as the \emph{spin-statistics theorem}, and it can be proven only in the framework of relativistic quantum mechanics, so we will simply accept it here. Since electrons have \(s=\frac{1}{2}\) (chapter \ref{Spin}), they are fermions, and their wave function must be antisymmetric with respect to the exchange of any two of them:

\begin{equation}
\Psi\left({\bf x}_1,{\bf x}_2,\ldots, {\bf x}_N\right) = - \Psi\left({\bf x}_2,{\bf x}_1,\ldots, {\bf x}_N\right)
\label{eq:atom2}
\end{equation}

This simple requirement has an enormous consequence. Suppose that we try to describe two electrons with exactly the same state, including their spin, so that \({\bf x}_1={\bf x}_2\). Swapping two identical things cannot change anything, so the wave function must be unchanged by the swap. At the same time, eq. \eqref{eq:atom2} says that it must change sign. The only number that is equal to minus itself is zero, so the wave function must vanish: two electrons can never be in the same state. This is the \textbf{Pauli exclusion principle}, which we will see again, in a more familiar form, when we build many-electron wave functions below. Bosons, instead, have no such restriction, and any number of them can be described by the same state. This difference is responsible for the shell structure of atoms, and therefore for the whole of chemistry: if electrons were bosons, all of them would be described by the \(1s\) orbital, and atoms would not form bonds the way they do.

\subsection{Spin-orbitals and Slater determinants}\label{spin-orbitals-and-slater-determinants}

A very important step in simplifying \(\Psi({\bf x})\) is to build it from one-electron functions. Since we need to take the spin coordinate into account as well, we define a new function, called \textbf{spin-orbital}, by multiplying a spatial orbital by one of the two spin functions, \(\alpha\) (spin up, \(m_s=+\frac{1}{2}\)) or \(\beta\) (spin down, \(m_s=-\frac{1}{2}\)), that we introduced in chapter \ref{Spin}:

\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\chi({\bf x}) &= \psi({\bf r}) \alpha({\bf s}), \\
\chi({\bf x}) &= \psi({\bf r}) \beta({\bf s}).
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:atom3}
\end{equation}

For a given spatial orbital \(\psi({\bf r})\) we can therefore form two spin-orbitals, one with spin up and one with spin down (the spin coordinate \({\bf s}\) has only two possible values, as we discussed in chapter \ref{Spin}). For the spatial orbitals we can use the same one-electron functions that solve the TISEq for the hydrogen atom, \(\psi_{n\ell m_{\ell}}({\bf r})\) (eq. \eqref{eq:HA6} in chapter \ref{Hydrogen}). Each spin-orbital then depends on four quantum numbers: the three of the spatial part, \(n,\ell,m_{\ell}\), plus the spin quantum number \(m_s\). We need to keep in mind, however, that the spin-orbitals \(\chi_{n\ell m_{\ell} m_{s}}\) \emph{are not} solutions of the many-electron TISEq, so any wave function that we build from them is an approximation, not the exact wave function of the system.

Once we have spin-orbitals, we can use them as the building blocks of the many-electron wave function, making sure that the result is antisymmetric. We start from the simplest case: two electrons, with coordinates \(\mathbf{x}_1\) and \(\mathbf{x}_2\), and two spin-orbitals, \(\chi_1\) and \(\chi_2\). The most general wave function that we can build from products of these two spin-orbitals is:

\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Psi({\bf x}_1, {\bf x}_2) =& b_{11} \chi_1({\bf x}_1) \chi_1({\bf x}_2) + b_{12} \chi_1({\bf x}_1) \chi_2({\bf x}_2) + \\
& b_{21} \chi_2({\bf x}_1) \chi_1({\bf x}_2) + b_{22} \chi_2({\bf x}_1) \chi_2({\bf x}_2).
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:atom4}
\end{equation}

For this function to change sign when we swap \({\bf x}_1\) and \({\bf x}_2\), we need \(b_{12} = -b_{21}\) and \(b_{11} = b_{22} = 0\), which gives:

\begin{equation}
\Psi({\bf x}_1, {\bf x}_2) = b_{12} \left[ \chi_1({\bf x}_1) \chi_2({\bf x}_2) - \chi_2({\bf x}_1) \chi_1({\bf x}_2)\right].
\label{eq:atom5}
\end{equation}

This wave function is sufficient to describe two-electron atoms and ions, such as helium. The coefficient is fixed by the normalization condition, \(b_{12} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\). For the ground state of helium, we can use the \(1s\) hydrogen-like orbital, \(\psi_{100}\), as the spatial part of both spin-orbitals, obtaining:

\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Psi({\bf x}_1, {\bf x}_2) &= \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} \left[ \psi_{100}({\bf r}_1)\alpha(1) \; \psi_{100}({\bf r}_2)\beta(2) - \psi_{100}({\bf r}_1)\beta(1) \; \psi_{100}({\bf r}_2)\alpha(2) \right] \\
&= \psi_{100}({\bf r}_1)\psi_{100}({\bf r}_2) \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} \left[ \alpha(1)\beta(2) - \beta(1)\alpha(2) \right],
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:atom6}
\end{equation}

where \(\alpha(1)\) is short for \(\alpha({\bf s}_1)\), and so on. This equation shows that we need just one spatial orbital, \(\psi_{100}\), to describe the system, while the antisymmetry is taken care of by the combination of spin functions, \(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} \left[ \alpha(1)\beta(2) - \beta(1)\alpha(2) \right]\). Notice that we commit a small inaccuracy when we say that ``two electrons occupy one spatial orbital, one electron has spin up, and the other electron has spin down,'' as is typically done in general chemistry textbooks with the configuration \([\uparrow\downarrow]\). The real spin state is more subtle: neither electron individually is ``the up one''; instead, the ground state of helium is the combination \(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left[\uparrow\downarrow-\downarrow\uparrow\right]\).

To go from two electrons to \(N\), we can first observe that eq. \eqref{eq:atom5} is exactly the determinant of a \(2\times2\) matrix built from the spin-orbitals:

\begin{equation}
\Psi({\bf x}_1, {\bf x}_2)= \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}{\begin{vmatrix} \chi_1({\bf x}_1)&\chi_2({\bf x}_1)\\\chi_1({\bf x}_2)&\chi_2({\bf x}_2)
\end{vmatrix}},
\label{eq:atom7}
\end{equation}

where each column contains one spin-orbital, each row contains the coordinates of one electron, and the vertical bars mean that we take the determinant of the matrix. This construction is called a \textbf{Slater determinant}, and it is the standard way of building an \(N\)-electron wave function. Slater determinants can be written for any number of electrons, using the same number of spin-orbitals, and they automatically produce an antisymmetric wave function. A general Slater determinant is written:

\begin{equation}
\Psi (\mathbf{x} _{1},\mathbf{x} _{2},\ldots ,\mathbf{x} _{N})={\frac {1}{\sqrt {N!}}}{\begin{vmatrix}\chi _{1}(\mathbf{x} _{1})&\chi _{2}(\mathbf{x} _{1})&\cdots &\chi _{N}(\mathbf{x} _{1})\\\chi _{1}(\mathbf{x} _{2})&\chi _{2}(\mathbf{x} _{2})&\cdots &\chi _{N}(\mathbf{x} _{2})\\\vdots &\vdots &\ddots &\vdots \\\chi _{1}(\mathbf{x} _{N})&\chi _{2}(\mathbf{x} _{N})&\cdots &\chi _{N}(\mathbf{x} _{N})\end{vmatrix}} = |\chi _{1}\,\chi _{2}\cdots \chi _{N}|,
\label{eq:atom8}
\end{equation}

where the notation \(|\cdots|\) on the right is a compact shorthand for the whole determinant (including its normalization factor), in which we only list the spin-orbitals that appear on its diagonal. When a spin-orbital is used in the Slater determinant to describe an electron, we say that it is \emph{occupied}, and orbitals that are not used are called \emph{unoccupied} (or \emph{virtual}). This is convenient language, and we will use it throughout the textbook, but it should not be taken literally: orbitals are not boxes that contain electrons, but mathematical functions that we use to describe them.

Two properties of determinants that you may remember from linear algebra have important physical consequences here:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Swapping two rows changes the sign of a determinant.} Swapping two rows means swapping the coordinates of two electrons, so a Slater determinant is always antisymmetric, as required by eq. \eqref{eq:atom2}.
\item
  \textbf{A determinant with two identical columns is zero.} If we try to describe two electrons with the same spin-orbital (same \(n,\ell,m_\ell\), \emph{and} same \(m_s\)), two columns become identical and the wave function vanishes everywhere. In other words, such a state does not exist. This is the familiar form of the Pauli exclusion principle from general chemistry, ``no two electrons in an atom can have the same four quantum numbers,'' which here appears as a direct consequence of the antisymmetry of the wave function.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Approximated Hamiltonians}\label{approximated-hamiltonians}

To solve the TISEq for a many-electron atom we also need to deal with the Hamiltonian, since analytic solutions of the full Hamiltonian in eq. \eqref{eq:atom1} cannot be found. The most important tool that chemists use for this purpose is called the \emph{variational method}.

\subsection{Variational method}\label{variational-method}

The idea behind the variational method is simple. We guess a ``trial'' wave function that contains some adjustable numbers, called \emph{variational parameters}. We then calculate the energy of the trial function and change the parameters until this energy is as low as possible. The trial function with the lowest energy, and that energy itself, are our best approximations to the true ground-state wave function and energy.

Why should the lowest energy be the best answer? The justification is the \textbf{variational theorem}: the energy calculated from any trial wave function is always greater than or equal to the exact ground-state energy, \({\cal E}_0\). The proof uses a nice trick. Let's call our trial function \(\Phi\). Even if we cannot solve the TISEq, its exact eigenfunctions \(\Psi_i\) (with energies \({\cal E}_0 \leq {\cal E}_1 \leq {\cal E}_2 \leq \ldots\)) still exist and form a complete set. We can therefore imagine expanding our trial function in terms of them:

\begin{equation}
\Phi = \sum_i c_i \Psi_i.
\label{eq:var1}
\end{equation}

We never need to know the \(\Psi_i\) or the \(c_i\); the expansion is only a tool for the proof. The energy of the trial function is the expectation value of the Hamiltonian:

\begin{equation}
E[\Phi] = \frac{\int \Phi^* {\hat H} \Phi d\mathbf{\tau}}{\int \Phi^* \Phi d\mathbf{\tau}},
\label{eq:var2}
\end{equation}

where \(\mathbf{\tau}=\left(\mathbf{r}_1,\mathbf{r}_2,\ldots,\mathbf{r}_N\right)\) collects the spatial coordinates of all electrons, so the integrals are \(3N\)-dimensional. The denominator takes care of normalization, so \(\Phi\) does not need to be normalized. Inserting the expansion, we obtain:

\begin{equation}
E[\Phi] = \frac{\sum_{ij} c_i^* c_j \int \Psi_i^* {\hat H} \Psi_jd\mathbf{\tau}}{
\sum_{ij} c_i^* c_j \int \Psi_i^* \Psi_jd\mathbf{\tau}}.
\label{eq:var3}
\end{equation}

Since each \(\Psi_j\) is an eigenfunction of \({\hat H}\), we can replace \({\hat H} \Psi_j\) with \({\cal E}_j \Psi_j\):

\begin{equation}
E[\Phi] = \frac{\sum_{ij} c_i^* c_j {\cal E}_j \int \Psi_i^* \Psi_j d\mathbf{\tau}}{
\sum_{ij} c_i^* c_j \int \Psi_i^* \Psi_j d\mathbf{\tau}}.
\label{eq:var4}
\end{equation}

The eigenfunctions of a Hermitian operator are orthonormal (or can be chosen to be), so all integrals with \(i\neq j\) vanish and those with \(i=j\) are equal to one:

\begin{equation}
E[\Phi] = \frac{\sum_{i} |c_i|^2 {\cal E}_i}{\sum_{i} |c_i|^2}.
\label{eq:var5}
\end{equation}

This equation already tells the whole story: the energy of the trial function is a weighted average of the exact energies, with weights \(|c_i|^2\). An average can never be lower than its smallest member. To make this explicit, we subtract \({\cal E}_0\) from both sides:

\begin{equation}
E[\Phi] - {\cal E}_0 = \frac{\sum_i |c_i|^2 ( {\cal E}_i - {\cal E}_0)}{
\sum_i |c_i|^2}.
\label{eq:var6}
\end{equation}

Every term on the right-hand side is greater than or equal to zero, because \(|c_i|^2\geq0\) and \({\cal E}_i \geq {\cal E}_0\). Therefore:

\begin{equation}
E[\Phi] \geq {\cal E}_0.
\label{eq:varprinciple}
\end{equation}

The energy of any trial wave function is an upper bound to the exact ground-state energy, and the equality holds only if \(\Phi\) is the exact ground state. This gives us a clear strategy: any change in the trial function that lowers its energy brings the energy closer to the exact answer. (As the energy goes down, the wave function usually gets better too, although ``better'' for a wave function is harder to define than for a number, and it is guaranteed to be exact only when \(\Phi = \Psi_0\).)

\subsection{Approximated solution for the helium atom}\label{approximated-solution-for-the-helium-atom}

We now have all the ingredients for the simplest approximate solution of the TISEq for a many-electron atom. We start by writing the wave function as the Slater determinant of eq. \eqref{eq:atom8}, in which each spatial orbital is used to describe two electrons of opposite spin:

\begin{equation}
\Psi (\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2},\ldots ,\mathbf{x}_{N})= |\chi_{1}\,\chi_{2}\cdots \chi_{N}| = |\psi_{1}\alpha\;\psi_{1}\beta\cdots \psi_{\frac{N}{2}}\alpha\;\psi_{\frac{N}{2}}\beta|,
\label{eq:hf1}
\end{equation}

and we insert it into the TISEq for \(N\) electrons. This results in a set of \(N\) one-electron equations, one for each electron. When we try to solve each of them, however, we run into the problem that we identified at the beginning of this chapter: because of the electron-electron repulsion term, the potential felt by each electron does not have spherical symmetry, and the one-electron TISEq cannot be solved in spherical polar coordinates as we did for the hydrogen atom in chapter \ref{Hydrogen}. The crudest way around the problem is to neglect the electron-electron repulsion altogether (i.e., to pretend that the electrons do not interact with each other). For a two-electron atom the Hamiltonian then becomes a sum of two one-electron Hamiltonians:

\begin{equation}
\hat{H} =\hat{H}_1+\hat{H}_2,
\label{eq:hf2}
\end{equation}

each of them identical to the Hamiltonian of a hydrogen-like atom with nuclear charge \(Z\). This Hamiltonian does not depend on spin, so we can drop the spin part of eq. \eqref{eq:atom6} and write the wave function for the ground state of helium simply as:

\begin{equation}
\Psi({\bf r}_1, {\bf r}_2) = \psi_{100}({\bf r}_1)\psi_{100}({\bf r}_2).
\label{eq:hf3}
\end{equation}

The TISEq then separates into two independent one-electron equations:

\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\hat{H}_1 \psi_{100}({\bf r}_1) &= E_1\psi_{100}({\bf r}_1) \\
\hat{H}_2 \psi_{100}({\bf r}_2) &= E_2\psi_{100}({\bf r}_2),
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:hf4}
\end{equation}

which we already know how to solve (chapter \ref{Hydrogen}), and whose solutions combine to give:

\begin{equation}
E = E_1+E_2.
\label{eq:hf5}
\end{equation}

In this approximation, the energy of the helium atom is twice the energy of a \(1s\) electron in a hydrogen-like atom with \(Z=2\). The energies in eq. \eqref{eq:HA7} scale as \(Z^2\) when the nuclear charge is \(Z\) instead of \(1\), so the \(1s\) energy is \(-\frac{Z^2}{2}E_h\) and the total is \(E=2\times\left(-\frac{Z^2}{2}\right)E_h=-4\,E_h\), or about \(-10{,}500 \text{ kJ/mol}\),\footnote{\(E_h\) is the hartree, the atomic unit of energy that we introduced in section \ref{AtomicUnits}: \(1\,E_h = 27.211\text{ eV} = 2{,}625.5 \text{ kJ/mol}\).} compared with the exact value of \(-2.9037\,E_h=-7{,}624 \text{ kJ/mol}\). The error is enormous, about \(40\%\), because we have neglected a large positive contribution to the energy. We can partially fix it by keeping the same orbitals and adding back the \emph{average} repulsion between the two electrons, which for two \(1s\) electrons is \(\frac{5}{8}Z\,E_h\). The result, \(-2.75\,E_h = -7{,}220 \text{ kJ/mol}\), is already much better, with an error of about \(5\%\).

\subsubsection*{Optimizing the effective nuclear charge}\label{optimizing-the-effective-nuclear-charge}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Optimizing the effective nuclear charge}

We can do better using the variational method. The two electrons do not feel the full charge of the nucleus, since each of them is partially \emph{shielded} from it by the other. We can account for this by using, for both electrons, a \(1s\) orbital with an adjustable charge \(\zeta\) in place of \(Z\):

\begin{equation}
\psi_{1s}(r) = \sqrt{\frac{\zeta^3}{\pi a_0^3}}\, e^{-\zeta r/a_0},
\label{eq:hevar1}
\end{equation}

and treat \(\zeta\) as a variational parameter. Calculating the expectation value of the full Hamiltonian, eq. \eqref{eq:atom1}, with \(\Psi=\psi_{1s}({\bf r}_1)\psi_{1s}({\bf r}_2)\), gives three contributions (in \(E_h\)): the kinetic energy of the two electrons, \(\zeta^2\); their attraction to the nucleus of charge \(Z\), \(-2Z\zeta\); and their mutual repulsion, \(+\frac{5}{8}\zeta\). Summing them:

\begin{equation}
E(\zeta) = \zeta^2 - 2Z\zeta + \frac{5}{8}\zeta.
\label{eq:hevar2}
\end{equation}

This energy is plotted in figure \ref{fig:fig1c9}. To find its minimum, we set the derivative to zero:

\begin{equation}
\frac{dE}{d\zeta} = 2\zeta - 2Z + \frac{5}{8} = 0 \quad\longrightarrow\quad \zeta_{\text{min}} = Z - \frac{5}{16} = 1.6875,
\label{eq:hevar3}
\end{equation}

and replacing this value in eq. \eqref{eq:hevar2} gives \(E(\zeta_{\text{min}}) = -\left(Z-\frac{5}{16}\right)^2 = -2.8477\,E_h = -7{,}477 \text{ kJ/mol}\).

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth,alt={Plot of the variational energy of helium versus the orbital exponent zeta: a parabola with its minimum at zeta equal 1.6875, above the dotted line of the exact energy, with the unshielded value at zeta equal 2 marked higher on the curve.}]{./img/He_var} 

}

\caption{Variational energy of the helium atom as a function of the orbital exponent $\zeta$. The orange dot is the energy of the unshielded orbitals ($\zeta=Z=2$, with the average repulsion included), the purple dot is the variational minimum, and the dotted line is the exact energy.}\label{fig:fig1c9}
\end{figure}

A few important considerations can be made by analyzing these results:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{The variational theorem at work}: every point of the curve in figure \ref{fig:fig1c9} lies above the exact energy, as guaranteed by eq. \eqref{eq:varprinciple}. The minimum gets within \(147 \text{ kJ/mol}\) (about \(2\%\)) of the exact value, using a single adjustable number.
\item
  \textbf{Shielding}: the optimal value \(\zeta_{\text{min}}=1.6875\) is smaller than the real nuclear charge, \(Z=2\). We can interpret it as the \textbf{effective nuclear charge}, \(Z_\text{eff}\), felt by each electron. Each electron spends part of its time between the nucleus and the other electron, partially canceling the attraction that the other electron feels. The amount of shielding, \(\frac{5}{16}\approx0.31\) of a proton charge, is much less than a full electron charge because the two electrons occupy the same region of space: a \(1s\) electron is only rarely found ``inside'' the other one.
\item
  \textbf{Orbital size}: since \(\langle r\rangle \propto 1/\zeta\), a smaller effective charge means a larger orbital. The shielded \(1s\) orbitals of helium are about \(2/1.6875\approx1.19\) times larger than the unshielded ones.
\item
  \textbf{What is still missing}: even the best possible orbitals cannot reach the exact energy if we keep the product form of eq. \eqref{eq:hf3}. We will come back to this point at the end of this chapter, when we discuss electron correlation.
\end{enumerate}

The results obtained with the different approximations are summarized in figure \ref{fig:fig2c9}, which also reports the Hartree--Fock limit that we will discuss in the next chapter.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth,alt={Energy levels of helium from different approximations: no electron repulsion near minus 10,500 kilojoules per mole, average repulsion added near minus 7,220, variational near minus 7,477, Hartree-Fock limit near minus 7,513, and exact near minus 7,624.}]{./img/He_ladder} 

}

\caption{Energy of the helium atom computed with the approximations discussed in this chapter, compared with the exact value.}\label{fig:fig2c9}
\end{figure}

\section{Shielding and Penetration}\label{shielding-and-penetration}

The helium example shows that the main effect of the other electrons on any given electron is to reduce the nuclear charge that it feels. We can generalize this idea to any atom by writing the effective nuclear charge felt by an electron as:

\begin{equation}
Z_{\text{eff}} = Z - \sigma,
\label{eq:zeff}
\end{equation}

where \(\sigma\) is called the \textbf{shielding constant}, and it measures how much the other electrons screen the nucleus. In the simplest model of a many-electron atom, called the \textbf{central-field approximation}, each electron moves in the field of the nucleus plus the \emph{spherically averaged} field of all other electrons. Since this effective potential depends only on \(r\), the angular part of the TISEq is the same as for hydrogen, and the orbitals keep the familiar shapes of the spherical harmonics and the labels \(n\), \(\ell\), \(m_\ell\). The radial part, however, changes, and with it the energies. What the central-field approximation does \emph{not} include is the instantaneous repulsion between electrons: each electron feels only the average position of the others, not where they actually are at any given moment.

Inner-shell electrons are very effective at shielding the outer ones, while electrons of the same shell shield each other only partially (as we saw for helium). As a result, outer electrons feel a much smaller effective charge than inner electrons, they are held less tightly, and their orbitals are more diffuse. This explains why the valence electrons of an atom are the ones involved in chemistry, while the core electrons are not.

The most important consequence of shielding, however, is that it breaks the degeneracy of orbitals with the same \(n\) and different \(\ell\). Recall from chapter \ref{Hydrogen} that in the hydrogen atom the \(2s\) and \(2p\) orbitals have exactly the same energy. In a many-electron atom this is no longer the case. To understand why, consider the lithium atom, which has two core electrons, described by the \(1s\) orbital, and one valence electron. The radial probability distributions of the \(2s\) and \(2p\) orbitals, compared with that of the \(1s^2\) core, are plotted in figure \ref{fig:fig3c9}.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth,alt={Radial probability of the lithium 1s core, a tall narrow peak near the nucleus, compared with the 2s and 2p orbitals farther out. The 2s orbital has a small inner lobe that overlaps the core, while the 2p orbital does not.}]{./img/Li_penetration} 

}

\caption{Radial probability distributions of the $2s$ and $2p$ orbitals of lithium compared with the $1s^2$ core (gray), computed using hydrogen-like functions with effective nuclear charges. The radial node of the $2s$ orbital is highlighted with a purple dot.}\label{fig:fig3c9}
\end{figure}

The \(2s\) orbital has a small inner lobe, the one that we already noticed for hydrogen in chapter \ref{Hydrogen}, which places part of its probability \emph{inside} the \(1s^2\) core. When it is there, a \(2s\) electron feels almost the full nuclear charge. We say that the \(2s\) orbital \textbf{penetrates} the core. The \(2p\) orbital, instead, starts at zero at the nucleus and has no inner lobe, so it is more effectively shielded by the core electrons. As a result, a \(2s\) electron feels a larger \(Z_\text{eff}\) than a \(2p\) electron, and it is lower in energy. This is the \(2s\)--\(2p\) splitting that general chemistry textbooks show in their orbital energy diagrams. In general, for the same \(n\), penetration decreases as \(\ell\) increases (because of the \(r^\ell\) behavior of the radial functions close to the nucleus), so the energies of the orbitals follow the order \(ns < np < nd < nf\).

The effective nuclear charges felt by the \(1s\), \(2s\), and \(2p\) electrons of the second-row atoms can be obtained from variational calculations similar to the one we did for helium. Some of the results are reported in table \ref{tab:tab1c9}.

\begin{table}

\caption{\label{tab:tab1c9}Effective nuclear charges for the orbitals of the ground-state configurations of the second-row atoms, from variational calculations by Clementi and Raimondi.}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{l|c|c|c|c}
\hline
Atom & $Z$ & $Z_\text{eff}(1s)$ & $Z_\text{eff}(2s)$ & $Z_\text{eff}(2p)$\\
\hline
Li & 3 & 2.69 & 1.28 & —\\
\hline
Be & 4 & 3.68 & 1.91 & —\\
\hline
B & 5 & 4.68 & 2.58 & 2.42\\
\hline
C & 6 & 5.67 & 3.22 & 3.14\\
\hline
N & 7 & 6.67 & 3.85 & 3.83\\
\hline
O & 8 & 7.66 & 4.49 & 4.45\\
\hline
F & 9 & 8.65 & 5.13 & 5.10\\
\hline
Ne & 10 & 9.64 & 5.76 & 5.76\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

Notice how the \(1s\) electrons feel almost the full nuclear charge (they are shielded only by each other, as in helium), while the valence electrons feel a much smaller charge. Notice also how \(Z_\text{eff}(2s)\) is larger than \(Z_\text{eff}(2p)\), as expected from penetration, although the difference becomes small for the heavier atoms of the row. When the electrons of an atom are described using the orbitals in order of increasing energy, following the Pauli exclusion principle, the different penetration of \(s\), \(p\), \(d\), and \(f\) orbitals produces the familiar \textbf{aufbau} order, \(1s<2s<2p<3s<3p<4s<3d<\ldots\), and with it the structure of the periodic table.

\section{Atomic States and Term Symbols}\label{TermSymbols}

An electron configuration, such as \(1s^2 2s^2 2p^2\) for carbon, tells us which orbitals are used to describe the electrons, but it does not tell us everything about the state of the atom. In the carbon configuration, the two \(2p\) electrons can be described using any of the three \(2p\) orbitals, with either spin, in several different ways, and these arrangements do not all have the same energy. Since electrons repel each other, the energy depends on how their orbital motions and their spins are arranged relative to each other. As a result, a single configuration gives rise to several distinct energy levels, called \emph{atomic states}. These are the levels that we actually observe in atomic spectra, and chemists and spectroscopists label them using \emph{term symbols}.

\subsection{Total angular momenta}\label{total-angular-momenta}

In an atom with several electrons, the individual orbital angular momenta (quantum numbers \(\ell_i\) and \(m_{\ell,i}\)) and spins (\(s_i=\frac{1}{2}\) and \(m_{s,i}\)) combine into a \textbf{total orbital angular momentum} and a \textbf{total spin}. Their projections on the \(z\) axis are simply the sums of the individual projections:

\begin{equation}
M_L = \sum_i m_{\ell,i}, \qquad M_S = \sum_i m_{s,i}.
\label{eq:term1}
\end{equation}

The total angular momenta are described by two new quantum numbers, \(L\) and \(S\), which play the same role as \(\ell\) and \(s\) for a single electron: \(M_L\) can take the \(2L+1\) values \(-L,\ldots,+L\), and \(M_S\) the \(2S+1\) values \(-S,\ldots,+S\). A great simplification comes from the fact that a closed subshell (such as \(1s^2\) or \(2p^6\), where every spin-orbital of the subshell is used) always has \(M_L=0\) and \(M_S=0\) for every possible arrangement, and therefore contributes \(L=0\) and \(S=0\). Only the electrons of open subshells need to be considered.

\subsection{Term symbols}\label{term-symbols}

The states that arise from a configuration are labeled with a \textbf{term symbol}:

\begin{equation}
^{2S+1}L_J,
\label{eq:term2}
\end{equation}

where:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(L\) is written with a capital letter, following the same code used for orbitals: \(S\) for \(L=0\), \(P\) for \(L=1\), \(D\) for \(L=2\), \(F\) for \(L=3\), and so on. (Notice the unfortunate double use of the letter \(S\), both for \(L=0\) and for the total spin.)
\item
  The left superscript, \(2S+1\), is called the \textbf{multiplicity}. States with \(S=0\) (all spins paired) have multiplicity \(1\) and are called \emph{singlets}; states with \(S=\frac{1}{2}\) are \emph{doublets}; states with \(S=1\) are \emph{triplets}, and so on.
\item
  \(J\) is the quantum number of the \textbf{total angular momentum}, obtained by combining \(L\) and \(S\). It can take the values \(J=|L-S|, |L-S|+1, \ldots, L+S\).
\end{itemize}

For example, a sodium atom (\([\mathrm{Ne}]\,3s^1\)) has a single electron outside closed shells, so \(L=0\), \(S=\frac{1}{2}\), and \(J=\frac{1}{2}\): its ground state is \(^2S_{1/2}\) (read ``doublet S one-half''). The noble gases, and any atom with only closed subshells, have the ground state \(^1S_0\).

\subsection{The terms of carbon}\label{the-terms-of-carbon}

When a subshell contains more than one electron, a single configuration produces several terms. To find them, we proceed in three steps: list all the possible ways of describing the electrons, sort them by \(M_L\) and \(M_S\), and then group them into terms.

\textbf{Step 1: list the microstates.} Each \(2p\) electron can be described by one of six spin-orbitals: three values of \(m_\ell\) (\(+1\), \(0\), \(-1\)), each with spin up (\(\uparrow\)) or down (\(\downarrow\)). For two electrons, we need two \emph{different} spin-orbitals (Pauli principle), and since electrons are indistinguishable, the order does not matter. There are \(\frac{6\times5}{2}=15\) ways of choosing them, called \textbf{microstates}. For example, \((+1\uparrow, 0\uparrow)\) is the microstate in which one electron has \(m_\ell=+1\) and spin up, and the other has \(m_\ell=0\) and spin up. For each microstate, we can calculate \(M_L\) and \(M_S\) by adding the values of the two electrons (eq. \eqref{eq:term1}). For \((+1\uparrow, 0\uparrow)\), \(M_L=+1+0=+1\) and \(M_S=+\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}=+1\).

\textbf{Step 2: sort them in a grid.} We now arrange the 15 microstates in a grid, with one row for each value of \(M_L\) and one column for each value of \(M_S\), as shown in figure \ref{fig:fig5c9}. Notice that some cells contain more than one microstate, and that some are empty. For example, \(M_L=+2\) requires both electrons to have \(m_\ell=+1\), and the Pauli principle then forces them to have opposite spins, so \(M_L=+2\) is only possible with \(M_S=0\).

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.7\linewidth,alt={A grid with five rows, M L from plus 2 to minus 2, and three columns, M S equal plus 1, 0, and minus 1. The cells list the microstates as pairs such as plus 1 up, 0 up. The top and bottom rows contain a single microstate in the M S equal 0 column; the rows M L equal plus 1 and minus 1 contain one, two, and one microstates; the row M L equal 0 contains one, three, and one microstates.}]{./img/Carbon_microstates} 

}

\caption{The 15 microstates of the $2p^2$ configuration, sorted by $M_L$ (rows) and $M_S$ (columns). Each microstate lists the $m_\ell$ value and the spin of the two electrons.}\label{fig:fig5c9}
\end{figure}

\textbf{Step 3: group them into terms.} The key idea is that each term with quantum numbers \(L\) and \(S\) contains exactly \((2L+1)(2S+1)\) states: one for each combination of \(M_L=-L,\ldots,+L\) and \(M_S=-S,\ldots,+S\). In the grid, a term therefore looks like a \emph{rectangle}: \(2L+1\) rows tall and \(2S+1\) columns wide, with exactly one microstate in each cell. The terms can then be found by ``peeling off'' rectangles from the grid, starting from the largest values of \(M_L\), as shown in figure \ref{fig:fig6c9}:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{The \(^1D\) term.} The largest value of \(M_L\) in the grid is \(+2\). A microstate with \(M_L=+2\) must belong to a term with \(L=2\) (a \(D\) term), since a term with smaller \(L\) cannot reach \(M_L=+2\). This term must also contain all the other values of \(M_L\) from \(+2\) down to \(-2\), so it is \(5\) rows tall. Since the \(M_L=+2\) row has a microstate only in the \(M_S=0\) column, the term has only \(M_S=0\), so \(S=0\), and it is \(1\) column wide. This is a \(^1D\) term, a rectangle of \(5\times1=5\) microstates, one in each row of the \(M_S=0\) column (figure \ref{fig:fig6c9}b).
\item
  \textbf{The \(^3P\) term.} After removing the five \(^1D\) states, the largest remaining value of \(M_L\) is \(+1\), which now appears in all three columns, \(M_S=+1,0,-1\). This must be a term with \(L=1\) (a \(P\) term) and \(S=1\) (since \(M_S\) reaches \(+1\)). It is a rectangle \(3\) rows tall (\(M_L=+1,0,-1\)) and \(3\) columns wide: a \(^3P\) term with \(3\times3=9\) microstates (figure \ref{fig:fig6c9}c).
\item
  \textbf{The \(^1S\) term.} Only one microstate is left, with \(M_L=0\) and \(M_S=0\). It is a term with \(L=0\) and \(S=0\): a \(^1S\) term with a single state (figure \ref{fig:fig6c9}d).
\end{enumerate}

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=1\linewidth,alt={Four copies of a grid with rows M L from plus 2 to minus 2 and columns M S plus 1, 0, minus 1, with dots for microstates. Panel a shows 15 gray dots. Panel b outlines in green the M S equal 0 column, taking one dot from each of its five rows. Panel c outlines in blue the three-by-three block of rows plus 1 to minus 1 and all columns, taking nine dots. Panel d outlines in red the single remaining dot at M L equal 0, M S equal 0.}]{./img/Carbon_peeloff} 

}

\caption{Finding the terms of the $2p^2$ configuration. (a) Each dot is one microstate in the grid of the previous figure. (b)–(d) Each term occupies a rectangle of the grid, with one microstate in each cell: $^1D$ (5 states, green), $^3P$ (9 states, blue), and $^1S$ (1 state, red). Microstates already assigned to a term are shown faded.}\label{fig:fig6c9}
\end{figure}

Notice that, in the cells with more than one microstate, we do not need to decide \emph{which} microstate belongs to which term: only the number of states in each cell matters. (In fact, the real states of each term are combinations of the microstates in the same cell.) The \(2p^2\) configuration of carbon therefore produces three terms, \(^3P\), \(^1D\), and \(^1S\), with \(9+5+1=15\) states in total. Their experimental energies are shown in figure \ref{fig:fig4c9}.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth,alt={Energy diagram for carbon. On the left, a single level for the 1s2 2s2 2p2 configuration. In the middle it splits into three terms: 3P at 0, 1D near 10,200, and 1S near 21,600 inverse centimeters. On the right, 1D2 and 1S0 are unchanged, while an inset shows the 3P term split into 3P0 at 0, 3P1 at 16.4, and 3P2 at 43.4 inverse centimeters.}]{./img/Carbon_terms} 

}

\caption{Energy levels arising from the $1s^22s^22p^2$ configuration of carbon (experimental values). Electron–electron repulsion splits the configuration into three terms, and spin–orbit coupling further splits the $^3P$ term into three levels with different $J$ (inset, magnified).}\label{fig:fig4c9}
\end{figure}

A few important considerations can be made by analyzing this diagram:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{The size of the splitting}: the three terms are separated by about \(10{,}000\;\text{cm}^{-1}\) (more than \(100\;\text{kJ/mol}\)), comparable to the energy of chemical processes. The configuration alone, which is all that the simple orbital picture provides, is not enough to describe the atom.
\item
  \textbf{The triplet is lowest}: the term with the highest multiplicity, \(^3P\), has the lowest energy. In the triplet, the two electrons have parallel spins, and the antisymmetry of the wave function keeps electrons with the same spin away from each other (two electrons with the same spin can never be found at the same point), which reduces their repulsion. We will see this effect again in chapter \ref{Molecules}, where it appears as the exchange term of the Fock operator.
\item
  \textbf{Spin--orbit coupling is much smaller}: the three levels of the \(^3P\) term are only a few tens of \(\text{cm}^{-1}\) apart, a thousand times less than the separation between terms (see below).
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Hund's rules}\label{hunds-rules}

Finding all the terms of a configuration can be tedious, but in most cases we only need the ground state. This can be found using three empirical rules, formulated by Friedrich Hund in the 1920s:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  The term with the \textbf{largest multiplicity} (largest \(S\)) has the lowest energy.
\item
  For terms with the same multiplicity, the one with the \textbf{largest \(L\)} has the lowest energy.
\item
  If the open subshell has \textbf{fewer than half} of its maximum number of electrons, the level with the \textbf{lowest \(J\)} has the lowest energy; if it has \textbf{more than half}, the level with the \textbf{highest \(J\)} is lowest.
\end{enumerate}

For carbon, the first rule selects \(^3P\) as the ground term, and the third rule (\(2p^2\) has fewer than half of the six possible \(2p\) electrons) selects \(J=0\): the ground state of carbon is \(^3P_0\), in agreement with figure \ref{fig:fig4c9}. Oxygen (\(2p^4\)) has the same terms as carbon, since the \(2p^4\) configuration lacks two electrons with respect to the closed \(2p^6\) subshell, and behaves like \(2p^2\) (the two missing electrons are often called ``holes''), but it has more than half of the six possible \(2p\) electrons, and its ground state is \(^3P_2\). In practice, the ground term can be found quickly with the orbital diagrams of general chemistry, used here only as a bookkeeping device: we describe the electrons one at a time using the orbitals with the largest \(m_\ell\) first, keeping their spins parallel as long as possible, and then reading \(S\) from the number of unpaired electrons and \(L\) from the sum of the \(m_\ell\) values. The ground states of the first ten elements obtained in this way are reported in table \ref{tab:tab3c9}.

\begin{table}

\caption{\label{tab:tab3c9}Ground-state configurations and term symbols of the first ten elements.}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{l|c|c}
\hline
Element & Configuration & Ground state\\
\hline
H & $1s^1$ & $^2S_{1/2}$\\
\hline
He & $1s^2$ & $^1S_0$\\
\hline
Li & $[\mathrm{He}]2s^1$ & $^2S_{1/2}$\\
\hline
Be & $[\mathrm{He}]2s^2$ & $^1S_0$\\
\hline
B & $[\mathrm{He}]2s^22p^1$ & $^2P_{1/2}$\\
\hline
C & $[\mathrm{He}]2s^22p^2$ & $^3P_0$\\
\hline
N & $[\mathrm{He}]2s^22p^3$ & $^4S_{3/2}$\\
\hline
O & $[\mathrm{He}]2s^22p^4$ & $^3P_2$\\
\hline
F & $[\mathrm{He}]2s^22p^5$ & $^2P_{3/2}$\\
\hline

\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\subsection{Spin--orbit coupling}\label{spinorbit-coupling}

Term symbols include the quantum number \(J\) because of an effect that we have neglected so far: \textbf{spin--orbit coupling}. From the point of view of the electron, the charged nucleus is the one that moves around it, and this moving charge produces a magnetic field proportional to the orbital angular momentum. This field interacts with the magnetic moment associated with its spin. This interaction adds to the energy a term proportional to \(\mathbf{L}\cdot\mathbf{S}\), which depends on whether the spin and orbital angular momenta are aligned or opposed, and therefore on \(J\). It splits each term with \(L>0\) and \(S>0\) into its levels with different \(J\).

Spin--orbit coupling is small for light atoms, but it grows very rapidly with the nuclear charge (approximately as \(Z^4\)), and it is important for heavy elements. A familiar example is the yellow light of sodium street lamps, which comes from the transition from the first excited configuration, \([\mathrm{Ne}]\,3p^1\), to the ground state, \(^2S_{1/2}\). The \(3p^1\) configuration gives a single term, \(^2P\), which spin--orbit coupling splits into two levels, \(^2P_{1/2}\) and \(^2P_{3/2}\), separated by \(17\;\text{cm}^{-1}\). As a result, the sodium emission is not a single line, but a pair of lines at \(589.6\) and \(589.0\;\text{nm}\), known as the \emph{sodium D lines}. Their small splitting was one of the first experimental clues that led to the discovery of the electron spin.

\section{Electron Correlation}\label{electron-correlation}

Even with the best possible orbitals, a wave function built as a product of orbitals (or as a single Slater determinant) does not give the exact energy. The best energy that can be obtained in this way is called the \textbf{Hartree--Fock limit}, and for helium it is \(-2.8617\,E_h=-7{,}513 \text{ kJ/mol}\). This value is still \(110 \text{ kJ/mol}\) above the exact energy (figure \ref{fig:fig2c9}). The difference is called the \textbf{correlation energy}:

\begin{equation}
E_\text{corr} = E_\text{exact} - E_\text{HF}.
\label{eq:ecorr}
\end{equation}

The origin of this error is the same approximation that is at the heart of the central-field model. In an orbital-based wave function, each electron moves in the \emph{average} field of the others, and the probability of finding electron \(1\) at some position does not depend on where electron \(2\) is at that moment. Real electrons, however, repel each other and tend to stay away from each other: if electron \(1\) is on one side of the nucleus, electron \(2\) is more likely to be found on the other side. In other words, the motions of the electrons are \emph{correlated}, and an orbital-based wave function misses part of this correlation. Since the correlated electrons avoid each other more effectively, their repulsion is lower, and the exact energy is lower than the Hartree--Fock limit.

The correlation energy is a small fraction of the total energy (about \(1.5\%\) for helium), but it is of the same order of magnitude as the energy of a chemical bond, so it can be very important for chemistry. We will see a dramatic example in chapter \ref{Bonds}, when we look at the breaking of the bond in the hydrogen molecule. Methods that go beyond the Hartree--Fock limit are called \emph{correlated methods}, and they are among the most important tools of modern quantum chemistry.

The procedure that we used for helium can be extended to atoms with more than two electrons, and to molecules, resulting in the Hartree--Fock method. We will explain it in more detail in the next chapter, since it is also the simplest approximation that can describe the chemical bond.

\section{Chapter Review}\label{rev9}

\subsection{Study Questions}\label{quest9}

\textbf{1. When going from hydrogen to many‑electron atoms, which new term in the electronic Hamiltonian prevents an analytic solution of the TISEq?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  electron--nuclear attraction
\item
  kinetic energy
\item
  electron--electron repulsion
\item
  nuclear--nuclear repulsion
\item
  spin--orbit coupling
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\tightlist

  it remains 3 (independent of \(N\))
\item
  it is \(3N\) (three spatial coordinates for each electron)
\item
  it is \(N\)
\item
  it is \(2N\) (one radial and one angular coordinate per electron)
\item
  it is \(N^2\)
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\item

\item
  normalization to 1
\item
  invariance under global phase changes only
\item
  periodicity in each coordinate
\item
  antisymmetry under exchange of any two electrons (Pauli principle)
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\begin{enumerate}

\item
  by adding a constant term to the Hamiltonian
\item

  by ignoring spin altogether
\item
  by symmetrizing products of orbitals
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  always less than the exact ground‑state energy
\item
  equal to the exact ground‑state energy for any trial function
\item
  always greater than or equal to the exact ground‑state energy
\item
  independent of the choice of trial function
\item
  meaningful only for excited states
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist

\item

\item

\item
  in a purely harmonic potential
\item
  in free space without any potential

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\begin{enumerate}

\item

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\begin{enumerate}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}

  \(^1S_0\)\\
\item
