\item
  \(^3P_0\)\\
\item
  \(^3P_2\)\\
\item
  \(^2P_{1/2}\)
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{13. According to Hund's first rule, which term of a configuration has the lowest energy?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  the term with the smallest multiplicity\\
\item
  the term with the largest multiplicity (largest total spin \(S\))\\
\item
  the term with the smallest \(L\)\\
\item
  the term with the largest \(J\)\\
\item
  all terms have the same energy
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{14. Why does the emission of sodium consist of two lines (the D lines) instead of one?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  because sodium has two valence electrons\\
\item
  because of the Pauli exclusion principle\\
\item
  because spin--orbit coupling splits the \(^2P\) term into \(^2P_{1/2}\) and \(^2P_{3/2}\)\\
\item
  because the \(3s\) and \(3p\) orbitals are degenerate\\
\item
  because of the Born--Oppenheimer approximation
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

Answers: 1.c, 2.b, 3.e, 4.c, 5.c, 6.a, 7.b, 8.b, 9.c, 10.d, 11.c, 12.c, 13.b, 14.c

\chapter{Introduction to Molecules}\label{Molecules}

In the previous chapter we saw that a single extra electron is enough to make the TISEq impossible to solve exactly. Molecules add a further complication: they contain several nuclei, which can also move. In this chapter we will introduce the two approximations that make molecules tractable: the Born--Oppenheimer approximation, which separates the motion of the nuclei from that of the electrons, and the Hartree--Fock method, which extends the ideas that we used for the helium atom to molecular orbitals.

\section{The Molecular Hamiltonian}\label{the-molecular-hamiltonian}

For a molecule, we can decompose the Hamiltonian operator as:

\begin{equation}
\hat{H} = \hat{K}_N +\hat{K}_{e} + \hat{V}_{NN} + \hat{V}_{eN} + \hat{V}_{ee}
\label{eq:molham1}
\end{equation}

where we have decomposed the kinetic energy operator into nuclear and electronic terms, \(\hat{K}_N\) and \(\hat{K}_e\), as well as the potential energy operator into terms representing the interactions between nuclei, \(\hat{V}_{NN}\), between electrons, \(\hat{V}_{ee}\), and between electrons and nuclei, \(\hat{V}_{eN}\). Each term can then be calculated using:

\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\hat{K}_{N} &=-\sum_{i}^{\text{nuclei}}{\frac  {\hbar ^{2}}{2M_{i}}}\nabla_{{{\mathbf  {R}}_{i}}}^{2} \\
\hat  {K}_{e} &=-\sum_{i}^{\text{electrons}}{\frac  {\hbar ^{2}}{2m_{e}}}\nabla _{{{\mathbf{r}}_{i}}}^{2} \\
\hat{V}_{{NN}} &= \sum _{i}\sum _{{j>i}}{\frac  {Z_{i}Z_{j}e^{2}}{4\pi \varepsilon _{0}\left|{\mathbf  {R}}_{i}-{\mathbf  {R}}_{j}\right|}} \\
\hat  {V}_{{eN}} &=-\sum _{i}\sum _{j}{\frac  {Z_{i}e^{2}}{4\pi \varepsilon_{0}\left|{\mathbf  {R}}_{i}-{\mathbf  {r}}_{j}\right|}} \\
\hat{V}_{{ee}} &= \sum _{i}\sum _{{j>i}}{\frac  {e^{2}}{4\pi \varepsilon _{0}\left|{\mathbf  {r}}_{i}-{\mathbf  {r}}_{j}\right|}},
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:molham2}
\end{equation}

where \(M_i\), \(Z_i\), and \(\mathbf{R}_i\) are the mass, atomic number, and coordinates of nucleus \(i\), respectively, and all other symbols are the same as those used in eq. \eqref{eq:atom1} for the many-electron atom Hamiltonian. Notice that the two potential energy terms between like particles (\(\hat{V}_{NN}\) and \(\hat{V}_{ee}\)) are positive (repulsive), while the term between electrons and nuclei is negative (attractive). The chemical bond is the result of a delicate balance between these three terms and the kinetic energy.

\subsection{Small terms in the molecular Hamiltonian}\label{small-terms-in-the-molecular-hamiltonian}

Eq. \eqref{eq:molham1} is often called the ``exact'' non-relativistic Hamiltonian of a molecule, but it is worth remembering what it leaves out. The first missing piece is relativity. The valence electrons of light atoms move at a small fraction of the speed of light (less than 1\% for the electron in the hydrogen atom), but the inner electrons of heavy atoms are pulled so strongly by the nucleus that relativistic effects become important. The second missing piece is the coupling between the spin of each electron and its orbital motion. We can picture it by sitting on the electron: from its point of view, the charged nucleus is the one that moves around it, and this moving charge creates a magnetic field proportional to the orbital angular momentum, \({\bf L}\). The electron carries its own magnetic moment, proportional to its spin, \({\bf S}\), and the interaction between the two gives an energy term proportional to \({\bf L} \cdot {\bf S}\), called \emph{spin--orbit coupling}. Both effects grow quickly with the nuclear charge, so they are small for the molecules made of first- and second-row atoms that we discuss in this textbook, and we will neglect them from now on.

\section{The Born--Oppenheimer Approximation}\label{the-bornoppenheimer-approximation}

Before looking at molecules, let's recall a general property that we already used for the helium atom. Suppose that a Hamiltonian can be written as the sum of two terms, each depending on a different set of coordinates, \(q_1\) and \(q_2\):

\begin{equation}
\hat{H} = \hat{H}_1(q_1) + \hat{H}_2(q_2),
\label{eq:molham3}
\end{equation}

with the TISEq:

\begin{equation}
\hat{H} \psi(q_1, q_2) = E \psi(q_1, q_2).
\label{eq:molham4}
\end{equation}

Let's try a wave function written as a product of two functions, one for each set of coordinates:

\begin{equation}
\psi(q_1, q_2) = \psi_1(q_1) \psi_2(q_2),
\label{eq:molham5}
\end{equation}

where \(\psi_1(q_1)\) and \(\psi_2(q_2)\) are eigenfunctions of \(\hat{H}_1\) and \(\hat{H}_2\) with eigenvalues \(E_1\) and \(E_2\). Since \(\hat{H}_1\) acts only on \(q_1\) and \(\hat{H}_2\) only on \(q_2\), we obtain:

\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\displaystyle \hat{H} \psi(q_1, q_2) &= ( \hat{H}_1 + \hat{H}_2 ) \psi_1(q_1) \psi_2(q_2) \\
    &= \psi_2(q_2)\hat{H}_1 \psi_1(q_1) + \psi_1(q_1)\hat{H}_2 \psi_2(q_2) \\
    &= E_1 \psi_1(q_1) \psi_2(q_2) + E_2 \psi_1(q_1) \psi_2(q_2) \\
    &= (E_1 + E_2) \psi_1(q_1) \psi_2(q_2) \\
    &= E \psi(q_1, q_2).
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:molham6}
\end{equation}

In other words, when a Hamiltonian is a sum of independent pieces, its eigenfunctions are products of the eigenfunctions of each piece, and its eigenvalues are sums of their eigenvalues. A big problem is broken into smaller ones.

Can we use this trick to separate the electrons from the nuclei in eq. \eqref{eq:molham1}? Not exactly, because the electron--nucleus attraction, \(\hat{V}_{eN}\), depends on the coordinates of both electrons and nuclei at the same time. However, nuclei are much heavier than electrons (a proton is about \(1{,}836\) times heavier than an electron), so they move much more slowly. From the point of view of the electrons, the nuclei are almost standing still; from the point of view of the nuclei, the electrons adjust almost instantaneously to any change in their positions. This suggests that we can write the total wave function, approximately, as:

\begin{equation}
\psi({\bf r}, {\bf R}) \approx \psi_e({\bf r}; {\bf R}) \psi_N({\bf R}),
\label{eq:molham7}
\end{equation}

where \(\psi_e({\bf r}; {\bf R})\) is the electronic wave function calculated with the nuclei held fixed at positions \({\bf R}\). The semicolon indicates that \(\psi_e\) is a function of the electronic coordinates \({\bf r}\) that depends only \emph{parametrically} on \({\bf R}\): for each arrangement of the nuclei we get a different electronic wave function, but the nuclear positions are not variables of the electronic problem. This approximation is called the \textbf{Born--Oppenheimer approximation}, and it is accurate for the vast majority of chemical applications.

In practice, we fix the nuclei at some configuration, \({\bf R_a}\), and solve the TISEq for the electrons only, using the \textbf{electronic Hamiltonian}:

\begin{equation}
\hat{H}_{\text{e}} = \hat{K}_e({\bf r}) +
\hat{V}_{eN}\left({\bf r}; {\bf R_a} \right) +
\hat{V}_{ee}({\bf r}),
\label{eq:molham8}
\end{equation}

where we have dropped the nuclear kinetic energy, \(\hat{K}_N\), since the nuclei are fixed (and, in any case, \(\hat{K}_N\) is smaller than \(\hat{K}_e\) by roughly the ratio \(\frac{m_e}{M_i}\)). We have also left out \(\hat{V}_{NN}({\bf R_a})\), since for fixed nuclei it is just a constant that shifts all the eigenvalues by the same amount and can be added back at the end. The electron--nucleus attraction stays in the electronic Hamiltonian, but now it depends on \({\bf R_a}\) only as a parameter. The electronic Hamiltonian results in the following TISEq:

\begin{equation}
\hat{H}_{e} \psi_e \left({\bf r}; {\bf R_a} \right) = E_{e}({\bf R_a}) \psi_e \left({\bf r}; {\bf R_a} \right),
\label{eq:molham9}
\end{equation}

which is the equation that we will use to explain the chemical bond in the next chapter. Notice that eq. \eqref{eq:molham9} is not the total TISEq of the system, since it says nothing about the motion of the nuclei. As is customary in quantum chemistry, in the remainder of this textbook we will use the term ``total energy'' for the ``total energy at fixed geometry,'' which is \(E_{e}\) of eq. \eqref{eq:molham9} plus the constant nuclear--nuclear repulsion, \(V_{NN}({\bf R_a})\) (i.e., everything except the nuclear kinetic energy).

\subsection{Potential energy surfaces}\label{potential-energy-surfaces}

The Born--Oppenheimer approximation has a very important consequence. If we repeat the electronic calculation for many different arrangements of the nuclei, we obtain the total energy at fixed geometry as a function of the nuclear coordinates:

\begin{equation}
U({\bf R}) = E_e({\bf R}) + V_{NN}({\bf R}).
\label{eq:pes1}
\end{equation}

This function is called the \textbf{potential energy surface (PES)}, and it is the potential energy that the nuclei feel when they move. The motion of the nuclei (vibrations, rotations, and chemical reactions) can then be described by solving a second TISEq for the nuclei, with the PES playing the role of the potential: \(\left[\hat{K}_N + U({\bf R})\right]\psi_N({\bf R}) = E\,\psi_N({\bf R})\).

For a diatomic molecule, the only coordinate that matters is the internuclear distance, \(R\), and the PES reduces to a curve. The potential energy curve of the hydrogen molecule is plotted in figure \ref{fig:fig1c10}.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth,alt={Potential energy curve of the hydrogen molecule versus internuclear distance: a steep wall at short distance, a minimum at 1.4 bohr about 458 kilojoules per mole deep, and a flat approach to zero at large distance. A parabola fits only near the minimum, and horizontal lines mark vibrational levels that get closer together near the top.}]{./img/H2_PEC} 

}

\caption{Potential energy curve of the hydrogen molecule (blue), compared with its harmonic approximation (orange, dashed). The horizontal lines are the vibrational energy levels, and the energy is measured from the separated atoms. The purple dot marks the equilibrium bond distance.}\label{fig:fig1c10}
\end{figure}

A few important considerations can be made by analyzing this curve:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Equilibrium geometry}: the minimum of the curve, at \(R_e=1.40\,a_0=0.74\,\text{Å}\), is the equilibrium bond distance of the molecule. For larger molecules, minima of the PES correspond to stable structures, and finding them is called \emph{geometry optimization}.
\item
  \textbf{Bond energy}: the depth of the well, \(D_e=458\text{ kJ/mol}\), is the energy needed to separate the two atoms starting from the bottom of the well. The molecule, however, can never sit exactly at the bottom: even in its lowest vibrational level it has a zero-point energy, as we saw for the harmonic oscillator in chapter \ref{Models}. The experimentally measurable dissociation energy, \(D_0=D_e-\text{ZPE}=432\text{ kJ/mol}\), is measured from the lowest vibrational level.
\item
  \textbf{Vibrations}: close to the minimum, the curve looks like a parabola, and the harmonic oscillator of chapter \ref{Models} is a good model, with force constant equal to the curvature of the PES at the minimum, \(k=\left.\frac{d^2U}{dR^2}\right|_{R_e}\). As the energy increases, the real curve becomes wider than the parabola, and it flattens out towards the dissociation limit. As a consequence, the vibrational levels get closer to each other as they approach dissociation, unlike the equally spaced levels of the harmonic oscillator. We will see how these features show up in experiments in chapter \ref{Spectroscopy}.
\end{enumerate}

For a molecule with \(N_{\text{at}}\) atoms, the PES depends on \(3N_{\text{at}}-6\) internal coordinates (\(3N_{\text{at}}-5\) for linear molecules), and it is a surface in many dimensions. We cannot draw it, but we can get a feeling for its features using a model surface in two dimensions, such as the one in figure \ref{fig:fig2c10}.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=1\linewidth,alt={Left: a contour map of an energy surface with three valleys labeled A, B, and C, connected by a dashed path that passes over two saddle points labeled TS1 and TS2. Right: the energy along the dashed path, starting low at A, rising to a high peak at TS1, dropping to a shallow valley at B, crossing a small peak at TS2, and ending at C. An arrow marks the activation energy from A to TS1.}]{./img/PES_2D} 

}

\caption{(a) Contour plot of a model two-dimensional potential energy surface (the Müller–Brown surface). White dots mark minima, red crosses mark saddle points (transition states), and the dashed line is the minimum energy path that connects them. (b) Energy along the minimum energy path, plotted as a function of the distance traveled along it: the reaction coordinate.}\label{fig:fig2c10}
\end{figure}

Each minimum (A, B, and C) corresponds to a stable structure of the molecule, such as a reactant, a product, or an intermediate. To go from one minimum to another, the molecule must pass over a mountain pass, called a \textbf{saddle point}: a point that is a maximum along the path that connects the two minima, but a minimum along every other direction. In chemistry, these saddle points are the \textbf{transition states} of chemical reactions, and the energy difference between a minimum and the transition state is the activation energy of the reaction.

If we want to go from one minimum to another, there are infinitely many possible paths on the surface, but the most important one is the path of lowest energy, which follows the bottom of the valleys and crosses each mountain pass exactly at the saddle point. This path is called the \textbf{minimum energy path}, and it is shown as a dashed line in figure \ref{fig:fig2c10}a. The distance traveled along this path, measured from the starting minimum, is called the \textbf{reaction coordinate}. It is a single number that tells us how far the reaction has progressed, even though, in a real molecule, moving along it may require changing many bond lengths and angles at the same time. If we plot the energy along the reaction coordinate, as in figure \ref{fig:fig2c10}b, the many-dimensional surface reduces to the familiar one-dimensional \emph{reaction profile} of general chemistry and kinetics courses: reactants and products are minima, the transition state is the maximum between them, and the height of the barrier is the activation energy. Notice that the reaction profile hides most of the information contained in the surface: the reaction coordinate is not one of the nuclear coordinates \(q_1\) or \(q_2\), but a curved path through both of them. The PES is therefore the bridge between quantum mechanics and chemical reactivity: structures, vibrational frequencies, reaction paths, and activation energies are all properties of the PES.

\section{Solving the Electronic Eigenvalue Problem}\label{solving-the-electronic-eigenvalue-problem}

Once we have invoked the Born--Oppenheimer approximation, we can try to solve the electronic TISEq in eq. \eqref{eq:molham9}. For any molecule with more than one electron (i.e., all molecules except \(\mathrm{H}_2^+\) and a few exotic ions), we need to keep in mind the antisymmetry of the wave function, so we write the electronic wave function as a Slater determinant, as we did for atoms in chapter \ref{Atoms}. Then we need to approximate the Hamiltonian, since the electron-electron repulsion term does not allow an analytic solution. Similarly to the many-electron atom case, the simplest approximation is to use the variational method and to treat the electron-electron repulsion only in an average way, as we did for the helium atom in the previous chapter. This approximation is called the Hartree--Fock method.

\subsection{The Hartree--Fock Method}\label{the-hartreefock-method}

The main difference when we apply the variational principle to a molecular Slater determinant is that we need orbitals (one-electron wave functions) that extend over the entire molecule. We can build them by assuming that, close to each nucleus, a molecular orbital looks similar to the orbitals of the corresponding atom. The molecular orbital can then be written as a linear combination of atomic contributions. This method is called \textbf{linear combination of atomic orbitals (LCAO)}. In practice, we expand each molecular orbital \(\psi(\mathbf{r})\) as a linear combination of suitable basis functions \(f_i(\mathbf{r})\) centered on the atoms:

\begin{equation}
\psi(\mathbf{r}) = \sum_i^m c_{i} f_i(\mathbf{r}).
\label{eq:molham10}
\end{equation}

A consequence of the LCAO method is that basis functions on two different atoms are not orthogonal to each other, so the simplification that we used to go from eq. \eqref{eq:var4} to eq. \eqref{eq:var5} is not possible anymore. Using the following notation for the integrals:

\begin{equation}
\displaystyle H_{ij} = \int f_i^* {\hat H} f_j d\mathbf{\tau}\;, \qquad \displaystyle S_{ij} = \int f_i^* f_j d\mathbf{\tau},
\label{eq:molham11}
\end{equation}

the variational energy of eq. \eqref{eq:var2} becomes:

\begin{equation}
E[\Phi] = \frac{\sum_{ij} c_i^* c_j H_{ij}}{\sum_{ij} c_i^* c_j S_{ij}}.
\label{eq:molham11b}
\end{equation}

The integrals \(S_{ij}\) are called \textbf{overlap integrals}, and they measure how much two basis functions share the same region of space. To find the best orbital, we minimize this energy with respect to all the coefficients \(c_i\), setting each derivative to zero. This gives a set of \(m\) linear equations for the coefficients, which have a non-trivial solution only if the following \textbf{secular determinant} is equal to zero:

\begin{equation}
\begin{vmatrix}
H_{11}-ES_{11}   & H_{12}-ES_{12}   & \cdots & H_{1m}-ES_{1m}\\
H_{21}-ES_{21}   & H_{22}-ES_{22}   & \cdots & H_{2m}-ES_{2m}\\
\vdots           &   \vdots         & \ddots & \vdots\\
H_{m1}-ES_{m1}   & H_{m2}-ES_{m2}   & \cdots & H_{mm}-ES_{mm}
\end{vmatrix}=0,
\label{eq:molham12}
\end{equation}

where \(m\) is the number of basis functions. Expanding the determinant gives a polynomial of degree \(m\) in \(E\), whose \(m\) roots are the energies of the molecular orbitals. For each root, the linear equations give the corresponding coefficients. We will solve this problem explicitly for the simplest molecules in the next chapter.

\subsubsection*{The Fock operator}\label{the-fock-operator}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{The Fock operator}

What Hamiltonian should we use in eq. \eqref{eq:molham11}? For a one-electron molecule, such as \(\mathrm{H}_2^+\), it is simply the electronic Hamiltonian of eq. \eqref{eq:molham8}. For more than one electron, the Hartree--Fock method replaces the true many-electron problem with a set of one-electron problems, one for each occupied spin-orbital \(\chi_i\):

\begin{equation}
\hat{f}\, \chi_i = \varepsilon_i\, \chi_i,
\label{eq:hfeq1}
\end{equation}

where \(\varepsilon_i\) is the energy of the orbital, and \(\hat{f}\) is an effective one-electron Hamiltonian called the \textbf{Fock operator}:

\begin{equation}
\hat{f} = \hat{h} + \sum_{j}^{\text{occupied}} \left( \hat{J}_j - \hat{K}_j \right).
\label{eq:hfeq2}
\end{equation}

Each piece of \(\hat{f}\) has a clear physical meaning:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \(\hat{h}\) is the \textbf{core Hamiltonian}: the kinetic energy of one electron plus its attraction to all nuclei. This is all there is in a one-electron molecule.
\item
  \(\hat{J}_j\) is the \textbf{Coulomb operator}: the repulsion felt by an electron because of the \emph{average} charge distribution, \(|\chi_j|^2\), of the electron described by orbital \(\chi_j\). This is the ``average repulsion'' that we added back for the helium atom in chapter \ref{Atoms}.
\item
  \(\hat{K}_j\) is the \textbf{exchange operator}. It has no classical analog, and it appears because the wave function is a Slater determinant. It acts only between electrons with the same spin, and it lowers their repulsion, since the antisymmetry of the wave function keeps same-spin electrons away from each other (two electrons with the same spin can never be found at the same point, as a consequence of the Pauli exclusion principle).
\end{enumerate}

Eqs. \eqref{eq:hfeq1} are called the \textbf{Hartree--Fock equations}. They look like ordinary one-electron TISEqs, but there is a catch: the Fock operator depends, through \(\hat{J}_j\) and \(\hat{K}_j\), on the occupied orbitals, which are the solutions we are looking for. The equations are coupled with each other, since every orbital affects the operator that determines all other orbitals. When we expand the orbitals using the LCAO method, eq. \eqref{eq:molham10}, the Hartree--Fock equations take the form of the secular determinant in eq. \eqref{eq:molham12}, with the integrals \(H_{ij}\) replaced by the matrix elements of the Fock operator, \(F_{ij} = \int f_i^* \hat{f} f_j\, d\mathbf{\tau}\).

\subsubsection*{The self-consistent-field procedure}\label{the-self-consistent-field-procedure}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{The self-consistent-field procedure}

Since the Fock operator and its solutions depend on each other, the Hartree--Fock equations cannot be solved in one step. Instead, they are solved iteratively, using a cycle called the \textbf{self-consistent-field (SCF) procedure}, which is summarized in figure \ref{fig:fig3c10}. Starting from an initial guess of the coefficients, we build the Fock operator, solve the secular determinant, and obtain new coefficients. These are used to build an improved Fock operator, and the cycle is repeated until neither the orbitals nor the energy change anymore (within a chosen threshold). At that point, the orbitals are ``consistent'' with the field that they generate, and the energy of the molecule can be calculated from them.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.55\linewidth,alt={Flowchart of the self-consistent-field procedure: choose basis and geometry, compute integrals, guess coefficients, build the Fock operator, solve the secular determinant, and check convergence, looping back to building the Fock operator until converged.}]{./img/SCF_cycle} 

}

\caption{Flowchart of the self-consistent-field (SCF) procedure used to solve the Hartree–Fock equations.}\label{fig:fig3c10}
\end{figure}

The SCF procedure is too tedious to perform by hand, even for small molecules, but it is easily done by computer programs. Every time that you run a quantum chemistry calculation, the first thing the program does is solve this cycle.

\section{Chapter Review}\label{rev10}

\subsection{Study Questions}\label{quest10}

\textbf{1. In the full non‑relativistic molecular Hamiltonian (before approximations), which terms are present?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  only electronic kinetic energy and electron--nuclear attraction
\item
  only nuclear kinetic energy and nuclear--nuclear repulsion
\item
  spin--orbit and relativistic corrections only
\item
  only electron--electron repulsion and nuclear--nuclear repulsion
\item
  electronic and nuclear kinetic energies, electron--nuclear, electron--electron, and nuclear--nuclear interactions
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{2. What makes the exact molecular Schrödinger equation harder than the atomic case?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  electrons disappear in molecules
\item
  nuclei can be treated classically in atoms but not in molecules
\item
  the presence of multiple moving nuclei in addition to many electrons
\item
  there is no Coulomb interaction in molecules
\item
  the electron mass changes in molecules
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{3. What is the central physical idea behind the Born--Oppenheimer approximation?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  electrons are much heavier than nuclei
\item
  nuclei are much heavier than electrons, so electrons adjust almost instantaneously to nuclear motion
\item
  electrons and nuclei move with the same characteristic time scale
\item
  both electrons and nuclei can be treated as classical particles
\item
  nuclei do not contribute to the energy at all
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{4. Mathematically, the Born--Oppenheimer approximation relies on what property of the Hamiltonian?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  exact commutation with the position operator
\item
  approximate separability into electronic and nuclear parts
\item
  absence of kinetic energy terms
\item
  Hermiticity of the potential energy operator
\item
  linearity of the time derivative
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{5. In practical quantum‑chemistry, what is usually meant (by abuse of language) by ``total energy'' of a molecule at fixed geometry?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  electronic energy \(E_e\) at that geometry plus the constant nuclear--nuclear repulsion term
\item
  nuclear kinetic energy only
\item
  electronic energy without nuclear--nuclear repulsion
\item
  purely classical potential energy
\item
  nuclear vibrational zero‑point energy
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{6. Which of the following statements regarding potential‑energy surfaces (PESs) is correct?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  a PES is the graph of electronic energy (plus nuclear repulsion) as a function of nuclear geometry
\item
  a PES is the graph of nuclear kinetic energy versus time
\item
  a PES is independent of nuclear positions
\item
  a PES depends only on electron spin
\item
  PESs are defined only for diatomic molecules
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{7. Which of the following is \emph{not} a direct consequence or assumption of the Born--Oppenheimer approximation?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  electronic and nuclear motions can be approximately separated
\item
  electrons move in the field of fixed nuclei
\item
  nuclear motion can be described on a PES obtained from electronic energies
\item
  electron--nuclear coupling can never be important for any property
\item
  total wave functions are approximated as products of electronic and nuclear parts
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{8. When the variational principle is applied to a Slater determinant built from molecular spin‑orbitals, minimizing the energy with respect to the orbitals leads to what?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  the time‑dependent Schrödinger equation
\item
  an over‑determined system with no nontrivial solutions
\item
  a single algebraic equation for the total energy
\item
  purely classical equations of motion for nuclei
\item
  a set of coupled one‑electron equations involving the Fock operator (Hartree--Fock equations)
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{9. Which of the following sentences describes the Fock operator \(\hat{f}\) in Hartree--Fock theory?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  a two‑electron operator containing only electron--electron repulsion
\item
  a purely nuclear operator
\item
  a one‑electron effective operator containing kinetic, nuclear attraction, and averaged electron--electron (Coulomb and exchange) terms
\item
  an operator that depends only on spin and not on spatial coordinates
\item
  the exact electronic Hamiltonian
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{10. In the Hartree--Fock method, how is the Pauli exclusion principle enforced for many‑electron systems?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  by forcing all electrons into different nuclei
\item
  by requiring all spatial orbitals to be orthogonal but ignoring spin
\item
  by constructing an antisymmetric Slater determinant of spin‑orbitals
\item
  by adding a large repulsive potential at short inter‑electronic distances
\item
  by using only hydrogen‑like orbitals
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

Answers: 1.e, 2.c, 3.b, 4.b, 5.a, 6.a, 7.d, 8.e, 9.c, 10.c

\chapter{The Chemical Bond in Diatomic Molecules}\label{Bonds}

In this chapter we will see a couple of examples of how the concept and mathematics of quantum mechanics can be applied to understand the chemical bond in molecules. We will start from the simplest molecule, the \(\mathrm{H}_2^+\) molecular ion, we will then move on to the simplest two-electron bond in the hydrogen molecule, and finally we will use the same ideas to understand the bonds in the diatomic molecules of the second row, such as \(\mathrm{N}_2\) and \(\mathrm{O}_2\). To simplify the notation, in this chapter we will use the atomic units that we introduced in section \ref{AtomicUnits}, in which \(\hbar=m_e=e=4\pi\varepsilon_0=1\), energies are measured in hartrees (\(1\,E_h = 2{,}625.5\text{ kJ/mol}\)), and distances in bohrs (\(1\,a_0 = 0.529\,\text{Å}\)).

\section{The Chemical Bond in the Hydrogen Molecular Cation}\label{the-chemical-bond-in-the-hydrogen-molecular-cation}

This system has only one electron, but since its geometry is not spherical (figure \ref{fig:Fig1c11}), the TISEq cannot be solved analytically as for the hydrogen atom.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.7\linewidth,alt={Geometry of the hydrogen molecular ion: two protons A and B separated by distance R, and an electron at point P at distances r A and r B from them.}]{./img/OEP_figure1} 

}

\caption{Geometry of the hydrogen molecular cation.}\label{fig:Fig1c11}
\end{figure}

The electron is at point \(P\), while the two protons are at positions \(A\) and \(B\) at a fixed distance \(R\). Using the Born-Oppenheimer approximation we can write the one-electron molecular Hamiltonian in a.u. as:

\begin{equation}
\hat{H} = \hat{H}_e+\frac{1}{R} = \left( -\frac{1}{2}\nabla^2-\frac{1}{r_A}-\frac{1}{r_B} \right)+\frac{1}{R}
\label{eq:bond1}
\end{equation}

As a first approximation to the variational wave function, we can build the one-electron molecular orbital (MO) by linearly combining two \(1s\) hydrogenic orbitals centered at \(A\) and \(B\), respectively:

\begin{equation}
\varphi = c_1 a + c_2 b,
\label{eq:bond2}
\end{equation}

with:

\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
a &= 1s_A = \left( \psi_{100} \right)_A\\
b &= 1s_B = \left( \psi_{100} \right)_B.
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:bond3}
\end{equation}

Using eq. \eqref{eq:molham11} and considering that the nuclei are identical, we can define the integrals \(H_{aa}=H_{bb}, H_{ab}=H_{ba}\) and \(S_{ab}=S\) (while \(S_{aa}=1\) because the hydrogen atom orbitals are normalized). The integral \(H_{aa}\) is called the \emph{Coulomb integral}, and it is the energy of an electron described by orbital \(a\), including its attraction to the second nucleus, \(B\). The integral \(H_{ab}\) is called the \emph{resonance integral} (or exchange integral), and it has no classical counterpart: it describes the energy associated with the electron being shared between the two centers. It is negative, and it is responsible for the bond. The secular equation, eq. \eqref{eq:molham12}, can then be written:

\begin{equation}
\begin{vmatrix}
H_{aa}-E   & H_{ab}-ES \\
H_{ab}-ES  & H_{aa}-E
\end{vmatrix}=0
\label{eq:bond4}
\end{equation}

The expansion of the determinant results in:

\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
(H_{aa}-E)^2 &=(H_{ab}-ES)^2 \\
H_{aa}-E &= \pm (H_{ab}-ES), \\
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:bond5}
\end{equation}

with roots:

\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
E_{+} &= \frac{H_{aa}+H_{ab}}{1+S} = H_{aa}+\frac{H_{ba}-SH_{aa}}{1+S}, \\
E_{-} &= \frac{H_{aa}-H_{ab}}{1-S} = H_{aa}-\frac{H_{ba}-SH_{aa}}{1-S}, 
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:bond6}
\end{equation}

the first corresponding to the ground state, the second to the first excited state. Solving for the best value for the coefficients of the linear combination for the ground state \(E_{+}\), we obtain:

\begin{equation}
c_1=c_2=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2+2S}},
\label{eq:bond7}
\end{equation}

which gives the bonding MO:

\begin{equation}
\varphi_{+}=\frac{a+b}{\sqrt{2+2S}}.
\label{eq:bond8}
\end{equation}

Proceeding similarly for the excited state, we obtain:

\begin{equation}
c_1=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2-2S}}\;\quad c_2=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2-2S}},
\label{eq:bond9}
\end{equation}

which gives the antibonding MO:

\begin{equation}
\varphi_{-}=\frac{a-b}{\sqrt{2-2S}}.
\label{eq:bond10}
\end{equation}

These results can be summarized in the molecular orbital diagram of figure \ref{fig:Fig2c11}. We notice that the splitting of the doubly degenerate atomic level under the interaction is non-symmetric for \(S\neq0\), the antibonding level being pushed up more than the bonding level is pushed down, compared with the symmetric case that would occur for \(S = 0\).

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.7\linewidth,alt={Molecular orbital diagram of the hydrogen molecular ion: the 1s levels of the two atoms combine into a lower bonding orbital and a higher antibonding orbital.}]{./img/OEP_figure2} 

}

\caption{Molecular orbitals diagram for the hydrogen molecular cation.}\label{fig:Fig2c11}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Wave function analysis}\label{wave-function-analysis-4}

The bonding and antibonding molecular orbitals are plotted in figure \ref{fig:Fig7c11} as color maps in a plane that contains the two nuclei, and in figure \ref{fig:Fig8c11} along the internuclear axis, together with their probability densities.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.9\linewidth,alt={Two color maps of the hydrogen molecular ion orbitals in a plane containing both nuclei. The bonding orbital is positive everywhere with density concentrated between the nuclei; the antibonding orbital is positive around one nucleus and negative around the other, with a nodal plane midway.}]{./img/H2p_MO_maps} 

}

\caption{Color maps of the bonding (left) and antibonding (right) molecular orbitals of the hydrogen molecular cation at $R=2\,a_0$, in a plane that contains the two nuclei (black dots). Color scheme: red (positive), blue (negative), white/light (close to zero). The nodal plane of the antibonding orbital is reported in purple.}\label{fig:Fig7c11}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=1\linewidth,alt={Left: bonding and antibonding orbitals of the hydrogen molecular ion along the bond axis, with the bonding orbital positive everywhere and the antibonding orbital crossing zero at the midpoint. Right: the bonding density is larger than the average atomic density between the nuclei, while the antibonding density drops to zero there.}]{./img/H2p_MO_lines} 

}

\caption{Left: the bonding (blue) and antibonding (red) molecular orbitals of the hydrogen molecular cation along the internuclear axis at $R=2\,a_0$, compared with the two atomic orbitals $a$ and $b$ (gray, dashed). The node of the antibonding orbital is reported as a purple dot. Right: the corresponding probability densities, compared with the average density of the two atomic orbitals (gray, dashed). The positions of the nuclei are marked by dotted vertical lines.}\label{fig:Fig8c11}
\end{figure}

A few important considerations can be made by analyzing these plots:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Constructive and destructive interference}: the bonding orbital is the sum of the two atomic orbitals, which add up in the region between the nuclei, just like two waves that interfere constructively. The antibonding orbital is their difference, and the two atomic orbitals cancel each other exactly halfway between the nuclei, like two waves that interfere destructively.
\item
  \textbf{Nodes}: as for all the systems that we have studied so far, the higher energy state has more nodes. The bonding orbital has no nodes, while the antibonding orbital has a nodal plane perpendicular to the bond axis, where the probability of finding the electron is exactly zero.
\item
  \textbf{Charge accumulation}: the probability density of the bonding orbital is \emph{larger} than the average density of the atomic orbitals in the region between the nuclei, at the expense of the regions close to the nuclei and behind them. An electron in this region is attracted by both nuclei at the same time, and this is the origin of the chemical bond. The antibonding orbital does the opposite: it removes probability from the region between the nuclei, leaving the two protons less shielded from each other.
\item
  \textbf{Symmetry labels}: both orbitals are symmetric with respect to rotations around the bond axis, which is why they are called \(\sigma\) orbitals. If we invert any point through the center of the molecule, the bonding orbital stays the same, while the antibonding orbital changes sign. These two behaviors are labeled \(g\) (from the German \emph{gerade}, even) and \(u\) (\emph{ungerade}, odd). Therefore, the bonding orbital is called \(\sigma_g\), and the antibonding orbital \(\sigma_u\).
\end{enumerate}

To obtain the energy as a function of the internuclear distance, we need to calculate the values of the integrals, and to repeat the calculation for different distances, \(R\). For \(1s\) orbitals, all the integrals can be calculated analytically. For example, the overlap integral is \(S=e^{-R}\left(1+R+\frac{R^2}{3}\right)\), which goes from \(1\) when the two nuclei coincide to \(0\) when they are far apart. The resulting energies of the two states, measured from the separated fragments (one hydrogen atom and one proton), are plotted in figure \ref{fig:Fig3c11}. The energy of the ground state is negative for a wide range of distances, which means that, in this range, the molecule is more stable than the separated fragments. The minimum of the curve is at \(R=2.49\,a_0\) and \(E_+=-0.065\,E_h=-170\text{ kJ/mol}\), which is a reasonable first approximation to the exact values (\(R=2.00\,a_0\) and \(-269\text{ kJ/mol}\)). The excited state, instead, is repulsive at all distances: if the electron is promoted to the antibonding orbital, the molecule falls apart.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth,alt={Energy of the hydrogen molecular ion versus internuclear distance: the bonding curve has a minimum at 2.49 bohr about 170 kilojoules per mole below the separated atoms, the antibonding curve is repulsive at all distances, and a star marks the exact minimum at 2.00 bohr.}]{./img/H2p_PEC} 

}

\caption{Born–Oppenheimer potential energy curves for the ground (bonding) and first excited (antibonding) states of the hydrogen molecular cation, computed with the LCAO wave functions. The purple dot marks the LCAO minimum, and the black star the exact minimum.}\label{fig:Fig3c11}
\end{figure}

\section{The Chemical Bond in the Hydrogen Molecule}\label{the-chemical-bond-in-the-hydrogen-molecule}

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.7\linewidth,alt={Geometry of the hydrogen molecule: two protons A and B separated by distance R, and two electrons 1 and 2 with their distances to each proton and to each other.}]{./img/OEP_figure4} 

}

\caption{Geometry of the hydrogen molecule.}\label{fig:Fig4c11}
\end{figure}

We can now examine the formation of the two-electron chemical bond in the \(\text{H}_2\) molecule. With reference to figure \ref{fig:Fig4c11}, the molecular Hamiltonian for \(\text{H}_2\) in a.u. in the Born-Oppenheimer approximation will be:

\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\hat{H} &= \hat{H}_e+\frac{1}{R} \\
&=\left( -\frac{1}{2}\nabla^2_1-\frac{1}{r_{A1}}-\frac{1}{r_{B1}} \right)+\left( -\frac{1}{2}\nabla^2_2-\frac{1}{r_{A2}}-\frac{1}{r_{B2}} \right)+\frac{1}{r_{12}}+\frac{1}{R}\\
&= \hat{h}_1+\hat{h}_2+\frac{1}{r_{12}}+\frac{1}{R},
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:bond11}
\end{equation}

where \(\hat{h}\) is the one-electron Hamiltonian. As for the previous case, we can build the first approximation to the molecular wave function from two \(1s\) atomic orbitals, \(a\) and \(b\), centered at \(A\) and \(B\), respectively, with overlap \(S\). If we neglect the electron-electron repulsion term, \(\frac{1}{r_{12}}\), the Hamiltonian is a sum of two identical one-electron Hamiltonians, and the molecular orbitals are exactly the same as those we obtained for \(\mathrm{H}_2^+\). The most important difference is that now we need to consider the spin of the two electrons. Proceeding as we did for the helium atom in chapter \ref{Atoms}, we can build an antisymmetric wave function for \(\text{H}_2\) using a Slater determinant in which the lowest molecular orbital, the bonding \(\sigma_g\) orbital of eq. \eqref{eq:bond8}, is used to describe two electrons with opposite spin:\footnote{Compare this equation to eq. \eqref{eq:atom6} for the helium atom.}

\begin{equation}
\Psi (\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2})= |\sigma_{g}\alpha\;\sigma_{g}\beta|=\sigma_{g}(\mathbf{r}_1)\sigma_{g}(\mathbf{r}_2) \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} \left[ \alpha(1)\beta(2) - \beta(1)\alpha(2) \right],
\label{eq:bond12}
\end{equation}

where:

\begin{equation}
\sigma_{g}=\varphi_{+}=\frac{\left(\psi_{100}\right)_A+\left(\psi_{100}\right)_B}{\sqrt{2+2S}}.
\label{eq:bond13}
\end{equation}

A Slater determinant in which each spatial orbital is used to describe two electrons with opposite spin is called a \textbf{restricted Hartree--Fock (RHF)} wave function, since both electrons are restricted to be described by the same spatial orbital. The energies and the resulting MO diagram are similar to those for \(\mathrm{H}_2^+\), with the only difference that two electrons are described by the same \(\sigma_g\) MO (figure \ref{fig:Fig5c11}).

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.7\linewidth,alt={Molecular orbital diagram of the hydrogen molecule: the 1s levels of the two atoms combine into a bonding sigma g orbital describing two electrons with opposite spins, and an unoccupied antibonding orbital above.}]{./img/OEP_figure5} 

}

\caption{Molecular orbitals diagram for the hydrogen molecule.}\label{fig:Fig5c11}
\end{figure}

As for the many-electron atoms, the Hartree--Fock method is just an approximation to the exact solution. The accurate theoretical value for the bond energy at the equilibrium distance, \(R_e=1.4\;a_0\), is \(-0.17447\;E_h\) (\(-458\text{ kJ/mol}\)). The variational result obtained with the wave function in eq. \eqref{eq:bond12} is \(-0.12778\;E_h\) (\(-335\text{ kJ/mol}\)), which is about \(73 \%\) of the exact value. In this calculation, the exponent of the \(1s\) orbitals, \(c_0\) in \(\psi_{100}=\sqrt{\frac{c_0^3}{\pi}}\exp[-c_0r]\), is used as a variational parameter, exactly as we did for helium in chapter \ref{Atoms}. Its optimal value at \(R_e\) is \(c_0=1.1695\), larger than \(1\): in the molecule, each electron feels the attraction of two nuclei, and the orbitals contract.

If we scan the Born--Oppenheimer potential energy curve with the wave function in eq. \eqref{eq:bond12}, as we did for \(\mathrm{H}_2^+\), we obtain the red curve in figure \ref{fig:Fig6c11}.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth,alt={Potential energy curves of the hydrogen molecule from Hartree-Fock and from configuration interaction. Both have a minimum near 1.4 bohr, but the Hartree-Fock curve rises far above the energy of two separated atoms at large distance, while the configuration interaction curve correctly approaches it.}]{./img/H2_HF_CI} 

}

\caption{Born–Oppenheimer potential energy curves for the hydrogen molecule computed with the restricted Hartree–Fock wave function (red) and with configuration interaction (blue). Both calculations use a minimal basis of $1s$ functions with the exponent optimized at each distance (approximated with three gaussian functions, so the numbers differ slightly from those in the text). Energies are measured from two separated hydrogen atoms.}\label{fig:Fig6c11}
\end{figure}

The Hartree--Fock results for \(\mathrm{H}_2\) describe the formation of the bond qualitatively around the equilibrium distance (the minimum of the curve), but they fail completely at dissociation: instead of going to the energy of two hydrogen atoms, the curve keeps rising, and at large distances the molecule is predicted to be \emph{less} stable than the separated atoms by hundreds of kJ/mol. To understand why, we can write the spatial part of eq. \eqref{eq:bond12} in terms of the atomic orbitals:

\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\sigma_{g}(\mathbf{r}_1)\sigma_{g}(\mathbf{r}_2) &= \frac{1}{2+2S}\left[a(1)+b(1)\right]\left[a(2)+b(2)\right] \\
&= \frac{1}{2+2S} \Big[ \underbrace{a(1)b(2)+b(1)a(2)}_{\text{covalent: } \mathrm{H}\!-\!\mathrm{H}} + \underbrace{a(1)a(2)+b(1)b(2)}_{\text{ionic: } \mathrm{H}^-\,\mathrm{H}^+ \text{ and } \mathrm{H}^+\,\mathrm{H}^-} \Big],
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:bond14}
\end{equation}

where \(a(1)\) is short for \(a(\mathbf{r}_1)\), and so on. The first two terms describe one electron on each atom, the covalent picture of the bond. The last two terms describe both electrons on the same atom, i.e., an \(\mathrm{H}^-\) ion next to a bare proton. The RHF wave function always contains the two types of terms \emph{with the same weight}, whatever the distance between the nuclei. Close to equilibrium this is not too bad, since the ionic terms give a reasonable description of the electrons being shared between the two nuclei. At dissociation, however, the correct answer is two neutral hydrogen atoms, and the ionic terms (which describe the much less stable \(\mathrm{H}^-+\mathrm{H}^+\) pair) should disappear completely. The RHF wave function cannot do that, and its energy at large distance is too high.

\subsection{Beyond Hartree--Fock}\label{beyond-hartreefock}

This failure is not a failure of quantum mechanics, but of the approximation that we used: it is an extreme example of the electron correlation that we discussed in chapter \ref{Atoms}. In the RHF wave function the two electrons are described by the same orbital and move independently of each other, so there is nothing that prevents them from being found on the same atom. In reality, when the two atoms are far apart, if one electron is on atom \(A\) the other is almost certainly on atom \(B\): their motions are strongly correlated.

We can fix the problem by mixing into the wave function a second Slater determinant, in which both electrons are described by the antibonding orbital, \(\sigma_u=\varphi_-\):

\begin{equation}
\Psi_\text{CI} = c_1\, |\sigma_{g}\alpha\;\sigma_{g}\beta| + c_2\, |\sigma_{u}\alpha\;\sigma_{u}\beta|,
\label{eq:bond15}
\end{equation}

where the coefficients \(c_1\) and \(c_2\) are determined with the variational method. Expanding the spatial part of the second determinant as we did in eq. \eqref{eq:bond14}, we obtain \(\sigma_u(1)\sigma_u(2)\propto\left[a(1)a(2)+b(1)b(2)\right]-\left[a(1)b(2)+b(1)a(2)\right]\): the same ionic and covalent terms, but with opposite relative sign. By choosing \(c_2\) negative, we can therefore reduce the weight of the ionic terms. At equilibrium the variational method gives a small \(c_2\), while at dissociation it gives \(c_2=-c_1\), which removes the ionic terms completely and leaves the correct covalent wave function. The resulting potential energy curve is the blue curve in figure \ref{fig:Fig6c11}. It goes smoothly to the energy of two hydrogen atoms at dissociation, and it is also lower than the RHF curve near the minimum, since it recovers part of the correlation energy.

A wave function written as a linear combination of several Slater determinants, such as eq. \eqref{eq:bond15}, is called a \textbf{configuration interaction (CI)} wave function, and it is the simplest example of a \emph{correlated method}. Modern quantum chemistry programs routinely use correlated methods to compute bond energies to within a few kJ/mol of the experimental values.

\section{Homonuclear Diatomic Molecules of the Second Row}\label{homonuclear-diatomic-molecules-of-the-second-row}

We can now use what we learned for \(\mathrm{H}_2^+\) and \(\mathrm{H}_2\) to understand the bonds formed by the atoms of the second row of the periodic table, from lithium to neon. Here we will not perform the calculations explicitly, but we will use the same ideas to build qualitative MO diagrams, which are one of the most powerful tools that chemists have to reason about bonding.

\subsection{Building the molecular orbitals}\label{building-the-molecular-orbitals}

Each second-row atom has a \(1s\) core and valence electrons, described by the \(2s\) and \(2p\) orbitals. As we saw in chapter \ref{Atoms}, the core orbitals are very compact and very low in energy, so the \(1s\) orbitals of the two atoms barely overlap, and they combine into a bonding and an antibonding orbital, which together describe the four core electrons and have almost the same energy as the atomic orbitals. Their effects cancel out, and we can ignore them. The interesting chemistry happens in the valence shell, where each atom contributes four atomic orbitals, \(2s\), \(2p_x\), \(2p_y\), and \(2p_z\), for a total of eight. The LCAO method of chapter \ref{Molecules} would then give an \(8\times8\) secular determinant. Fortunately, we do not need to solve it to understand the results, because of two simple rules:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Only orbitals with similar energies interact strongly.} The difference between the energies of the atomic orbitals enters the solution of the secular determinant in the same way as \(H_{aa}\) did for \(\mathrm{H}_2^+\): the closer two atomic orbitals are in energy, the more they mix and the larger the splitting between the resulting bonding and antibonding orbitals.
\item
  \textbf{Only orbitals with the same symmetry with respect to the bond axis can interact.} If we take the bond axis as \(z\), the \(2s\) and \(2p_z\) orbitals are symmetric with respect to rotations around the axis, and they form \(\sigma\) orbitals, as we saw for hydrogen. The \(2p_x\) and \(2p_y\) orbitals are perpendicular to the axis, and they change sign when we rotate the molecule by \(180^\circ\) around it. Their overlap with an orbital of \(\sigma\) symmetry is exactly zero (the positive and negative lobes cancel), so they form separate molecular orbitals, called \(\pi\) orbitals.
\end{enumerate}

Using these two rules, the \(8\times8\) problem splits into smaller pieces. To a first approximation, the two \(2s\) orbitals combine into a bonding \(\sigma_g 2s\) and an antibonding \(\sigma_u^* 2s\) orbital, exactly as the \(1s\) orbitals did in \(\mathrm{H}_2\). The two \(2p_z\) orbitals, which point towards each other, combine into a bonding \(\sigma_g 2p\) and an antibonding \(\sigma_u^* 2p\) orbital. Finally, the \(2p_x\) orbitals on the two atoms combine side by side into a bonding \(\pi_u 2p\) and an antibonding \(\pi_g^* 2p\) orbital, and the same happens for the \(2p_y\) orbitals. The two \(\pi_u\) orbitals (one from \(p_x\) and one from \(p_y\)) have the same energy, and so do the two \(\pi_g^*\) orbitals. (The asterisk is a common way of marking antibonding orbitals.) The orbitals formed by the \(2p\) functions are plotted in figure \ref{fig:Fig9c11}.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth,alt={Four color maps of molecular orbitals formed from 2p atomic orbitals: sigma bonding with density between the nuclei, sigma antibonding with a node between them, pi bonding with lobes above and below the bond axis, and pi antibonding with an additional node between the nuclei.}]{./img/MO_2p_maps} 

}

\caption{Color maps of the molecular orbitals formed by the $2p$ atomic orbitals in a homonuclear diatomic molecule, in a plane that contains the two nuclei (black dots, bond axis horizontal). Color scheme: red (positive), blue (negative). Nodal surfaces are reported in purple.}\label{fig:Fig9c11}
\end{figure}

A few important considerations can be made by analyzing these orbitals:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{\(\sigma\) versus \(\pi\) overlap}: the \(2p_z\) orbitals point directly at each other and overlap strongly along the bond axis. The \(2p_x\) orbitals overlap side by side, above and below the axis, and their overlap is smaller. As a consequence, the splitting between \(\pi_u\) and \(\pi_g^*\) is usually smaller than the splitting between \(\sigma_g 2p\) and \(\sigma_u^* 2p\).
\item
  \textbf{Nodes}: as always, the antibonding orbitals have an extra nodal plane perpendicular to the bond axis, halfway between the nuclei. The \(\pi\) orbitals also have a nodal plane that \emph{contains} the bond axis, so the electrons in a \(\pi\) bond are found above and below the axis, never on it.
\item
  \textbf{Symmetry labels}: if we invert the \(\pi\) bonding orbital through the center of the molecule, the upper positive lobe goes into the lower negative lobe, so the orbital changes sign and is labeled \(u\). The \(\pi\) antibonding orbital, instead, does not change sign, and is labeled \(g\). Notice that this is the opposite of what happens for \(\sigma\) orbitals, where the bonding orbital is \(g\) and the antibonding one is \(u\).
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Molecular orbital diagrams of N\(_2\) and O\(_2\)}{Molecular orbital diagrams of N\_2 and O\_2}}\label{molecular-orbital-diagrams-of-n_2-and-o_2}

Once we know the molecular orbitals and their approximate order, we can use them to describe the valence electrons of the molecule, at most two electrons (with opposite spins) for each orbital, starting from the lowest, and following the Pauli exclusion principle as we did for many-electron atoms. The resulting MO diagrams for \(\mathrm{N}_2\) (10 valence electrons) and \(\mathrm{O}_2\) (12 valence electrons) are shown in figure \ref{fig:Fig10c11}.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=1\linewidth,alt={Molecular orbital diagrams of N2 and O2. In N2 the pi bonding orbitals lie below the sigma 2p orbital and all ten valence electrons are paired. In O2 the sigma 2p orbital lies below the pi orbitals, and the last two electrons are described by the two pi antibonding orbitals, one each, with parallel spins.}]{./img/MO_N2_O2} 

}

\caption{Qualitative valence molecular orbital diagrams of N$_2$ (left) and O$_2$ (right). The atomic orbitals of the two atoms are on the sides, and the molecular orbitals in the middle (blue: bonding, red: antibonding). The $1s$ core orbitals are not shown.}\label{fig:Fig10c11}
\end{figure}

The two diagrams are not identical: in \(\mathrm{N}_2\) the \(\pi_u 2p\) orbitals are \emph{below} the \(\sigma_g 2p\) orbital, while in \(\mathrm{O}_2\) the order is reversed. The reason is a mixing between the \(2s\) and \(2p_z\) orbitals, which we neglected in the simple picture above. The \(\sigma_g 2s\) and \(\sigma_g 2p\) orbitals have the same symmetry, so, according to the second rule, they can interact. When they do, the lower orbital is pushed further down, and the upper one, \(\sigma_g 2p\), is pushed up, above the \(\pi_u 2p\) orbitals. According to the first rule, this mixing is important only if the \(2s\) and \(2p\) atomic orbitals are close in energy. As we saw in chapter \ref{Atoms}, the \(2s\) orbitals are lower than the \(2p\) orbitals because they penetrate the core. This effect grows across the row, as the nuclear charge increases (see the difference between \(Z_\text{eff}(2s)\) and \(Z_\text{eff}(2p)\) in table \ref{tab:tab1c9}, multiplied by the larger charges). For the first part of the row (from \(\mathrm{Li}_2\) to \(\mathrm{N}_2\)), the \(2s\)--\(2p\) gap is small, the mixing is strong, and \(\pi_u 2p\) is below \(\sigma_g 2p\). For \(\mathrm{O}_2\) and \(\mathrm{F}_2\), the gap is large, the mixing is small, and \(\sigma_g 2p\) is the lower of the two. This change in order is confirmed experimentally by photoelectron spectroscopy, which measures the energies needed to remove electrons from each orbital.

\subsection{Bond order and magnetic properties}\label{bond-order-and-magnetic-properties}

A simple way of summarizing an MO diagram is the \textbf{bond order}:

\begin{equation}
\text{bond order} = \frac{1}{2}\left(n_\text{bonding} - n_\text{antibonding}\right),
\label{eq:bondorder}
\end{equation}

where \(n_\text{bonding}\) and \(n_\text{antibonding}\) are the numbers of electrons described by bonding and antibonding orbitals, respectively. Since each electron described by a bonding orbital stabilizes the molecule and each electron described by an antibonding orbital destabilizes it by a similar (slightly larger) amount, the bond order measures the net number of bonds between the two atoms. For \(\mathrm{H}_2\) it is \(\frac{1}{2}(2-0)=1\), a single bond. From figure \ref{fig:Fig10c11} we obtain:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(\mathrm{N}_2\): 8 electrons described by bonding orbitals (\(\sigma_g 2s\), \(\pi_u 2p\), \(\sigma_g 2p\)) and 2 by antibonding orbitals (\(\sigma_u^* 2s\)), so the bond order is \(\frac{1}{2}(8-2)=3\). This is the triple bond of the Lewis structure, \(\mathrm{N}\equiv\mathrm{N}\), made of one \(\sigma\) and two \(\pi\) bonds.
\item
  \(\mathrm{O}_2\): 8 electrons described by bonding orbitals and 4 by antibonding orbitals (\(\sigma_u^* 2s\) and \(\pi_g^* 2p\)), so the bond order is \(\frac{1}{2}(8-4)=2\), the double bond of the Lewis structure, \(\mathrm{O}=\mathrm{O}\).
\end{itemize}

So far, the MO diagrams agree with the Lewis structures. The most interesting difference is in the last two electrons of \(\mathrm{O}_2\). They are described by two orbitals with exactly the same energy, the two \(\pi_g^*\) orbitals, and there are two ways of doing so: using the same orbital for both electrons, with opposite spins, or a different orbital for each electron. As for the many-electron atoms (where this is known as Hund's rule), the lowest energy is obtained by describing the two electrons with different orbitals, with \emph{parallel} spins. Electrons described by different orbitals are, on average, farther apart and repel each other less, and electrons with the same spin are kept even farther apart by the antisymmetry of the wave function, which is the origin of the exchange term that we saw in the Fock operator in chapter \ref{Molecules}. Molecular oxygen, therefore, has two unpaired electrons. Since each unpaired electron behaves like a tiny magnet (chapter \ref{Spin}), the molecule is attracted by a magnetic field: it is \textbf{paramagnetic}. This can be seen in a classic demonstration, in which liquid oxygen poured between the poles of a strong magnet stays suspended between them, while liquid nitrogen, which has no unpaired electrons (it is \emph{diamagnetic}), flows straight through. The Lewis structure of \(\mathrm{O}_2\), which pairs all electrons, cannot explain this behavior, while the MO diagram predicts it naturally. This was one of the earliest and most convincing successes of molecular orbital theory.

The same procedure can be applied to all the homonuclear diatomic molecules of the second row, and the results are summarized in table \ref{tab:tab1c11}.

\begin{table}

\caption{\label{tab:tab1c11}Bond orders predicted by MO theory for the homonuclear diatomic molecules of the second row, compared with approximate experimental bond lengths and bond energies.}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{l|c|c|c|c|c}
\hline
Molecule & Valence electrons & Bond order & Unpaired electrons & Bond length (pm) & Bond energy (kJ/mol)\\
\hline
Li$_2$ & 2 & 1 & 0 & 267 & 105\\
\hline
Be$_2$ & 4 & 0 & 0 & — & ~10\\
\hline
B$_2$ & 6 & 1 & 2 & 159 & 289\\
\hline
C$_2$ & 8 & 2 & 0 & 124 & 599\\
\hline
N$_2$ & 10 & 3 & 0 & 110 & 942\\
\hline
O$_2$ & 12 & 2 & 2 & 121 & 494\\
\hline
F$_2$ & 14 & 1 & 0 & 142 & 154\\
\hline
Ne$_2$ & 16 & 0 & 0 & — & ~0\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

Notice how the experimental data follow the predicted bond orders: as the bond order increases from \(\mathrm{Li}_2\) to \(\mathrm{N}_2\), the bonds get shorter and stronger, and as it decreases from \(\mathrm{N}_2\) to \(\mathrm{F}_2\), they get longer and weaker. The molecules with a bond order of zero, \(\mathrm{Be}_2\) and \(\mathrm{Ne}_2\), are not chemically bound (they exist only as extremely weak van der Waals complexes at very low temperatures). Notice also that MO theory correctly predicts that \(\mathrm{B}_2\), like \(\mathrm{O}_2\), is paramagnetic: because of the \(2s\)--\(2p\) mixing, its last two electrons are described by the two degenerate \(\pi_u\) orbitals, with parallel spins.

\section{Heteronuclear Diatomic Molecules}\label{heteronuclear-diatomic-molecules}

When the two atoms of a diatomic molecule are different, the two rules that we used to build the molecular orbitals still apply, but with an important difference: the atomic orbitals of the two atoms no longer have the same energy. Recall from chapter \ref{Atoms} that the energy of an atomic orbital depends on the effective nuclear charge, which increases across a row of the periodic table. Orbitals of more electronegative atoms are therefore lower in energy. This has two consequences, which we can understand by going back to the \(2\times2\) secular determinant of \(\mathrm{H}_2^+\), with \(H_{aa}\neq H_{bb}\):

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Smaller splitting.} The larger the energy difference between the two atomic orbitals, the less they mix, and the smaller the stabilization of the bonding orbital. Orbitals that are very far apart in energy do not mix at all, even if they have the same symmetry.
\item
  \textbf{Unequal sharing.} The bonding orbital is closer in energy to the lower atomic orbital, and it has a larger coefficient on the more electronegative atom. The antibonding orbital, instead, resembles the higher atomic orbital, and it is concentrated on the less electronegative atom. In other words, an electron described by the bonding orbital is more likely to be found near the more electronegative atom: the bond is \textbf{polar}.
\end{enumerate}

These ideas are illustrated by the MO diagrams of hydrogen fluoride and carbon monoxide in figure \ref{fig:Fig11c11}.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=1\linewidth,alt={Two molecular orbital diagrams. Left, HF: the hydrogen 1s level at minus 13.6 eV on the left and the much lower fluorine 2s and 2p levels on the right. The fluorine 2s and two of the 2p orbitals remain nonbonding; the hydrogen 1s and fluorine 2p z combine into a bonding 3 sigma orbital close to the fluorine level and an antibonding 4 sigma orbital close to the hydrogen level. Right, CO: carbon levels on the left are higher than oxygen levels on the right; the occupied orbitals 3 sigma, 4 sigma, and 1 pi lie closer to the oxygen levels, the highest occupied 5 sigma orbital is a carbon lone pair, and the lowest unoccupied orbital, 2 pi star, lies closer to the carbon levels.}]{./img/MO_HF_CO} 

}

\caption{Qualitative valence molecular orbital diagrams of HF (left) and CO (right). The atomic levels are placed at the approximate energies of the valence atomic orbitals, and the occupied molecular orbitals of CO at the negative of the experimental ionization energies (blue: bonding, red: antibonding, black: nonbonding).}\label{fig:Fig11c11}
\end{figure}

\textbf{Hydrogen fluoride.} The \(1s\) orbital of hydrogen (\(-13.6\;\text{eV}\)) is much higher in energy than the valence orbitals of fluorine, whose large effective nuclear charge pulls the \(2s\) orbital down to about \(-40\;\text{eV}\) and the \(2p\) orbitals to about \(-19\;\text{eV}\). By symmetry, the hydrogen \(1s\) orbital can only combine with the fluorine orbitals of \(\sigma\) symmetry, \(2s\) and \(2p_z\), and the \(2s\) is too low in energy to mix significantly. The bond is therefore formed by the hydrogen \(1s\) and the fluorine \(2p_z\) orbitals, which give a bonding orbital (\(3\sigma\)) much closer to the fluorine level, and therefore mostly localized on fluorine, and an antibonding orbital (\(4\sigma^*\)) mostly localized on hydrogen. The fluorine \(2s\) (\(2\sigma\)) and the two fluorine \(2p\) orbitals perpendicular to the bond (\(1\pi\)) have no partner on hydrogen, and they remain \emph{nonbonding}: they are the three lone pairs of the Lewis structure of HF. The eight valence electrons are described by \(2\sigma\), \(3\sigma\), and \(1\pi\), so the bond order is \(\frac{1}{2}(2-0)=1\), a single bond. Since the two bonding electrons are found mostly near fluorine, the molecule has a large dipole moment (\(1.83\;\text{D}\)), with the negative end on fluorine.

\textbf{Carbon monoxide.} CO has the same number of valence electrons as \(\mathrm{N}_2\) (it is \emph{isoelectronic} with it), and its MO diagram looks similar: the bond order is \(\frac{1}{2}(8-2)=3\), a triple bond, and CO is, in fact, one of the strongest bonds known (\(1{,}072\;\text{kJ/mol}\)). The orbitals of oxygen are lower in energy than those of carbon, so the bonding orbitals (\(3\sigma\), \(4\sigma\), and \(1\pi\)) are concentrated on oxygen, and the antibonding orbitals on carbon. Two orbitals deserve special attention, since they control the chemistry of CO. They are shown in figure \ref{fig:Fig12c11}.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.85\linewidth,alt={Two color maps around a carbon and an oxygen nucleus. Left: the 5 sigma orbital is a large positive lobe on the outer side of the carbon atom, away from the oxygen, with a small negative region near oxygen. Right: the 2 pi star orbital has a large two-lobed shape perpendicular to the bond on carbon, positive above and negative below, and a smaller lobe pair of opposite signs on oxygen.}]{./img/CO_frontier} 

}

\caption{Schematic color maps of the frontier orbitals of CO, in a plane that contains the bond axis (red: positive, blue: negative). Left: the highest occupied orbital, $5\sigma$, is a lone pair on carbon pointing away from oxygen. Right: the lowest unoccupied orbital, $2\pi^*$, has its largest lobes on carbon. The maps are drawn from simple combinations of atomic orbitals and are meant to show the qualitative shape only.}\label{fig:Fig12c11}
\end{figure}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{The HOMO (\(5\sigma\))} is mostly a carbon orbital, a combination of carbon \(2s\) and \(2p_z\) that points \emph{away} from the oxygen atom. It is essentially a lone pair on carbon, the same lone pair that appears in the Lewis structure \(:\mathrm{C}\equiv\mathrm{O}:\).
\item
  \textbf{The LUMO (\(2\pi^*\))} is a pair of antibonding \(\pi\) orbitals, which, as the antibonding orbitals of a polar bond, have their largest lobes on carbon.
\end{enumerate}

These two orbitals explain why CO binds to metal atoms through carbon, and not through the more electronegative oxygen. In metal carbonyls, such as \(\mathrm{Ni(CO)_4}\), and in the binding of CO to the iron atom of hemoglobin (which is why CO is so toxic), the carbon lone pair donates electron density to an empty orbital on the metal (\(\sigma\) donation), while filled \(d\) orbitals of the metal donate electron density back into the \(2\pi^*\) orbitals of CO (\(\pi\) back-donation). Both interactions involve orbitals that are concentrated on carbon, so carbon is the end that binds. The back-donation also weakens the C--O bond, since it adds electron density to an antibonding orbital, which is observed experimentally as a lower C--O vibrational frequency in metal carbonyls (around \(2{,}000\;\text{cm}^{-1}\)) than in free CO (\(2{,}143\;\text{cm}^{-1}\), chapter \ref{Spectroscopy}).

The MO picture also explains a famous puzzle: despite the much larger electronegativity of oxygen, the dipole moment of CO is very small (\(0.11\;\text{D}\)), and its negative end is on \emph{carbon}. The polarization of the bonding orbitals towards oxygen is almost exactly compensated by the carbon lone pair (\(5\sigma\)), which places electron density on the outer side of the carbon atom.

These qualitative MO diagrams are the starting point of molecular orbital theory, but they only work so well for diatomic molecules. For a molecule with more than two atoms, such as water, we cannot easily guess the order and shape of the molecular orbitals, and we need to solve the Hartree--Fock equations explicitly, as we will do in the next chapter.

\section{Chapter Review}\label{rev11}

\subsection{Study Questions}\label{quest11}

\textbf{1. In the H\(_2^+\) treatment, which one‑electron molecular Hamiltonian (in atomic units, and using the Born--Oppenheimer approximation) is used?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \(\hat{H} = -\tfrac{1}{2}\nabla^2 - \dfrac{1}{r_a}\)\\
\item
  \(\hat{H} = -\tfrac{1}{2}\nabla^2 - \dfrac{1}{r_b}\)\\
\item
  \(\hat{H} = -\tfrac{1}{2}\nabla^2 - \dfrac{1}{r_a} - \dfrac{1}{r_b}\)\\
\item
  \(\hat{H} = -\tfrac{1}{2}\nabla^2 + \dfrac{1}{r_a} + \dfrac{1}{r_b}\)\\

\item

\begin{enumerate}

\item

\textbf{12. Why is the \(\sigma_g 2p\) orbital above the \(\pi_u 2p\) orbitals in N\(_2\), but below them in O\(_2\)?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  because N\(_2\) has a triple bond\\
\item
  because the \(2p\) orbitals of nitrogen are not degenerate\\
\item
  because the \(2s\) and \(2p\) orbitals of nitrogen are closer in energy, so the \(\sigma_g\) orbitals from \(2s\) and \(2p\) mix more strongly\\
\item

\item
  because \(\pi\) orbitals always overlap more than \(\sigma\) orbitals
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{13. In the HF molecule, why are the two \(1\pi\) orbitals nonbonding?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item

\item

\item

\textbf{14. Why does carbon monoxide bind to metal atoms through carbon rather than through oxygen?}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}

\item

\item

\item

\item

\item
